Batles -

To All of my Students in the Ohio Emergency Junior Radio College: 

Prefaces, as a rule, are unnecessary, uncalled for, and utterly worthless. Doubtless this one is too. Authors usually write them, I believe, as confessions of a sort, in order to ease their consciences. This is specially true, I am sure, of academic writers. 

And in my own case I candidly admit my pedantic soul's uneasiness.

I am not concerned for my slips (God bless 'em!), such, for instance, as my momentarily misremembering Tennyson's labored lines, "The moan of doves in immemorial elms, And murmuring of innumerable bees", and my speaking of them as one line, and an altogether impossible line to boot: "The murmuring of bees in immemorial elms":

Diabolically I hope that here and there I've slipped even worse than that, if worse slips be possible. Such slips, I should say to myself, comfortingly, proceed from composing hastily and with too great fervor.

And that's what I am concerned about, most earnestly and sincerely: fervor. Here I've been talking to you beginning students in English literature for weeks. My central purpose has been to infect you with some of my own intense enthusiasm for literature. 

If I've steadily been sufficiently fervent, as I've meant to be, I'll eagerly bet my bottom dollar that the virus is already active in your systems, bet that you've discovered or rediscovered literature for yourselves and that you are now reading for the sheer joy of it.

But if, on the other hand, I've unwittingly slipped back very often into mere names and dates and titles and commonplace facts, and have presented them apparently for themselves alone and not as aids and means to the infinitely larger end of personal enjoyment in a boundless realm of thought and emotion of truth and beauty and art, if I've been guilty of that, I'm profoundly sorry, for in that case our class-periods together have constituted for you just "another course".

Which alternative is true, only you can properly say, for you have listened to what is in the following pages, whereas others can now only read them. I tremble, as I look back over some of these brats of my brain, fearful that your decision must inevitably be for the second alternative. My only hope is that they sounded better than they read. 

And you will remember that I meant them all to sound, as I read them, as if I were apeaking them naturally and somewhat extemporaneously. "What," I exclaimed one day, "what is more dull and deadly than a printed lecture?" And I went on to say that "no one -- that is, no one in his right mind -- writes for lecturing as he writes for printed publication. The techniques of the two are altogether different." iii

There it is, lurking right behind those last two sentences: the abhorrent pontifical manner and tone! Now I concede that that manner and tone have their place, and perhaps a very important place, but I'm sure that that place is not rightfully in a beginning course in literature. That manner and tone befit one who is officially exercising his authority, perhaps a traffic cop, not one who is intent primarily on sharing pleasure. 

Consider mercifully the pitfalls and temptations I was subjected to. There was I, day after day, sitting alone at a low table in the darkened studio, my manuscript, illuminated by a desk-lamp, spread out before me, -- and my only visible auditor an utterly expressionless microphone! For a week or so I gestured at the "mike' occasionally, and sometimes I even went so far as to smile at it; but it only stared back at me vacantly with its hungry blind mouth, and at last it wore me down to a similar imperturbability. There were five or six hundred of you out there over Ohio listening to me, or at least I was so assured by the College office; but I couldn't really see or realize any of you.

I couldn't, as in the classroom, glance up and be encouraged by a general look of interest, -- or, by a perception of your boredom and unrest, be warned to take another tack! Furthermore, I couldn't, as in the classroom, take you into active partnership in the business in hand by asking you questions or by having you read some selection aloud. And I couldn't obtain a moment's respite from lecturing by rousing you to an animated discussion of your own, over which I could preside as a sort of de facto chairman. I had to keep on talking, steadily talking. Talking day after day, too.

And at night, when I sat down to my desk in my study to prepare the next day's lecture, I'd keep seeing that round, flat, black face of the microphone. I tried desperately to brush it aside, to shut it out, to see, instead, a rectangular lake of faces in a large lecture room; but, necessarily to repeat, whether I succeeded is for you to say.

And here now in a sort of book are the ghosts of all those Winter Quarter lectures come back to haunt me. My good friend the 

Superintendent of the Radio College would have this book be a collection of materials for the course. Perhaps it ought to be that. 

And perhaps that's what it will be the next time, if there be a next time. But for this time it is, instead, a record of the course, of the lectures, of the reading lists, of the quiz questions, and of the examinations.

I have omitted nothing that I said to you over the air, except a few impromptu remarks and a few announcements of free public lectures here at the University which I thought some of you who live nearby would be glad to learn of. Occasionally, in proofreading typescripts before they were mimeographed, I made slight alterations in the text, but I have had no time for revision aiming special grace or eloquence of style. 

I was at least honest with you. I faked no enthusiasms. unhappy fact was, and is, that I am not much interested in the English and Scottish popular ballads. Yet in going over some of the chief types in English poetry I could not justifiably ignore One iv

those ballads. So I disposed of the unfortunate fact by frankly confessing it. Then conscientiously, though summarily, I went ahead and talked of the ballads. And then, to my surprise -- for 

I had been hoping merely for generous tolerance, and not at all for agreement, came this from an alumna of the University of 

Michigan: "How I do agree with your view of the ballad." Perhaps, if they encourage heresy, my personal likes and dislikes should be outlawed! 11 One or two of the ballads that I do enjoy heartily, I read aloud to you. Indeed, I found some excuse to read something aloud with you almost every class-period. I was practicing what I preached to you at the outset of our work together, namely, that you must read poetry aloud if you are to realize it as it is meant to be realized. Also, I was enjoying myself. Finally, I was trying to interest you in the literature itself. And on this point of my practice I have no qualms whatever, for so many of you in your letters have spoken cordially of the oral reading that I cannot doubt your general approval.

The reading lists, you will observe, are not consistent either in style or in quality. Even the skimpiest of them, however, entailed a deal of preparation, and I trust that I have overlooked little of essential importance on an elementary plane. 

The quizes, for the most part, are merely the several points of the lectures turned into short, quickly-answerable questions which served you pedagogically by their thinly disguised repetitions Sometimes a question checks up on a point in your reading, a point that I have not stated directly. Such, for example, is the question, "What happened to Edward's father, in the ballad called Edward?" Sometimes a series of questions leads you up to making a critical judgment of your own. Such, for example, is this series: "Is Wordsworth responsive to Nature?" "Is Longfellow?" "Which is the more sentimental in his response, Wordsworth or Longfellow?"

There should have been many more questions of these last two kinds, but such questions require more careful preparation than the exigencies of time permitted. 

As you who took them know, the quizes were self-grading. That is, I'd give a question, pause, then give the correct answer; during the pause you would jot down an answer, then mark it as either right or wrong, and, at the close of the quiz, figure your own grade One of you wrote to me, "the old bugbears of examns' now become fun", and that was the general tenor of the response received at the 

College office. Yet I wonder about those quizes: Did they keep on being fun? Were there too many of them? Were they worth the time they took up?

I have enjoyed, and still am enjoying, working with you all. 

I've given you the best of me that I could muster in the circumstances. And now that we've done all we may do with poetry this year, won't you, on your part, give me the benefit of your comments at some length? 

The Ohio State University, Columbus, Ohio, April 23, 1934. 

Sincerely yours,

Roket S Verdick 

V.

GETTING ACQUA INTED 

As I think of this occasion today it seems to me to be the first meeting of regular college students of a regular college class working for regular college credit in a regular college course under a regular college instructor in a regular college. Now that lengthy and apparently involved statement of all-around regularity resolves itself into six connected but separable elements, each of which calls for some amplification. 

First: The College itself is the Ohio Energency Junior Radio College. This college, this college of you listeners-in, is under the superintendence of Mr. A. Lee Henderson, who is, as it were, its dean. Mr. Henderson, in turn, is responsible to Mr. Edwin S. Burdell. Mr. Burdell is director of the 

Ohio Emergency Schools Administration in its vist entirety, and therefore is functionally the president of the college. And Mr. Burdell is answerable to the 

Civil Works Administration, so that really the board of trustees of the college is the government of the United States. 

The whole project is part and parcel of that splendidly heroic New Deal which is being given to us by our socially-minded and indomitably courageous President, Franklin Delano Roosevelt, with the counsel and assistance of the Congress of the United States. The State of Ohio is cooperating in the project: 

Mr, Ben H. Darrow, who has done such excellent work as director of the School of the Air, is consultant for the College. And the Ohio State University is cooperating in a number of ways: The broadcasts of the College will go out into the air from the University's radio station, WOSU, a station which pioneered in radio education and which has never swerved from its educational course. The broadcasts of the College will be made by regular members of the Univeristy's instructional staff, whose chief purpose is always to serve to the utmost of their ability the University and the citizens who created and support that University. Such wholehearted cooperation in a project designed butter to serve the people of Ohio is typical of the University's president, the Vice-President and of all other administrative officers and agencies of the University. If there be any virtue in cooperation, the Junior Radio College will most certainly succeed. --Now let me move on to the next point, Second: The college instructor is your humble servant who is speaking. Usually at the first meeting of a class the instructor writes his name out on the blackboard. Unhappily, however, we shall have to make shirt without a blackboard, so I shall spell the name for you: Robert S. ("s" as in "salute"),

Robert S. New-dick (N-e-w-d-i-c-k). 

Third: The college course is a specially evolved new one in Some of the Chief Types of English Literature. Of the course,more anon.

Fourth: The college credit will consist in some adequate official recognition of the achievement of students in the course, adequate official recognition by the Ohio State University at Columbus, and perhaps also by the other, state-supported schools and universities, that is, by the Ohio University at Athens, by Miami University at Oxford, and by the normal schools at Kent and at Bowling Green. This matter of college credit for the course has not yet been worked out in detail by the committee in charge; but I am assured, and therefore confidently assure you, that your achievement in the course will receive adequate official recognition, (1)

Fifth: The college class is state-wide in extent, and yet is not nearly so novel in its organization as at first it may appear to be. In the first course in Chemistry in the Ohio State University, a course that numbers its students in the hundreds, all of the fundamental lectures are delivered to the class meeting as a single unit in a large lecture hall. All of these lectures are delivered by one master, the scholarly and universally beloved Professor William Lloyd Evans, affectionately known on the campus as "Billy". But both for their individual practical experimental work in the laboratory and for their more abstract discussion of principles and problems, the members of the class are divided up into comparatively small groups meeting under the direction of a number of different instructors. 

Also in like man- 

And finally in

In like manner, in this first course in English literature in the Radio Junior College, all of the Lectures will be delivered to a class that meets as a unit by tuning in at the same time on radio station WOSU, ner all of these lectures will be delivered by ono instructer. like manner the members of the class, for their discussions of the lectures and their reading, will be divided into relatively small groups each meeting under the supervision of a special mentor. Thus you see that the organization of our class follows, as closely as is possible under the circumstances, a pattern already worked out in a great state university, Sixth: The college students are you, all of you students out there in groups over the whole proud state of Ohio who are purposefully listening in to this broadcast now and who will tune in on it daily hereafter, daily except for Saturdays and Sundays, until the middle of March, when our Winter Quarter's work will come to a close. You are graduates of first class Ohio high schools, a re you not?

And you were graduated from those fine high schools with standings sufficiently high to enable you to enter any one of the higher state schools without taking entrance examinations, were you not? And you are eager and determined, aren't you, to obtain what we speak of as a higher education? Surely those are the essential qualifications of college freshmen, and you have all of them. 

I imagine you remember quite well the shock you received when you passed from your grammar school into your high school. I know certainly the t I shall not forget my experience in that transition. But it soon turned out to be a pleasant shock, didn't it? Well, you are in for another such shock as you pass now from your high school to your college. Perhaps the differences between high school and college are even greater than those between grammar school and high school, and perhaps therefore the shock will be somewhat greater. But, and again from personal experience, I can assure you that the shack will turn out to be a very pleasant one.

For now at last you will be regarded by your instructors not as boys and girls, but as young men and young women. Now at last you will be regarded as grown up, and you will be treated accordingly. Let me be more explicit, from an instructor's point of view, concerning some of the chief implications of this fundamental proposition, one proposition that you are now intellectually mature. 

In the first place your instructors will assume your interest in your course of study. Though the class-roll will be checked daily by your local supervisors as a matter of course, your attendance at the lectures, at all of the lectures, will be assumed, will be assumed as the simplest of all evidences of your - (2)

www .: 1,0 am t 13

interest in the course. There will be no recognition of such a thing as an "excuse" for an absence. If you are once absent from a class, your instructor will assume that you had good reason to be; if you are absent twice, he will question your interest; if you are absent three times he will conclude that you simply are not interested; and if the authorities learn that your attendance in all your courses is similarly irregular, they will conclude that as a college student you show no promise.

Again, your instructors will assume that you, quite on your own, are determined to master the substance of your course of study. They will, from time to time, make assignments to you of specific material to be read or to be studied. 

They will not, ever, enquire whether you have read it; they will, in all cases, assume that you have studied it, and their questions will be such that you cannot answer them if you have not.

In their lectures your instructors will do their best to be interesting, but their prime object will be to instruct you, not to entertain you. There are plenty of directions in which you can turn for entertainment; you can go to a movie; you can read a cheap magazine; you can tune in on a commercial radio program; you can dance or watch a floor show or do both at a night club. Entertainment, or what passes for entertainment, is all around you, and is offered to you on all conceivable levels, from the bottom up, but mostly on levels near the bottom. Your college instructors will assume, however, that you, as college students, are after an education, after information and arter intellectual stimulus. 

Of course, even college instructors have their lighter moments, and they have (not) authorized me to say that they will be duly grateful if you will be kind enough to smile at their sallies of wit and to laugh at their not too funny jokes.

In their lecturos, then, your instructors will largely dispense with the tricks of showmanship often utilized by skillful teachers in grammar schools and high schools, the psychological acrobatics resorted to in order to gain the attention and arouse the interest of students for whom all learning has to be dressed up and disguised as some sort of fascinating game. Time spent in entertainment is time stolen from learning.

Hence your college instructors will habitually come to grips quickly with their subject matter, and their wrestling with it will usually continue strenuously for the full class period. Their lectures, you will find, are characteristically substantial; characteristically, too, the substance is highly concentrated. None of this, however, need bother you in the least; if you are mentally alert during a lecture you will find numerous guides in it as to its structure, as to its most emphatic points, and consequently as to notes you ought to take on it. 

You may, indeed, for a while, be somewhat taken aback by the vocabularies, especially by the technical terms, employed by your instructors, and you may therefore, at the outset especially, be driven frequently to your dictionary. But this again need not dismay you: sooner than you would believe you too will be using such language, such words, and not batting an eye.

And now I frankly call your attention to some of the major handicaps under which we shall pursue our work together in the College, By far the greatest handicap for me will be the fact that only figuratively can I look out over my lec- (3)

at d 1: :{

-- tern into your faces, read the responses expressed there, and be guided accordingly, for there is, after all, even in lecturing, some give and take. And similarly I should suppose your greatest handicap to be the fact that your instructors in your several courses will be no more substantial to you than their voices, --unless, perchance, you come to find that as professors their real substance resides not at all in their physical persons but rather in their having some thing to say and in their saying it so that you remember it. We shall be going forward, too, not in a tried and proven path, but shall be pioneering, as it were, in the little-explored country of radio education on a college level; and we probably shall, in consequence, make hore and there some false starts and fruitless quests.

There are several objectives toward which we might set out in an introductory radio course in literature. We might undertake to survey the whole history of English literature. Or, we might concentrate on the literary productions of a few great figures in that literature. Or, we might ignore literary history and literary men, as such, altogether, and rim instead at the cultivation of our taste for literaturegenerally. 

Our objective this quarter, however, will be neither of these three objectives by itself, but rather a sort of blend of all three. We shall exomine and study together some of the chief types of literature, particularly English literature. While I am defining a given type, such as the novel, the short story, the lyric, or the essay, and explaining it and tracing its historical development, you will be reading, in chronological order, some of the outstanding excmples of thet type, and will be discussing those examples, and the lectures on them with your fellow students. 

Suppose, for a moment, that I should first take up the Essay. I should then speak, in order, of the Nature and Origin or the Essay, of the Beginnings of the Essay in Antiquity, of the Forerunners of the Essay in English Literature, of Montaigne the Father of the Modern Essay, of Bacon the Father of the Essay in English, and so on. You, meanwhile, would be reading essays themselves, first, perhaps, some of those to be found in the Bible, as typical of the Essay in Antiquity; next, a selection of Montaigne's essays; then, a dozen or so of Bacon's and so on.

It is my purpose and determination than when the quarter is over you shall have: first, a fair knowledge of the ne ture and origin of a number of different types of literature; second, a high-spot knowledge of the historical development of those types; third, a knowledge of the chief practitioners of those types; and, fourth, and most important, a direct and personal knowledge of some of the greatest literature in those types. 

Your assignments of required reading will be, for several reasons, somewhat fewer in number and shorter in length than they would be in a classroom course. But, conversely, you are more strenuously urged to do as much additional reading as it is possible for you to do, for you to do carefully and thoughtfully, For it is true, in the study of literature as in the study of anything else, that your returns, your dividends, your gains from that study will be in direct proportion to your investment or time and energy and effort. 

For tomorrow's lecture, on How to Study and Read Poetry, read some of the lyrics of John Masefield (spelled capital M-a-s-e-f-i-e-1-d).

(4)

---

I earnestly counsel you to keep ahead of the lectures in your reading and study. There are two very good reasons for your doing so.. One is that when I lecture on, say, Montaigne, and his essays, you will listen not with minds utterly blank, but instead with some personal, first-hand honest impressions of your own. The other reason is that. in your after-class discussions you will be prepared to cite chapter and verse, as it were, in support of any critical or appreciative position you may take there, either voluntarily or in response to a question.

And you will do well to go back over your mading after each lecture and discussion-meeting, in order to verify both of your own and the lecturer's points, and in order also to deepen your knowledge and impression of the literature itself. For all that you road will be from established classics, and classical literature, like classical music or painting or sculpture, bears re-examinɛtion with pleasure, indeed with pleasure gradually increasing with every re-examination. The essential characteristics of literary classic re that it has afforded pleasure to generation after generation of readers and that it continues today to give pleasure to readers of understanding and taste. I have no traffic with, and counsel you to have no traffic with any person who suggests any other aim for the reading of literature than pleasure. Certainly, as one reads, his fund of information and vicarious experience is enriched, but that is merely incidental. And certainly as one reads, his taste and judgment develop inevitably, but that too is only incidental. Literature comes into being fathered by intelligence and born of pleasure.

Unfortunately, however, pleasure alone, pleasure by itself, pleasure absolutely, affords no possible basis for examination, and examinations are the sacred white cows of most of our present educational institutions. There is no unit, no standard, by means of which your pleasure can be measured, either in its extent or in its intensity. But we can, at least to some extent, gauge your taste, if we define taste as preference made manifest through choice, and understand that choice to be based consciously or unconsciously, on considerations involving pleasure; we can gauge it by the solutions you make of problems involving observation, comparison, and contrast. And we can measure your knowledge best of all, whether it be your knowledge about literature--that secondary kind of knowledge crammed into literary histories and lectures,--or whether it be your knowledge of literature itself,--that primary knowledge you must obtain on your own. Your examinations in this course, when we get to them, will be formulated on the basis of these considerations and of these relative values. 

Perhaps, despite what I have just said in general terms, there is still a question in your minds, a question something like this: "What sort of questions shall I be prepared to answer in quizzes and mid-terms, and final examinations?" The answer is: "First, but less important, be prepared to answer questions as to the facts and points of view prosented to you in lectures or in reference works to which you have been directed. Second, and more important, be prepared to answer reasonably any question that may be asked you on the literature itself, More specifically, you will not be asked to translate quotations from Greek, Latin or ther foreign languages that may be used incidentally by your authors, though you should consult the notes for translations as you go along: the quotations have a definite purpose or they would not have been inserted. Neither will you be asked to repeat in detail any more anecdotes or illustrative matter utilized by your authors, though you will probably find this material to be significant. 

But you will be expected to know the meaning of what you read--the meaning of any English word in the text, of any sentence or paragraph that may be singled out. (5)

PATA WWW i... ::.. 

10% and

You are quite likely to be asked to describe the impression created in your mind by any assigned selection as a whole: and you will certainly be required to make comparisons between or among selections and authors."

Perhaps you would like for me to answer another question for you, a question as to whether you should or should not take notes. You must, in the long run, decide that for yourself. For my part, I found it well worth while, when I was an under-graduate, to take voluminous notes,--notes on what I read by myself, notes on what my instructors had to say, notes on class discussions, and notes on reference readings. I found my notes to be quite useful, not only in preparing for mid-terms and final examinations, but also in gaining, for my more strictly personal purposes, large over-all impressions and views. Other students, however, and often better students, took no notes at all. I used to marvel at their memories, or a t their fond ideas of their memories. Sometimes I wondered whether, after all, my practice was a wise one; but I kept on taking notes just the same. So, as man to man, I should say, "Yes; take notes; take very full notes, too, at least until you are sure that you don't need to".

Here is my parting work: Bear in mind always that your reading is far more important to you, and, strange as it may at first seem to you, far more important to me too, than lectures or notes or anything else. I shall be happy to serve you with lectures and reading lists and topics for group discussion, to serve you as guide, philosopher, and friend; but I cannot read for you, and I would not if I could. I doubt whether literature at secondhand is possible at ull; but, if it is possible, I am certain that it is vile and deadly stuff, and I counsel you to avoid it as you would the plague. Read ye for yourselves; read ye widely and deeply and joyfully in the realm of good literature; do but this and all literary "extras" will be added unto you. (6)

II: HOW TO READ POETRY 

I suggest that we begin the study of lyric poetry together by coming to grips at once with an example indeed worthy of our analytical steel. Let me read to you a poem by John Masefield, the present poet laureate of England. The title of the poem is, "A Consecration". I would have you first hear the poem in its entirety, as a whole, so that it make upon you its own pleasurable first impression.

For, as you know doubtless as well as I, poems come into being not in order that professors and students may have objects for dissection, but simply because poets are inexorably driven, by peculiar inner impulses of their own, to express themselves in verse. And poems are published so that other men, with lesser gifts, may read them and participate in the vision and the music of the poet, may participate with delight, with deep joy. That is enough, for the moment, about poetry in general. Here is the poem: 

Note 1: (The text of this poem is copyrighted and therefore may not be reproduced here.) 

Well, there is the poem, and a glorious thing it is, too, it seems to me. But what is it to you? Before you answer that question completely for yourself, let me raise some questions for your consideration, and call your attention rather closely to some interesting points and details. 

Vou perceive, of course of course, the significance of the title, "A 

Consecration"? A "consecration" is a sort of dedication, and in this case the poet is dedicating himself to the indisputably notable object of celebrating of the under-dog. That is strikingly unusual and interesting in itself, isn't it?

Now listen again to the first two lines of the poem and reflect upon the wealth of meaning Masefield has packed into them: (See Note 1.) 

Do you know exactly what a "prelate" is? Do you understand what is meant by 'periwigged", and by "laurelled"? I feel almost like apologizing to you for asking such questions, and, if you can answer all three of them, I do apologize to you. But it has been my observation in some years of experience in reading poetry with young men and young women, that all too often they grow impatient with poetry and scornful of poets, because, for one thing, they have not taken the trouble to understand just what the poet has said. And I counsel you to use the dictionary, if need be, in order to understand on the most elementary plane what the poet has said, because in poetry, particularly in distinguished poetry, no single word is ever used hastily or carelessly or ignorantly. 

Words have, you know, every one of them, at least two sets of meanings. The first meaning, or set of meanings, is to be understood on a purely literal plane, The second meaning, or group of meanings, is to be understood on a much higher plane, to be understood figuratively. These latter and superior meanings are (9)

comparable, I suggest, to overtones in music.

Let me illustrate this difference in words by a specific example which I am sure all of you will perceive and understand immediately. Consider the word "mother". Now the word "mother", understood literally, is applicable, I take it, to any female who has fulfilled her biological function of producing offspring. But, surely, when you and I use the word "mother" we are scarcely conscious at all of this literal, elementary, mundane meaning of the word. To us, it means a great deal more, a great deal more, - much more, indeed, than any lexicographer can encompass in a dictionary definition. 

It means home or at least the homiest element in home. 

It means, from one point of view, a household carefully and thoughtfully administered, perhaps mended stockings, perhaps our favorite kind of pie. It means also, from a loftier point of view, infinite patience, interminable kindness, affection hovering over us and sustaining us through ever conceivable vicissitude. It means all this, and a great deal more. And by this time we have come a long way haven't we? 

from the bare, cold meaning assigned to the word "mother" by the dictionary.

I have not forgotten that my point of departure for this digression was the very beginning of a poem. I come back to the poem now, and raise a general question for your consideration, regarding this poem and regarding also all other poetry that you read: "What more does the poem really say than it seems to say on the surface?" On the surface a poem says only what its words mean when they are taken literally, or nearly so; but below the surface it says what the words mean when you hear their overtones, when you perceive the trains of associations that they trail after them.

Now, literally, a "prince" is the son of a sovereign. And, literally, a "prelate "prelate" is an ecclesiastic of superior rank and authority, such as an archbishop. And one is "periwigged" when he wears a wig, - usually for the purposes of ostentatious dress. 

But the truth of the matter is, I think, that Masefield is speaking here quite generally, not of prince and prelates and charioteers, in any literal sense whatsoever, but, on the contrary, of the rich and the powerful and their parasitical retainers as a class. His princes" and "prelates are, then, merely symbols for the rich and the powerful, and his "neriwigged charioteers symbolize perhaps the externally magnificent lackeys who are at the beck and call of the rich and the powerful. 11 ?? "How is one Well,

At this point you may interrupt with a question: to show his understanding of the inner meaning of a poem? one way is to translate the poem into the flippant jargon or slang of your own day. And if you thus translate the first line of 

Masefield's poem - (See Note 1.) you get something like this: 

Not of the big shots, riding in showy limousines with liveried chauffeurs. (10)

That rendering is shocking, I grant you, and I believe there is only one justification for venturing this sort of thing at all; that justification is, as I have suggested, that by such means you can prove your understanding of a given passage. "Triumphantly laurelled"! 

Did that bring to your mind, as

I hope it did, some image or impression of a handsome Greek athlete, or of a masterful young Greek poet, successful above his peers in competition with them and therefore crowned with a wreath of laurel? "Successful" that's the word: these big shots have, in their way, made good. (How sardonic is Masefield's "triumphantly laurelled" applied to them!) 

And now, in conse- 

quence, they lap the fat of the years.

An 

What are we to understand by "the fat of the years"? economist would tell us, no doubt, that "the fat of the years" is symbolical of an accumulated economic surplus. The difficulty with that explanation is that it drives us on to further interpretation. Perhaps we can approach the underlying meaning through reasoning like this: If a man eats too much fat, his body stores up the excess, insofar as it can. Nations, as the years roll by, sometimes produce more wealth than can, under the prevailing order, be consumed. The unconsumed portion constitutes the economic surplus, or, as Masefield puts it, "the fat of the years". 

Which do you prefer, "accumulated economic surplus" or "the fat of the years"? 11 Now let us look into that little three-letter word that begins with "1" and ends with "p" (I suppose some of you are still addicts of crossword puzzles), that little word "lan" If someone tells you about a cute little kitten lapping up its milk, you think merely of a cute little kitten lapping up its milk. You take the word "lap", and properly take it, in its merely literal, worka-day meaning. But if, on the contrary, someone tells you of a certain person's lapping up his food, you are immediately aware of an overtone of meaning in his use of the word "lap" It is natural for a cat or a dog to lap up its food; it is unnatural, in the present state of civilization, for a human being to lap up his food. The word "lap", then, applied to the eating of a human being, suggests something raw, coarse, uncultivated, animalistic. Similarly, there is something raw, coarse, uncultivated, and animalistic in a human being's eating of fat, that is, of course, if we understand him to be eating fat by itself. 

Surely you see now perhaps you saw it all before, and perhaps I have been merely wasting our time in this word by word analysis; but certainly you see now how much suggestion Masefield has packed into those few words in which he describes the big shots and their hangers-on as selfishly and somewhat predatorily lapping the fat of the years.

And let me take occasion to observe that Masefield is not unique, is not alone, in cramming such a plenitude, such a wealth of rich meaning into a few words. This suggestiveness, this allusiveness, this compression, is typical, is characteristic of good poetry.

And of course there is no such thing as bad poetry. There (11)

are oceans, unplumably deep oceans, of bad verse, but there is no bad poetry. The essence of poetry is identical with the essence of truth, is identical with the essence of beauty. I can't define poetry or truth or beauty No one since the beginning of recorded time has been able satisfactorily to define them. But I do know that they are inextricably woven into the warp and woof of meaningful living, I will not try your good patience by plodding on through the poem word by word, line by line. For I am content if I have indicated to you a reasonable, intelligent, thoughtful sort of approach to poetry, an approach that demands of you just two things: first, a close reading of the text itself, in order that you may understand it intellectually; and, second, a thoughtful mulling over of the text, in order that you may grasp its wider meanings and realize it in terms of emotion and art. 

You will not, by such an approach as this, kill for yourselves the noetry which you have in the first instance started out to enjoy. 

You will indeed, by this approach, kill your enjoyment of bad verse; you will find that bad verse dies quickly on the operating table of analysis because there is really but very little life in it to begin with. But poetry will survive any study that you may give it. Mark the words well: poetry will survive any study that you may give it. Maybe it will not survive a study that 

I make of it for you, and almost surely it will not survive any study that you are driven to; but it will survive any study that you give it. So far I have been speaking of poetry chiefly in terms of idea, in terms of meaning, in terms of intellectual cargo. There is, however, another side to poetry, a side equally important and significant, and a side of which I tried to give you some glimpse in my reading of Masefield's poem in the first place, the side of rhythm and music.

In the very best poetry there is no dominance either of idea or music. There is, rather, a complete, harmonious, and satisfying balance or equilibrium between the two. On the one hand, you are not so lost in the meaning of the poem that you lose sight of it as the rhythmical creation of beauty (that was Edgar Allen Poe's definition of poetry), and, on the other hand, you are not so lost in the melody and lilt of the poem that you lose sight of what it has to say.

Remember always that poetry originally had no existence in written form, that it was passed from generation to generation by word of mouth, that its life was in the hearts of the people around their firesides and not in tomes in their libraries or in textbooks in their rude schools. Poetry was meant not to be seen but to be heard, and a poem realizes itself fully only in oral expression. When you read a play you miss a great deal, for a play is its real self only while it is being acted on a stage before an audience. In like manner a poem is only half itself if you read it silently from a printed page. To realize it fully you must read it aloud. Don't tell me that you hear what you read silently; I know you do; I do too; but none of us hears as well (12)

ww

reading silently as he hears when he reads aloud. And therefore it is that I urge you, that I beg you, to read aloud the poems you are studying.

It is especially necessary and desirable that you read lyrics aloud, for the lyric in its simple original form is essentially and primarily a cry, from the poet's heart or mind, or both. 

And we don't, any of us, and poets no more than the rest of us, reach for a sheet of paper on which to record our impressions when we smash our thumbs with a hammer. So, practise reading poetry aloud, even if that necessitates your going out into the middle of a pasture in order to ensure the safety of your physical person while you do so. 

Here are four or five practical suggestions regarding your oral reading of verse: (1) As you read, place most emphasis on the words that carry the meaning of the poet. Emphasize his meaning just as naturally as you would emphasize your own if you were speaking for yourself. (2) Furthermore, accent the words precisely as you do in speech. Do not wrench the accent in order to fit the underlying rhythmical pattern, because poets, like all masters, play variations on their patterns. 

(3) I suggest also that you read slowly. Most inexperienced. readers of verse speak it out far too rapidly. Remember that he who hears you and you always imagine to yourself a hearer or a group of hearers, even if you are actually very much alone, remember that your imagined auditor must have time, not only to hear the words as you go along but also to understand them. 

(4) Again, don't read a line of verse without making some pause somewhere between the beginning and the end. There are, you will find, definite pauses within the lines, usually one pause, but often two, sometimes more. These pauses mark the natural rhythms of speech. If you have not been aware of these natural rhythms in speech, of these groupings of words into comparatively small units, listen attentively for the next day or so to the speech of your friends or of other members of your family. You will observe that in a sentence of eight or ten words a speaker will almost invariably make at least one pause; in longer sentences, he will doubtless make several. Carry this phrasing 

I borrow the term for the moment from poetry's sister art, music, carry this phrasing over into your reading of verse. (5) And, finally, in your reading aloud, pause somewhat at the end of every line of verse. If there is at the end of the line a mark of punctuation you will of course pause without anyone's reminding you to do so. But if the end of the line is not marked by any punctuation, if the line, that is, is enjambed, runs on, pause anyway; pause but the slightest fraction of a moment, but pause. Only by such reading will the lines, individually and collectively, yield up the full measure of their music. 

Now listen to see whether I follow my own counsel in once again reading to you Masefield's "Consecration": (Turn back to the text and read it again.). 

Now, go thou and read likewise. (13)

And by the way:

Tomorrow I shall discuss the sonnet; so, read before then, if you can, a translation of some of the sonnets of Petrarch (capital P-e-t-r-a-r-c-h). 

Good luck to you! (14)

THE ITALIAN OR PETRARCHAN SONNET LESSON I. 

Listen while I read to you a short poem, entitled "Nature": 

As a fond mother, when the day is o'er,

Leads by the hand her little child to bed, Half willing, half reluctant to be led, And leave his broken playthings on the floor,

Still gazing at them, through the open door, Nor wholly re-assured and comforted 

By promises of others in their stead, Which, though more splendid, may not please him more;

So Nature deals with us, and takes away Our playthings one by one, and by the hand 

Leads us to rest so gently, that we go 

Scarce knowing if we wish to go or stay,

Being too full of sleep to understand 

How far the unknown transcends the what we know. 

Do you like that?

It strikes me as a delightful little poum, with its easily understood comparison between our flesh-and-blood mothers and our "Mother"Nature,our gentle Mother Nature, as one poet takes her to be. Doubtless the whole idea is a bit thin and sentimental; but none of us lives on the highest peaks all the time, and there is, I believe, a quite legitimate place for poetry on the simpler levels.

This poem is, in form, a sonnet, and it is with the sonnet that we shall concern ourselves today. The sonnet is a sub-form, or special form, of the lyric. It is, to be exact, a lyric of fourteen lines of iambic pentameter with a definite rhymo-scheme. And of the sonnet itself there are two forms, the 

Italian and the English.

THE ITALIAN OR PETRARCHAN SONNET 

Let us consider first the Italian form. Sometimes this is called the 

Petrarchan form, because it was so admirably used, after its beginnings in early Italian poetry, by Francis Petrarch (spelled capital P-e-t-r-a-r-c-h). Petrarch was born in 1304 and died in 1374. I give you those dates simply because you may wish to have them, not because I attach any great weight to the importance of birth dates and death dates in the study of literature. It is quite enough for my purposes if you remember, concerning Petrarch, these simple facts: that he was one of the great figures of the early Renaissance in Italy, that he was a great lover of all the fine arts but especially of ancient classical literature, and that in himself he so nearly realized his ideal of man as a many-sided, well-rounded creature l'uomo universale 

we call him "the first of thʊ moderns". GOD that we come very near the truth if

The form and structure of the sonnet as Petrarch wrote it is something like this. Take your pencil in hand, turn to a fresh page in your notebook, and follow the directions as I give them to you. Beginning near the left margin of your page, draw a short horizontal line. At the end of the line write a small letter "a". Directly below that first line draw another, but bugin it about half an inch farther to the right than you began the first line. At the end of this line write a small letter "b". Directly below the second line, draw the third line, and after it too write a small letter "bi. For the fourth line, 134-2 (21)

start clear over to the left margin, the same margin you used for the first line, draw the line, and after it too write a small letter "a". You now have four lines, the first and fourth bugiming at the left m: rgin and followed by the letter "a", the second and third lines indented about half an inch from the left margin and both of them followed by the letter "b". This diagram which you have made constitutes a graphical or pictorial representation of a quatrain (spelled q-u-a-t-r-a-i-n). Draw a bracket embracing these four lines and after it write the word "quatrain".

Now, directly below the first quatrain, draw a graphical representation of another, and after it too draw a bracket and write the word "quatrain". (Pause.) These two quatrains, taken together, constitute what is known, in technical language, as an octave (spelled o-c-t-a-v-e). Draw a bracket embracing the two quatrains, and after it write the word "octave". 

Now draw the ninth line directly bolow the eighth, beginning at the left margin, and writing after it a small letter "c". Indent the tenth line one unit, that is, indent the tenth line just as you did the second, but write after it a small letter "d". Indent the eleventh line two units, that is, about an inch, and after it write a small letter "e". These three lines, the ninth, tenth, and eleventh, make up what is known as a torcet (spelled t-e-r-c-e-t). 

Draw a bracket embracing them and write the word "tercet" after it.

Below this first tercet draw a graphical representation of another one, together with its bracket and label. (Pause). These two tercets, taken together, compose what is known as a sestet (spelled s-e-s-t-e-t); so, draw a bracket embracing the tercets and after it write the word "sustet". The octave and sestet, taken together, compose the sonnet (spelled s-o-n-n-e-t); and if you will draw a bracket embracing both the octave and sestet and write after it the word "sonnet", your diagram will be complete.

Now let us take a moment to check over your diagramatical representation of the structure of the Italian form of the sonnet, in order to make sure that in all respects it is correct. The first line begins at the left margin and is followed by "a"; both the second and third lines are indented one unit and are followed by "b"; the fourth line is in all respects like the first, that is, it begins at the left margin and is followed by "a". The fifth and eighth lines are in all respects like the first and fourth lines; the sixth and seventh lines are like the second and third. The ninth and twelfth lines begin at the left margin and are followed by the letter "c"; the tenth and thirteenth lines are indented one unit and are followed by the letter "d"; the eleventh and fourteenth lines are indented two units and are followed by the lotter "ell. 

That's quite a diagram, isn't it? -- quite a complicated diagram for the form of a poem of only fourteen lines. Some doubting Thomas, sueing it, may ask you, "Why in the world should any one make a diagram of a sonnet?" As a student of poetry you can quite satisfactorily answer that question with another: "How, except by closely analyzing it, can onu understand and appreciate the extreme complexity of the Italian form of the sonnet?" This complexity becomes even more impressive as you probe further into the technique of the form. 

Consider, for instance, the rhyne-scheme. Those little letters at the ends of the lines in your diagram represent the rhymes, The eight lines of the octave are built upon only two rhymes, one represented by "a" and the other represented by "b"; and the sestet with its six lines is built upon the three rhymes represented by "c", "d", and "e". Thus Longfellow's rhynes, in the sonnet we read, are, for "a", the terminal sounds of the words "o'er", "floor", "door", and "more"; for "b" the rhyming words are "bed", "led", "comforted", and "stead". 134-3 (22)

In the sestet of the sonnet the order of the rhymes may be varied from the order of cde, cde, but Longfellow holds exactly to the pattern, and the three pairs of words with echoing sounds are "hand" and "understand", "away" and "stay", and "go" and "know".

To a mere versifier, composition with so few rhymes is almost insurmountably difficult; to a poct, however, the paucity of the rhymes presents only a challenge which he joys in mecting. 

Consider also what we may perhaps call the "movement" of the Italian form of the sonnet. Through the octave the thought rises, as it were, to its height. 

Thereafter, in the sestet, after the "turn" or the volta, the thought falls, so to speak, or, to say it differently, the thought is resolved. 

To have a thought suitable for such treatment, and then successfully o to treat it, creating an entirè poem in the short space of fourteen lines, is art, art without any "buts" or "ifs".

Listen while I re-read the octave of Longfellow's sonnet. 

As a fond mother, when the day is o'er,

Leads by the hand her little child to bed, Half willing, half reluctant to be led, And leave his broken playthings on the floor,

Still gazing at them through the open door, Nor wholly reassured and comforted: 

By promises of others in their stead, Which, though more splendid, may not please him more;

There is a well-nigh impeccable octave, illustrating to perfection the first aspect of the thought, its rise, the surge of it. 

And now listen while I read the sestet:

So Nature deals with us, and takes away Our playthings one by one, and by the hand Leads us to rest so gently, that we go 

Scarce knowing if we wish to go or stay,

Being too full of sleep to understand 

How far the unknown transcends the what we know.

There is a sestet for the book, one that has just about every virtue that a sestet should have, and one that beautifully illustrates the second aspect, the resolution of the thought.

We might easily prolong this technical analysis and discussion of the farm and technique of the Italian sonnet, but I believe that enough has been pointed out to you to make abundantly clear the highly-wrought, gem-like character of this form of the lyric.

Let us, then, lock at it for awhile as pure literary art, the end of which art is, let me remind you once again, pleasure. (23) 1039-4

Here, to begin with is a sonnet of Petrarch's number in which he celebrated his remote and idealized sweetheart, Laura: 

Loose to the breeze her golden tresses flowed, Wildly in thousand mazy ringlets blown, And from her eyes unconquered glances shone,

Those glances now so sparingly bestowed, And true or false, meseemed some signs she showed 

As o'er her cheek soft pity's hue was thrown;

Upon the ear with more than human sound.

That old translation may not render every line with unmistakable clarity, I grant you; but once you hear it you have no doubt have you? that Petrarch was in love, and that Laura seemed to him to be the loveliest woman in the world. 

Men and women have been reading that sonnet, and enjoying it, for nearly six hundred years. Why? Why, because a young man in love engages the interest and attention of all who see him mooning around; and if he be a poet, and succeed in getting that mooning down on paper, and in getting it down better than anyone else ever has, his account of his lovesickness will become immortal. 

The plain, unvarnished fact, in the case of Petrarch, is that he was much more in love with Love than with Laura; but that too is a universally and eternally interesting ailment -- being in love with Love, and so this sonnet lives on, rightfully lives on, in all its pristine freshness and delightfulness. 

The Italian form of the sonnet was introduced into England very early in the English Renaissance by a brilliant young man who had travelled in Italy and who had been captivated by the achievements of the native poets in this compar¿tively new form of theirs. That young man was Sir Thomas Wyatt (spelled capital 

W-y-a-t-t). Wyatt was born about 1503 and died in 1542. None of his poems was published until 1557. Those dates, in themselves, are quite unimportant; but, if you compare them with Petrarch's dates, you will realize at once that the Renaissance began to manifest itself in England nearly two full centuries after its beginnings in Italy.

Literally, you know, the word "renaissance" (spelled r-e-n-a-i-s-s-a-n-c-e) means "rebirth". (Another correct spelling of the word is r-e-n-a-s-c-e-n-c-e, but in that case the word is pronounced re-nas-cence.) That which was reborn in Italy, and subsequently in most of the countries of Europe, was an honest and natural joy and delight in the world, in this world, the world of hills and dales and trees and flowers and birds and bees, the physical world of sound and motion and color, of love and romance. For some centuries before the Renaissance, men had been concerned chiefly with the welfare of their souls, with preparation for life not here on earth but life hereafter in a heaven quite literally conceived and understood. 

This they took to be the way pointed out to them by the Scriptures. Then, gradually, they came to realize that there was really no good reason at all for their (24) 139-5-

ཅང་མ་འགོར་བར་གོར་་་་་་ིལ་དར་ ་གས་་

not enjoying their earthly existence. The word by which we sum up and speak of that whole great period of thought and progressive movement toward renewed joy in the world and in the things of the world, is the word "Renaissance". 

The sonnet, then, was introduced into England by Sir Thomas Wyatt early in the period of the Renaissance. Soon it attracted the attention and interest of other Englishmen, notably Henry Howard, Earl of Surrey (spelled capital S-u-r-r-e-y). 

At first both Wyatt and Surrey tried to follow their Italian modcl as closely as possible in English, both in technique and in the dominant theme of love. They had no trouble in sticking to the theme, but the technique was too much for them. The result was that they began to experiment, trying to find a variation suitable to their tastes and abilities. One of these variations was successful, in the long run, over all the others. And it will be of that variation, known sometimes as the English form of the sonnet, that we shall deal tomorrow. 

Let your class-preparation for tomorrow consist in your reading some of the sonnets of Sir Philip Sidney (spelled capital S-i-d-n-e-y), and a considerable number of the sonnets of William Shakespeare, (There's a name you can spell correctly in half a dozen different ways!). THE SHAKESPEAREAN SONNET 

Yesterday we looked casually into the origin of the sonnet early in the Italian Renaissance and into the general definition of the sonnet as a lyric complete in fourteen lines of iambic pentameter with a definite rhyme-scheme. 

Next we diagrammed and studied in some detail the structure of the Italian form of the sonnet, and remarked its manifold difficulties and complexities. We paused for a while to enjoy a typical example of Petrarch's love sonnets and to determine what gave it enduring vitality. Thereafter we followed the sonnet to 

England, whither it was taken early in the English Renaissance by Sir Thomas Wyatt. Then again we paused, this time to consider the large general meaning of the word "Renaissance" as a specific term. And at the very end of the period we noted that one of Surrey's variations on the classic form came to dominate over all other variations, came, indeed, to crowd out altogether, for a generation or two or three, the normal form. 

In battle, however, success goes not to the first on the field but to the strongest forces there. (Purists will insist in vain that I should have said "to the stronger forces",) So it is that Surrey, who invented, as it were, the typical English variation, has not had his name attached to it. That variation is known simply as the English form, or, from its greatest exponent, as the 

Shakespearean sonnet.

THE ENGLISH OR SHAKESPEARAN SONNET 

In order to get the technical characteristic of this form of the sonnet clearly in mind, suppose we read and analyze one from the pen of its supreme master, Shakespeare, - say, the thirtieth:

When to the Sessions of sweet silent thought I summon up remembrance of things past, I sigh the lack of many a thing I sought, And with old woes new wail my dear time's waste; 

Then can I drown an eye, unused to flow, For precious friends hid in death's dateless night, And weep afresh love's long-since-cancell'd woe, And moan th' expense of many a vanish'd sight: 

Then can I grieve at grievances foregone, And heavily from woe to woe tell o'er

The sad account of fore-bemoaned moan, Which I now pay as if not paid before. 

But if the while I think on thee, dear friend, All losses are restored and sorrows end,

That's one of the old favorites, and no wonder. What a memorable tribute to the strengthening power of friendship! abab; 

But observe the rhyme-scheme: - "thought", "past", "sought", "waste";- "flow", "night", "woe". "sight": cdcd; "forgone, "over", "moan", "before": efef; "friend", "end": - gg. Seven rhymes, then, instead of the five in the 140-1 (26)

Petrarchan form. Easier to write. 

And built of four elements 

three quatrains and a couplet - instead of two elements - octave and sestet of the Italian form. Hence a fundamentally altered movement: no rise through eight lines, followed after the turn by the resolution in six lines; but, instead, three nearly separate feints at the subject, followed by a quick and surprising thrust, a pronounced volta after the twelfth line, then the snap of the couplet. Quite obvious, isn't it, that with Shakespeare's sonnet a poet can obtain striking effects, but effects unlike those obtainable in the Petrarchan sonnet? 

Do not, however, misunderstand me; I am trying not at all to belittle the English sonnet but simply to describe it and at the same time to make the inevitable comparisons. ..

Indeed, the English sonnet, as Shakespeare wrote it, and as a few others have written it, is a glorious contribution to the rich store of English lyrical forms.

Observe, first, the remarkable degree of self-completeness in the several parts of this sonnet. 

Take the first quatrain:

When to the Sessions of sweet silent thought I summon up remembrance of things past, I sigh the lack of many a thing I sought, And with old woes new wail my dear time's waste:

There, in a figure of speech drawn from the courts of law (we still speak of courts as being in "session" and of culprits being "summoned" before them), is the first expression of the poet's regrets of the past. figures: 

The second quatrain voices those regrets in a veritable profusion of 

Then can I drown an eye, unused to flow, For precious friends hid in death's dateless night, And weep afresh love's long-since-cancell'd woe, And moan the expense of many a vanish'd sight:

In the third quatrain the poet pictures himself as going over a list of his woes as if he were gloomily checking through a stack of accounts payable figure all of us can understand only too well! 

Then can I grieve at grievances foregone, And heavily from woe to woe tell o'er

The sad account of fore-bemoaned moan, Which I new pay as if not paid before.

Then comes the couplet with, to repeat, its unforgetable acknowledgment of the balm of friendship: 

But if the while I think on thee, dear friend, All losses are restored and sorrows und. 140-2 (27)

TN

Now observe, if you will, how artfully those nearly self-completo quatrains are bound together:

To begin with, the substance of the three is in essence identical. The same general thought is presented in a number of different ways: the basic idea remains constant.

Furthermore, the quatrains and the couplet are unified by their conjunctive elements: the first quatrain begins with "when", the second and third with "then". and the couplet begins with "But if the while", four parallel conjunctive adverbs of time.

Such design, you know, doesn't just happen. There isn't such a thing as an architect building better than he knows.

Did you observe what Shakespeare called the leafless boughs of the trees in autumn? "Bare ruined choirs where late the sweet birds sang."

That's one of the most celebrated lines in all the literature of the world. 

And that's no wonder, when you reflect upon the richness of its suggestive-. ness. For with its "bare ruined choirs" it calls up in our minds a veritable host of impressions, associations, memories, and pictures. 

If you have travelled only through the medium of books (and you can travel far and wide through the medium or books, if you will),--- if you have travelled only through the medium of books, "bare ruined choirs" will probably recall to you pictures you have looked at in histories of England in the time of 140-3 (28)

Henry the Eighth, pictures of the ruins of some of the church buildings destroyed by order of that strong-willed Tudor nonarch shortly after he disavowed Catholicism and embraced Protestantism in 1534, just thirty years before Shakespeare was born.

If you have had the privilege of actually travelling through England, "bare ruined choirs" will swiftly summon to your memory the images of literal church and choir ruins that you there gazed upon with strangely mingled feelings and impressions.

And there's a wealth of associations in that phrase "bare ruined choirs" even for one who hasn't either read or travelled much, for oven today churches are built with choirs, that is, with special architectural provisions for the singers in the choir, and surely everyone will see that there would be some thing more touching in the ruins of a choir-loft than, say in the ruins a refectory.

The third quatrain I, for my part, find far below the other two in poetic quality. To me it is one of those tortuous bits all too common in Shakespeare, one of those involved passages in which he exhibits himself rather as a ringmaster in a circus of words than as a poet sincerely writing out the truth and beauty of the world as he sees it steadily and sees it whole. I do admire the verbal dexterity of the man, admire it heartily; but, to me at least, such verbal dexterity is not poetry.

Didn't you, in reading that sonnet by yourselves, didn't you, when you got through that third quatrain, have only a very general understanding of what it said, only a much blurred impression of its meaning, and didn't you let it go at that? Well, I don't blame you if you did. 

But you see now that it is possible to dig out the meaning, don't you? I believe that the meaning can always be dug out of Shakespeare, if one applies himself to the task with adequate assiduousness. Now turn your attention to some other characteristics and virtues of this seventy-third of Shakespeare's sonnets:

You will observe that its quatrains are again very nearly self-complete. 

Each quatrain, that is, would make good sense if read by itself, if taken as a little poem complete in four lines. The self-completeness of each of these three quatrains is indeed even more striking than the self-completeness of the quatrains of the thirtieth sonnet.

Furthermore, the substance of each of the three quatrains is almost 140-5 (30)

identical. The figure is varied, to be sure, from quatrain to quatrain, but cach of the figures offers a description of the state of the pout's physical being at the moment of composition. He first explains his condition as being comparable to the autumn of the year; next he presents it as analagous to twilight; finally he likens it to the last faint glowing of the embers of a fire. And, as I need not insist, autumn, twilight, and embers are three of a kind.

Finally, did you remark the egocentric keynote frankly sounded at the beginning of each of the quatrains? "That time of year thou mayst in me behold" so begins the first quatrain. The second and third open in the same strain, in identical words, in fact: "In me thou see'st. ​G So, through their form, through their substance, through the parallelism of their figures, also through the device of a like keynote for each of them, the lovely or adroit quatrains are integrated with amazing subtlety and skill,… nḍ, with the addition of the quickly turned couplet, are wrought into a whole that attains the pinnacle of poetic perfection. 

And that perfection, I need not remind you, is the perfection of William

Shakespeare, the undying perfection of a man mortal like other men -- he died in 

1616, three hundred and sixteen years ago, mortal, that is, physically, but forever immortal through the astounding range and depth and power and accomplishment of his genius.

All pains the immortal spirit must endure, All weakness which impairs, all griefs which bow, Find their sole voice in that victorious brow.

THE ELIZABETHAN AND MILTONIC SONNET 

During the latter part of the reign of Queen Elizabeth, which came to an end in 1603, the dramatists and poets of England fairly flooded the country with lyrics.

A notable feature of this inundation was the rich variety of the lyric forms employed.

Yet one form was favorite over all the others, period alone more than 2,000 sonnets are extant. 

the sonnet: from this

Such was the vogue of the sonnet, indeed, that poets took to writing them in cycles, that is, in long groups and series on one general subject, usually love.

The begetter of this fashion was, apparently, one Thomas Watson (spelled capital W-a-t-s-o-n), who published in 1582 a collection of sonnets - or, rather, of what he called "sonnets" - under the title of Hekatonpathia. 

Then Sir Philip Sidney wrote a series, called Astrophel and Stella, which was published in 1591, five years after his death. Perhaps it was this series that aroused Shakespeare to emulation. Shakespeare's sonnets, though not published until 1609, and then only in what appears to be a pirated cdition, were written, according to careful students of them, early in the fifteennineties. Samuel Daniel's Delic and Henry Constable's Diana both appeared in 1592, and 1593 saw Lodge's Phyllis, Barnes's Parthénophil and Parthénope, Fletcher's Licia, and Drayton's Idea. Then, in 1595, come Spenser's Amoretti. 

The list might be much extended; the cycles that I have named do not by any means constitute a complete census of those that were published during the last decade of the sixteenth century. There's no need, here, for such a catalogue, for the one one essential fact, for our purposes, is that sonnet cycles were, toward the close of Elizabeth's reign, in very high esteem among the poets.

As you would expect, Shakespeare's sommets, whether taken individually or as a cycle (or, as some students maintain, as several cycles) are by long odds the best of their time. And further: for three centuries now, anong numerous other sonnet cycles that fro.. time to time have appeared, they have easily retained their place at the very head of the procession. 

Nevertheless there are many memorable sonnets among those written by Shakespeare's elder and younger contemporaries. 

Listen, for example, to this one by Sir Philip Sidney, that paragon of Renaissance virtues who died on the field of battle in 1560. It is 

No. XXXI in the series of Astrophel and Stella (spelled capital A-s-t-r-o-p-h-e-1 and capital S-t-e-1-1-a): 

With how sad steps, O Moon, tho. climb'st the skies: 

How silently, and with how wan a face!

What, may it be that ever in heɛvenly place 

That busy archer his sharp arrows tries!

Sure, if that long-with-love-acquainted eyes 

Can judge of love, thou feel'st a lover's case, I read it in thy looks; thy languished grace, To me, that feel the like, thy state descries. (Cupid) (32)

From that light note, and from the late Elizabethan days in England, we turn to a very sombre chapter in English history, to a very dark and gloomy period in Britain's national life, - to, that is, the period of the Commonwealth, to that interregnum during which the sovereignty of the country was vested not in the person of a monarch, but, instead, in a commoner, who, by the decree of Parliament, held almost dictatorial powers. an infant, that he must have often got into honest mischief as a small boy, and that as an undergraduate in college he must have participated in some wild pranks.

But when we first see him as sonneteer he is already mature and philosophical beyond his years. The year is 1631. Milton, just about to take his Master's degree at Cambridge, has just turned twenty-three: in these lines, he pauses, reflects, and chooses a temper of mind; 

How soon hath Time, the subtle thief of youth, Stolen on his wing my three and twentieth year! 

My hasting days fly on with full career, But my late spring no bud or blossom show' th. 

Perhaps my semblance might deceive the truth

That I to manhood am arrived so near; 

And inward ripeness doth much less appear, That some more timely-happy spirits endu'th.

Yet be it less or more, or soon or slow, It shall be still in strictest measure even To that same lot, however mean or high, Toward which Time leads me, and the will of Heaven.

All is, if I have grace to use it so, As ever in my great Task-Master's eye. 23션 ​And new,

Few youths, I think so ponder on the future when they re, by the calendar, only twenty-three years old. 

The scene changes. How the year is 1655. Now Milton, though only fortyseven, has grown old indeed, has almost been broken utterly under the staggering, unbelievably heavy load of his work as Cromwell's special secretary of state. But the fire, the volcanic fire and passion still burn furiously within hin. And when he hears of a fiendishly cruel and bloody massacre, his titanic wrath floods forth like the thunder of Olympian Jove. 

Here, in brief, is the background, sketched, for you by William Vaugh Moody, one of Milton's best editors: "The Vaudois had cherished, long before 

Luther's time, presumably indeed from the earliest Christian centuries, a form of worship and a theology conceived in the purest spirit of the Reformation. 

Amid the intense religious ferment of the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries they had stood as a type of the prisca fides of the early church, a survival of the golden age of apostolic faith. In January, 1655, the Duke of Savoy determined to suppress them. An edict was issued ordering the inhabitants of three valleys either to leave the country or to embrace the Catholic ruligion. On their refusal to comply, a general massacre was instituted, and carried out with frightful refinements of cruelty". 

Here is Milton's mighty sonnot, froth.of, say, Drayton: immeasurably far from the amoristic (35)

The Sonnet-III- 

Avengo, O. Lord, thy slaughtered Saints, whose bones

Lie scattered on the Alpine mountains cold; 

Evon them who kept thy truth so pur of old, When all our fathers worshiped stocks and stones, Forget not: in thy book record their groans 

Who were thy sheep, and in their ancient fold

Slcin by the bloody Piemontese, that rolled Mother with infant down the rocks. Their moans 

The vales redoubled to the hills, and they 

To heaven. Their martyred blood and ashes sow

O'er all the Italian fields, where still doth sway 

The triple Tyrant; that from these may grow 

A hundred fold, who, having learnt thy way 

Early may fly the Babylonian woe.

I make no pretensions as a reader of verse; but don't you, even from my poor reading of that sonnet, don't you feel that only "organ-voiced" will adequately describe its tone? It seems like the deep, soul-stirring imprecation of some old Hebrew prophet. 

An equally lofty tone, but yet a far different one, pervades Milton's sonnet on his blindness. This also is from the year 1655. For three years now he has been blind, totally blind, having written out the light of his eyes in his entire devotion to his country as its foreign secretary. Nineteen long sightless years yet stretch before him, the years in which he is to write his great epics of Paradise Lost and Paradise Rogained and his mighty drama of Samson Agonistes. 

How does ho look at life in his darkness, the double darkness of one of who long had sight but now sees no more? Fearfully? Dejectedly? Bitterly?

Listen. Bear in mind simply the parable told in Matthew, chapter XXV, verses 17 to 30, the parable of the master who entrusted talents to his servants in his absence, and listen: 

When I consider how my light is spent

Ere half my days in this dark world and wide, And that one Trlent which is death to hide 

Lodged with me useless, though my scul more bent 

To serve therewith my Maker, and present

My true account, lest He returning chide, "Doth God exact day-labour, light denied?" 

I fondly ask. But Patience, to prevent 

That murmur, soon replies, "God doth not need 

Either man's work or his own gifts. Who best 

Bear His mild yoke, they serve Him best. 

Is kingly: thousands at His biuding speed, And post o'er land and ocean without rest; 

They also serve who only stand and wait. 

His state

That sonnet with its soul-deep Christian resignation, the resignation not of a weakling but of a very man-mountain of strength, is Milton's greatest. 

And Milton's somets, cast in the classic mould of Italy, are the greatest in the English language, are the greatest in any language.
