THE WORDSWORTHIAN SONNET AND OTHERS 

Yesterday's class period closed with deep echoes and reverberations of

Milton's sonnets singing in our ears, and in our hearts as well. For in 

Milton's hands, as Wordsworth declared, the sonnet becane a trumpet; whence he blew 

Soul-animating strains 

alas, too few! 

Yes, a trumpet. What had been, at the lips of earlier sonneteers, little more than a horn of pleasant tone, at Milton's mouth became a very trumpet. And so clearly supreme had been Milton's use of the sonnet that for more than a hundred years other poets hesitated even to touch it. There are, it is true, a few sonnets in the literature of the eighteenth century, but only a few, by Gray, and Gowper, and Bowles. And these few, whether looked at in themselves, or whether regarded historically, are unimportant. They are merely the exceptions that help to support the generalization that in the literaturo of the prosy and pedestrian eighteenth century the sonnet is of altogether negligible importance. 

Then early in the nineteenth century came the third of England's great triumvirate of sonneteers, William Wordsworth (whose name is spelled exactly es it sounds). Others too were using the sonnet in these days of the Romantic 

Movement; but most of their sommets fall short of the merit of Wordsworth's. 

Coleridge wrote some; so also did Byron, Shelley, and Keats. Keats, indeed, wrote some lovely soumets. Yet Wordsworth's overshadow them. 

I've selected four of Wordsworth's sonnets to read with you. The first of these was composed by the sea-side near Calais Calais is in France, just across the English Channel from England in 1802. At the outset it reflects the poet's deep joy in Nature; at its close it reflects the young idealist's fears for the political welfare of his country! 

Fair Star of evening, Splender of the west, Star of my Country! - on the horizon's brink 

Thou hangust, stooping, as it might seem, to sink 

On England's bosom: yet well pleased to rost, Meanwhile, and be to her a glorious crost 

Conspicuous to the Nations. Thou, I think, Shouldst be my Country's emblem; and I should'st wink, Bright Star! with laughter on her banners, drest 

In thy fresh beauty. There! that dusky spot 

Beneath thee, that is England; there she lies. 

Blessings be on you both one hope, one lot, One life, one glory! I with many a fear 

For my dear Country, many heartfelt signs,

Among men who do not love her, linger here. 

Did you observe that this son..et, like Milton's, is cast in the Italian mould? Yet the movement is different: here Wordsworth has broken down the traditional relation between the octave and the sestet. (37)

The Sonnet-IV- 

The next sonnet too reveals the poet's profound response to Nature. Indeed, there is very little of Wordsworth's poetry that does not reveal that response to the external world in all its myriad aspects. The sestet is addressed to his sister Dorothy, she who was for so many years his dear and constant companion:

It is a beauteous evening, calm and free. 

The holy time is quiet as a Nun, Breathless with adoration: the broad sun 

Is sinking down in its tranquillity; 

The gentleness of heaven broods o'er the sea;

Listen the mighty Being is awake, And doth with His eternal motio make 

A sound like thunder everlastingly. 

Dear Child! dear Girl that walkest with me here, If thou appear untouched by solemn thought, Thy nature is not therefore less divine: .

Thou liest in Abraham's bosom all the year, And worshipp'st at the Temple's inner shrine, God being with thee when we know it not. 

Sonnets are, you will remember, lyrics. And lyrics, you will likewise remember, were originally meant to be sung. Indeed, the sonnot, in its very early Italian beginnings, was regularly a song-lyric. Gradually, however, it became a lyric that carried its own music, that was complete without instrumental accompaniment. But I hope for you that some day you may hear a large, welltrained chorus sing this sonnet of Wordsworth's, heat; in music as well as in words,

the broad sun 

Is sinking down in its tranquility, and 

A sound like thunder 

everlastingly. 

The third sonnet shows us Wordsworth in another mood. Nature is stil 1 constant, it is true; the trouble is with the people in it, with us, with all our hurrying and bustling and blind superficiality. Just listen to him:

The world is too much with us: late and soon, Getting and spending, we lay waste our powers: 

Little we see in Nature that is ours; 

We have given our hearts away, a sordid boon! 

This Sea that bares her bosom to the moon;

The winds that will be howling at all hours, And are up-gathered now like sleeping flowers;

For this, for everything, we are out of tune; (38)

The Sommet-IV- 

It moves us not. Great God! 

I'd rather be 

A Pagan suckled in a creed outworn.

So might I, standing on this pleasant. lea 

Have glimpses that would make ne less forlorn; 

Have sight of Proteus rising from the sea; 

Or hear old Triton blow his wreathed horn.

Perhaps Proteus and Triton are not as richly suggestive to us as they were to Wordsworth, who had been brought up on the classics and in mythology; but, that consideration apart, isn't his an unforgetable indictment of our general crass stupidity, of our characteristic insensitiveness to the many-faceted loveliness of the physical world? And Wordsworth, you know, read this world of ours as if it were a book written in celestial hieroglyphics by a beneficent Creator.

As the fourth and last of the sonnets from Wordsworth I have selected one on the sonnet, not the one beginning Scorn with the sonnet; Critic, you have frowned, Mindless of its just honors; with this key 

Shakespeare unlocked his heart; the melody

Of this small lute gave ease to Petrarch's wound; and so on, - not that one, from which, as you will find, I have already quoted, but this more descriptive one:

Nuns fret not at their convent's narrow room; 

And hermits are contented with their cells; 

And students with their pensive citadels: 

Maids at the wheel, the weaver at his loom, Sit blithe and happy; bees that soar for bloom, High as the highest Peak of Furnuss Fells, Will murmur by the hour in foxglove bells:

In truth, the prison, unto which we doom Ourselves, no prison is: and hence for mo, In sundry moods, 't was pastime to be bound Within the sonnet's scanty plot of ground; 

Pleased is some Souls (for such there needs must be) Who have felt the weight of too much liberty, Should find brief solace there, as I have found. 

Yes, the sonnet is a "scanty plot of ground", but what bumper crops true poets can harvest from it! To poets who tend too much to wander, who tend too often to spin out their verses at too great a length, and, curiously enough, Wordsworth was one of these; he himself could never tell whether he was writing great poetry or more doggerel, to such poets; the sonnet is a veritable godsend: kept to a "scanty plot of ground", they produce really first-rate results.

THE SONNET-IV- 

Such a one, I think was Elizabeth Barrett Browning, who, born in 1809 and dying in 1861, is rather a Victorian than Romantic. She and Robert 

Browning, her husband, seemed like angels to each other. I, for my part, don't much care for either of them, and care less for Elizabeth's work in general than for Robert's. But in her love sonnets to him • one of the modern sonnet sequences, by the way, called Sonnets from the Portuguese, maybe because they aren't in her love sonnets to him she's at her best: 

How do I love thee? Let me count the ways. 

I love thee to the depth and breadth and height

My soul can reach, when feeling out of sight For the ends of Being and ideal Grace. 

I love thee to the level of every ay's 

Most quiet need, by sun and candle-light. 

I love thee freely, as men strive for Right; 

I love thee purely, as they turn from Praise. 

I love thee with the passion put to-use 

In my old griefs, and with my childhood's faith. 

I love thee with a love I seemed to lose 

With my lost saints, 

I love thee with the breath, Smiles, tears, of all my life! - and, if God choose, I shall but love thee better after death: 

That, no two ways about it, is love.

New cross the ocean in thought and come from England to America, from English literature to American literature. I want you again to hear the sonnet with what is, after all, its proper movement in the Italian form the octave presenting a thought, the sestet applying the thought. So, once again, I read a sonnet of Longfellow's, one of those he wrote while engaged in translating Dante's Divine Comedy. It's into that translation, he says, thot he enters as people enter cathedrals to pray in silence. The "time disconsolate" of which he speaks is the period of our American Civil War. Here's the sonnet: 

Oft have I seen at some cathedral door A laborer, pausing in the dust and heat, Lay down his burden, and with reverent feet 

Enter, and cross himself, and on the floor 

Kneel to repeat his paternoster o'er;

Far off the voices of the world retreat; 

The loud vociferations of the street 

Become an undistinguishable roar. 

So, as I enter here from day to day, And leave my burden at this minster gate, Kneeling in prayer, and not ashamed to pray,

The tumult of the time disconsolate 

To inarticulate murmurs dies away, While the eternal ages watch and wait. 

truth and beauty made 

That's poetry. That's truth and beauty, too, almost palpable, almost substantial, in poetry. In no other poetic form was 

Longfellow so much as true poet as he was in the sonnet. (40)

THE SONNET-IV- 

One more sonnet and I have done. For this one we recross the Atlantic and return to England. Nay, we don't stop in England: we go on across the channel to the Continent. Now we're alost up-to-date; now we're in the year 1915. A great war is raging, the Great War, as we've come to call it. 

In the midst of that war, with all its grime and gore and ghastly wounds and terrible death, is a young English soldier, Rupert Brooke. And Can you conceive it? - such is young Brooke's genius that he makes poetry out of his horrible situation and grim destiny 

If I should die, think only this of me: 

That there's some corner of a foreign ficld

That is forevor England. There shall be 

In that rich earth a richer dust concealed; 

A dust who England bore, shaped, made aware, Gave, once, her flowers to love, her ways to roam,

A body of England's, breathing English air, Washed by the rivers, blest by suns of hone, And think, this heart, all evil shed away,

A pulse in the eternal mind, no less Gives somewhere back the thoughts by England given; 

Her sights and sounds; dreams happy as her day; 

And laughter, learnt of friends; and gentleness,

In hearts at peace, under an English hoven. 

That, to me, is one of the supreme sommets among all the Italian sonnets and French sommets and English sonnets and American sonnets ever written. 

Hear through it the chime of "Engl.nd, England, England's, English, England, English". And see beneath it the steel-strong character of that island breed, and at the same time its hominess, its gentleness, its restrained and yet profound love of friends and country.

Such is the true stuff of poetry. (42)

QUESTIONS ON THE SONNET 

Careful mariners are constantly making observations and checking their course. Suppose that today you check your progress in mastering the sonnet in English. I'll read questions to you; you answer them. Don't try to write down the questions; do try to answer them. The questions will come to rapidly for you to take them down, but not to rapidly fer you to answer them. But write out every answer. Usually the answer can be made with one word; hardly ever will it require mère than three or four words. Then, after a pause, I'll give the correct answer. You mark your answer as right or wrong. Then I'll ge on to the next question.

NOTE ON THE SPENSERIAN SONNET 

Before beginning with our formal study today I should like to take a moment to say that I shall be very glad to answer, if I can, any pertinent questions that you may wish to ask me about the literature which we are, and have been, reading together. 

Questions that seem to me to be of class-wide interest I shall, answer, to the best of my ability, by radio. Questions of less general importance I shall answer by mail. Address your questions to me personally, not at the headquarters of the Radio College but at my office, 120 Derby Hall, Ohio State University, 120 (1-2-0) Derby Hall (capital D-e-r-b-y, capital H-a-1-1), Ohio 

State University, Columbus, Ohio.

I have here today a letter from Mr. Paul Shivell of Dayton, Ohio. Thank you sincerely, Mr. Shivell, if you are listening in today, for all the nice things you say about my lecturebroadcasts. It is a genuine pleasure, believe me, to learn directly from you listeners-in that you find my daily half-hour worth your time. And may I not add that that is especially true when you tune in not as students for college credit but as members of an interested general audience. 

Mr. Shivell raises a question which, taken in connection with its larger implications, may be of interest to many others of you. 

Mr. Shivell observed that in talking about the sonnet I didn't speak specially of that form of it employed by Edmund Spenser, and Mr. Shivell who quite understandably regards Spenser as his "beloved master" , enquires whether that form was original with Spenser. Yes, Mr. Shivell, I believe it was. Spenser's sonnets, others of you may care to know, are composed of three quatrains and a couplet, and the rhyme-scheme is abab, bcbc, cdcd, ee. 

I omitted special mention of that form, and also of other interesting variations, in the interests of simplicity. Addressing myself, for this Quarter at least, primarily to a class of young men and women who have but recently been graduated from high school, I try steadily to confine myself to the main highways. 

The consequence is often that what I do, and oftener what I don't do, mildly outrages my professional conscience.

In this instance I justified my radio-self to you by Harvard-Ph.D.-self by two different but related lines of reasoning. 

First, I urged that the Spenserian form, despite its admirable perfection in the hands of its creator, and espite the excellent work done in it by a few other poets, has never become firmly established as a sonnet-pattern in the stream of English literary tradition. Then, second, I urged that the Spenserian form is merely a variation of the English or Shakespearian form, because, like that, it is composed of three quatrains and a final couplet, and neither three quatrains nor a final couplet is (Or should I say "are" "?) to be found in the Italian or Petrarchan form. "But", you may interpose, "what about the rhyme-scheme?

Ah, yes; the rhyme-schemes Spenser's allows, like that of the 

Italian sonnet, only five rhymes, abab, bcbc, cdcd, ee, whereas Shakespeare's makes use of seven, abab, cdcd, efef, gg. 

(49)

Note-Spenserian Sonnet 

The rhyme-scheme, then, reveals Spenser's form of the sonnet to be a sort of compromise between the Italian and the English forms. 

By and large, however, it seems to me, even when I speak figuratively from beneath a doctor's mortar-board, that Spenser's form is more accurately to be thought of as a variety of the English form than as establishing another form. And as to the reason why it hasn't been more widely popular with other poets than it has, I should point to its technical difficulty, a difficulty akin to that involved in writing terza rina. 

I wouldn't emphasize this last point, for, as you know, I've already minimized technical difficulties so far as poets are concerned, but well, have you ever tried your skill in writing sonnets? 

Of course,

Let me supplement this excursus about the sonnet with another that is called for. The other day I heard someone remark casually some one who ought to know better that Milton followed the Italian sonnet in its rhyme-scheme but ignored its difference between octave and sestet in movement and expression, ignored the "rise", the "turn" and the "fall". Such a statement is simply not true. Milton wrote eighteen sonnets in English: nine make the pause and "turn" at the end of the eighth line; six others make it just hefore or after that point, breaking either the eighth or ninth line; the remaining three make it, but at a greater remove than one line. In other words, the plain fact is that all of Milton's sonnets follow the movement of the Italian; all of them have its volta; and most of them put that "turn" precisely or very nearly where it strictly should be. 

Well, so much for mopping up. (50)

THE LYRIC 

I.

What better way is there to begin our study together again, after our week's holiday, than by reading aloud a short poem? 

For my part, I can think of no better way. 

Three of the words in the poem might strike you as strange, and might therefore hinder your enjoyment of it, so let's clear the way by familiarizing ourselves with these words taken singly. 

The first word is the word greasy (spelled g-r-e-a-s-y). 

When you hear it in the poem, don't, I pray you, think of lard or of butter or of any grease at all; think, rather, of grease as meaning "bulky" or "fat" and hence of greasy as meaning fat of body" or "largebodied", The second word is the word keel (spelled k-e-e-1), and it means simply "to keep cool by stirring. The third word is crabs (spelled c-r-a-b-s), and crabs looks stranger than it really is, for really it is only an abbreviation of crabapples. 

Now, with the way cleared for it, let's to the poem: 

When icicles hang by the wall, And Dick the shepherd blows his nail,

And Tom bears logs into the hall, And milk comes frozen home in pail; 

When blood is nipt, and ways be foul, Then nightly sings the staring owl Tu-whit! 

Tu-whoo! A merry note!

While greasy Joan doth keel the pot. 

When all about the wind doth blow 

And coughing drowns the parson's saw 

And birds sit brooding in the snow, And Marian's nose Icoks red and raw; 

When roasted crabs hiss in the bowl 

Then nightly sings the staring owl Tu-whit! 

Tu-whoo! A merry note!

While greasy Joan deth keel the pot. 

How's that for hitting off the weather we've been having lately? Just listen to a list of those elements again: icicles hanging by the wall; Dick blowing his finger nails; Tcm bringing in logs for the fireplace; milk frozen; foul roads; wind, and then more wind; coughing, small birds huddled in the snow; and Marian's raw, red nose. 

Of course that was written recently?' No; the strange words give the right clue: that was written three hundred and forty-odd years ago by an English country boy who came up to London and made good there, by William Shakespeare of Stratford, a village on the river Avon in Warwickshire. 

(55)

The Lyric-I- 

Shakespeare wrote it for some incidental music in his early comedy of Love's Labours Lost. The Elizabethans were extremely fond of music and particularly fond of songs. They liked them so well that their dramatists found or made ways to insert them into their plays. And in this, as in nearly everything else, Shakespeare merely fell in with the fashion. Well, no; not "merely": always, he superlatively capped the vogue. as But don't be alarmed: I didn't set out to talk about 

Shakespeare. I set out to talk about the lyric in general, and, wishing a capital example of a lyric to begin with, I inevitably gravitated toward the works of the master. And that's how Shakespeare came in.

Consider the word lyric by itself. Write it out in your notebook: l-y-r-i-c. Now, beside lyric, write the word lyre: l-y-r-e. A lyre as of course you know, is a stringed instrument of the harp class. The ancient Greeks used to make them out of some hollow body, such as a tortoise shell, from which were raised two arms outward and forward, and joined at the top by a crossbow or yoke, from which gut strings were stretched to the bridge or the shell. The strings were stopped, that is, were fingered, by the left hand, and were struck by a plectrum, or pick, in the right hand. The connection between lyric and lyre is quite obvious. 

The lyric, then, was born in Greece, and there it was composed to be sung by a single voice to the accompaniment of a lyre. 

Take careful note: was composed to be sung ... by a single 

We shall return to that definition voice anon. 11. to an accompaniment.'

Greatest of Greek lyric poets was Sappho (spelled capital S-a-p-p-h-o). Sappho lived some six centuries before the birth of 

Christ. From her sea-girt island home of Lesbos (spelled capital 

L-e-s-b-o-s) she sent forth over the unbelievably blue waters of the Mediterranean a stream of melody such as the world had never heard before, such as the world has never heard since. For she was celebrated not only by other Greek poets as the greatest of them all in song, but she has also been so celebrated by later poets of other nations. She was, said the modern English poet Swinburne "beyond all question and comparison the very greatest poet that ever lived". 

Unfortunately, however, Sappho's lyrics are preserved only in fragments, And most of us must perforce read oven those fragments in translation, and translations are never really satisfying. Yet here's one great poem of Sappho's in a noble translation by Lord 

Byron; it's called "Hesperus, the Bringer", and alludes, of course, to the evening star: 

O Hesperus, thou bringest all good things

Home to the weary, to the hungry cheer, To the young bird the parent's brooding wings,

The welcome stall to the o'erlabored steer; 

Whate'er of peace about our hearthstone clings 

Whate'er our household gods protect of dear, Are gathered round us by thy look of rest; 

Thou bring'st the child too to its mother's breast. (56)

The Lyric-I- 

1 Usually Sappho wrote of love or of beauty, or lamented, as centuries later the Renaissance poets did, that beauty passes and must die. 

Such poetry we call erotic poetry e-r-o-t-i-c - a word derived from Eros (capital E-r-o-s), Eros the god of love, known in Roman mythology as Cupid. But in "Hesperus" Sappho wrote on a theme quite as universal and as timeless as love, and on one much less hackneyed. 

O Hesperus, thou bringest all good things

Whate'er of peace about our hearthstone clings, Thou bring'st the child too to its mother's breast.

Older than Greek poetry, of course, but not in a tradition that was regarded as literary until fairly recent times, is Hebrew poetry, particularly the pieces that are attributed to David. The clear and powerful imagery of the lyrics that compose the Book of Psalms is unrivaled in any other lyric poetry in the world's literature. The tone of the Psalms is usually sombre and moralistic; their subjects are manifold; but always the imagery is vivid and unforget able. Take, for instance, Psalm VIII, A Song of Praise": 

O Lord our Lord, how excellent is thy name in all the earth! 

Who hast set thy glory above the heavens.

Out of the mouths of babes and sucklings hast thou ordained strength because of thine enemies, 1 

That thou mightest still the enemy and the avenger. 

When I consider thy heavens, the work of thy fingers 

The moon and the stars, which thou hast ordained, What is man, that thou art mindful of him? 

And the son of man, that thou visitest him?

For thou hast made him a little lower than the angels, And hast crowned him with glory and honor.

Thou madest him to have dominion over the works of thỷ hands; 

Thou hast put all things under his feet: 

All sheep and oxen,

Yea, and the beasts of the field; 

The fowl of the air, and the fish of the sea, And whatsoever passeth through the paths of the seas. 

O Lord our Lord, how excellent is thy name in all the earth! 

Among Roman lyric poets perhaps the two greatest are Catullus and Horace young Catullus (capital C-a-t-u-1-1-u-s), who lived from 84 B.C. to 54 B.C., and urbane Horace (capital H-o-r-a-c-e), who lived from 65 B.C. to 8 B.C. Here again 

Here again, of course, most of us can read their work only in translation, but here's a capital translation by Theodore Martin (capital M-a-r-t-i-n) of Catullus's "Farewell to Bithnyia": 

A balmy warmth comes wafted o'er the seas

The savage howl of wintry tempests drear 

In the sweet whispers of the western breeze 

Has died away; the spring, the spring is here! (57)

The Lyric-T- 

Now quit, Catullus, quit the phrygian plain, Where days of sweltering sunshine soon shall crown 

Nicaer'a fields with wealth of golden grain, And fly to Asia's cities of renown! 

Already through each nerve a flutter runs 

Of eager hope, that longs to be away;

Already 'neath the light of other suns My feet, new-winged for travel, yearn to stray. 

And you, ye band of comrades tried and true, Who side by side went forth from home, farewell! 

How far apart the paths shall carry you 

Back to your native shore, ah, who can tell? 

Isn't that a delightful case of wanderlust?

THE LYRIC 

Monday we talked a little about the lyric. First, for a pleasant introduction into the subject, we delighted together in one of the justly famous songs from Shakespeare's plays, "When ioicles hang by the wall" and noted the vogue for songs in Elizabethan drama generally. Next we considered the iriginally close onrection between the lyric and the classic Greek lyre. Then we enjoyed Byron's translation of one of the bits of great Sappho's work that has been preserved. not a characteristically erotic piece, one concerned with love, but "Hesperus", a lovely tribute to the evening star and a listing of some of the good things it brings to day-worn men and women, 'Thou bring'st the child too to its mother's breast' Then we paused to recall the psalms of David, to remember how richly fraught they are not only with moralisings but also with beautiful and powerful imagery, and in the King James' version of the eighth we heard rolling forth a solemn, deep, organ-like music of thought end experience that reminded us where Milton must have learned the awful, majestic tones that surge forth from his mightiest passages, "When I consider the heavens, the work of thy fingers,

The moon and the stars, which thou hast ordained, What is man, that thou art mindful of him? 

Cr the son of man, that thou visitest him?" 

We closed the period by nodding to the Roman lyrists young Catullus and urbane Horace, and indeed read Martin's translation of Catullus's "Farewell to Bithnyia", an immortal record of the wanderlust begot in the poet by the return of spring, "Now quit, Catullus, quit the Phrygian plain, Where days of sweltering sunshine soon shall crown 

Nicea's fields with wealth of golden grain, And fly to Asia's cities of renown! 

Already through each nerve a flutter runs 

Of eager hope, that longs to be away;

Already nerth the light of other suns 

My feet, new-winged for travel, yearn to stray."

It was a short but happy class-period, and so freighted with beauty that we can easily understand why an excellent critio specks of the lyric as "the finest and truest form of poetry". Nevertheless, as the same critic observes, and as we too shall observe in our study, the lyric is the "most... elusive variety of poctry".

There are many types, 

I use the word types in its strict sense; and if you are not very sure of your understanding of that strict sense, I counsel you to look the word up in a good dictionary and study it there are many types of liters ture. Thus in his little book on the typical forms of English literature (end that is its title: 

The Typical Forms of English Literature), Dr.H. H. Uphan (spelled 

3apital U-p-h-a-m), now president of Miami University where he was (61)

The Lyric formerly a professor of English, lists seven: the popular ballad, the lyric, the epic, the personal essay, the novel, the short story, and the drama. And any one of those types, with perhaps the oxcoption of the personal essay, is easily divisible into a number of sub-types. 

Thus Norman Hepple (spelled capital H-c-p-p-1-e) in his excellent book on the lyrical forms in English (and that is the title of this anthol ogy-treatise: Lyrical Forms in English),lists five types of the lyric: 

the song-lyric, the sonnet, the odo, the idyll, and the clogy. We have already explored the sonnet, and you will rocall that even of this subform there were lesser subforms. And the English song-lyric has even more numerous subforms than the English sonnet has We shall note the subforms of the song-lyric at a later time but it is not either the subforms themselves or the number of them that makes the lyric particularly "clusive", It is, rather, the nature of lyric quality generally. In view of this fact let us try to determino as many of the elements of general lyric quality as we can. willing to follow closely? All right, then; lot's go.

The first point is that the lyric is subjective, (spelled exactly as it sounds). 

Are you subjective

Fully to understand that point we must digross to do something clsc, and that is to gain a sound understanding of the word subjective - and also an understanding of the word of opposite meaning, the word objective. Subjective and objective are more than merely words; they are fundamental terms in literary criticism, and wo shall havo many occasions besides the present one on which to make use of them in our literary criticism.

We observed some days ago that poetry, like every other artform, carries a two-fold cargo, an intcllcctual cargo of idea, and an emotional cargo of feeling. That is true of all poetry, with no exceptions. But where lie the springs of the emotion? There is our question now: Where lie the springs of the emotion? What prompted it, what aroused it, what called it forth? If the springs of the emotion lie outside of the poet himself, his poem will be objective; if they lie within him, it will be subjective. The old English and Scottish popular ballads, as we shall see when we come to study them, were, and are, objective. They tell storics, recount adventuros, narrate joys and sorrows, objectively. Nowhere does the poot shine through them; nowhere do we oven glimpse what he felt as a man. As Professor Kittredge says, it is as if the ballads told and sang themselves. The personal clcmont is entirely absent. Of their authors we know absolutely nothing, I don't moan that we know nothing about the authors factually, nothing about when. they were born, where they died, where they lived, and so on, though as a matter of fact we don't have any such information; I mean that we don't know anything at all about individual ballad authors don't know what a gorgeous sunset made any one of them fool, don't know what any one of them felt when he was in lovc don't know anything. So far as the ballads show, their authors might have lived like contented cows, without thinking, without ferling. We are confident, of course, that they did think and feel, despite their apparently ontire preoccupation with objects, doods, and ovents. But that thinking and that f cling found no cxpression in the ballad poctry. 

(62)

The Lyric- 

That poetry remained entirely objectivel So until we read and study them together, take my word for it that the ballads are objective; 

And understand that objective poetry is poetry in which the character and personality of the writer is entirely concealed. 

And conversely, subjective poetry is poetry in which the author reveals himselfi 

Listen to George Herbert's poom called "Virtue". By the crlen der, it's several hundreds of years old, but by the heart it's as fresh as a new-blown rose: 

Sweet day, so cool, so calm, so bright!

The bridal of the earth and sky, 

The dow shall weep thy fell tonight, For thou must die. (Herbert wrote this poem in the spring, as you can hear; but we can remember what spring is like, can't we?) 

Swcet rose, whose hue, angry and brave, Bids the rash gazer wipe his eye, Thy root is ever in its grove, And thou must die. 

Sweet spring, full of sweet days and roses,

A box where sweets compacted lie, My music shows yo have your closes, And cll must die. 

Only a sweet and virtuous soul, Like seasoned timber, never gives;

But though the whole world turn to coal, Then chiefly lives. 

Isn't that an unspeakably lovely thing? as calm and lovely as the spring day that set the poct to thinking, to meditating. Ah, Sec? I'm talking about the poet, even before I'm were of it. 

I'm talking about him easily and casually, as if I know him. but I do know him! Don't you? 

What if he has been in his gravo for centuries? The grave can hold only mortal remains, and not even them for long; dead bodies feed worms, and dry out, and crumble, and then at last return to the dust and ashes whence they sprang. But the mind of the man and the heart of the man - well, you'll grant, won't you, if he was poet, that these live on forever? And if he was a lvric poet, you and I can enter into that mind and heart of his, just as surely as if we had known him in the flesh, can't we? For a lyric poet reveals himself. He is - to make use of our new term ive. gree subject- Now listen to one of the Scottish popular ballads. No, our time is just about up, so you must go and read one yourself. I've decided, you see, that you shouldn't take my word for it, that you should verify for yourself what I've said about the popular ballad, namely, that it is objective. I wish to be sure that you understand what objective means. (53)

The Lyric- 

But I misspoke myself in one respect: 

I wish you not merely to read a popular ballad, but also to sing it. True, you can check on its objectivity without singing it, but the old popular ballads invariably were sung for centuries they had no other existence than and they always should be sung. in song 

I suggest that you read, I mean that you sing, "Sir

Patrick Spens" (spelled capital S-p-e-n-s), the ballad that Samuel Taylor Coleridge was so fond of because of the lines "Late late yestreen I saw the new moone, Wi the auld moone in hir arme." 

The tune? Well, it will go to "It Ain't Gonna Rain No More", if you can't think of a better one! (64)

THE LYRIC 

?? 

Yesterday we heard a distinguished critic remark that the lyric is not only the finest and truest form of poetry" but also that it is the "most elusive variety". We took that epithet "elusive" as a challenge and set out to find the why and wherefore of it. We noted that there are many types of literature, that of each type thero are numerous subtypes, and that even the subtypes are further divisible into lesser subtypes. That line of investigation, however, throw no light on the elusiveness' of the lyric, so we abandoned it and startod out on another. This latter enquiry was directed into the lyric quality by and large. The first point we determined was that all lyrics are in some dogrce subjective: In order to establish that point firmly we paused to clarify the meaning of the term subjective in literary criticism, and to clarify also our understanding of the opposite term, objective. We found subjective poetry to have its origin in an impulse born within the poet, and objective poetry to have its origin in an impulse coming from outside the poet. The former arises from individual feeling and thinking, the latter from a group response to 'objcots, deeds, and events! The personality of the poct is submerged or concealed in objective poetry, whereas in subjective poetry the poet reveals, indeed even capitalizes, his own peculiar character and personality. And you will remember that in order to realize this distinction vividly for ourselves we looked directly at some poetry. 

As an example of lyric subjectivity we read George Herbert's unforgetable poem walled "Virtue", in which we not only heard a soothing melody of expression but also perceived the meditations and conclusions of a sensitive, appreciative, pious, melancholy mind, Only a sweet and virtuous soul, Like seasoned timber never gives;

But though the whole world turn to coal, Then chiefly lives.

For an example of objectivity in poetry, you read the old popular Scottish ballad of "Sir Patrick Spens" I'm sure you liked it, especially those lines that Coleridge liked 

"Late late yestreen I saw the new moone,

Wi the auld mocne in hir arme' But 

Even though such a "literary" figure of speech is rare, and indeed a mild sort of blemish, in a true, in an ideal, popular ballad. not to lose sight of our hare - the first and chief characteristic of lyric poetry is its subjectivity. 

Is that clear? I'm afraid it's too clear. I'm fearful that. perhaps I've made a rather difficult matter appear to be a simple one. 

The difficulty lies in the fact that subjectivity and objectivity are not absolute but relative terms. That is, there are degrees of them. 

And one is sometimes puzzled as to where to draw the line. 

For example, objectivity is ideally characteristic of modern artistic ballads as well as of the old popular ballads. Nevertheless, there is often an admixture of subjectivity in modern ballads. Are they therefore to be thought of, classified, and discussed as lyrics? 

Or should we approve a hybrid form and call them "lyrical ballads"?

(56)

The Lyric-III- 

1-1 Very fro- Again, objectivity is ideally characteristic of cnic and dramatic poetry. But consider the verse-dramas of Byron. quent in those plays are passages in which the persons of the drama step out of their characters and take on Byron's, speaking Byron's thoughts, expressing Byron's feelings. Are these pieces, then, dramas or lyrics? Perhaps they may be most easily classified as "lyrical dramas", absurd as that term seems to be. 

The pocts and artists generally are not to be, and should not bo, "bound" by "rules". Rules are properly deduced from the past practice of the masters, deduced by critics who are capable of such arduous work. And, conversely, rules are not properly to be set up to crib, cabin, or confine the futuro practice of masters, 

not to bo set up by pedants who can see life only in the forms of dorth. And whenever a master produces a masterpiece that ill sorts with the rules, the rules must be revised and enlrged and made of more comprehensive scope. The only proviso in all this is that the burden of justification for a hybrid form or for a new form rests upon the work done in it. If that work affords pleasure to readers of judgment of taste and if it continues to afford them pleasure, - continues, if it is old, through generations, or continucs, if it is ncw, through rereadings, then it may rightly be called great, or classic, and may rightly domand a rovision of the rules.

Understand, then, that I am setting forth the characteristics of the lyric descriptively, not arbitrarily. And I counsel in you a similar humility. 

The second distinguishing mark of a lyric is its unity, its unity (spelled as it sounds: u-n-i-t-y). The roots of the English word unity lie back in the Latin word unus (spelied u-n-u-s); meaning "one" 

Unity is therefore oneness. 

That which has oneness, - or, better, that which unity, term in literary criticism, is complete in itself. 

better because unity is another fundamental that which has unity is self-contained, And isn't it true of the lyrics we've read, that they are selfcomplete? Think of Shakespeare's "When icicles hang by the wall":"

Isn't that delightful all by itself, delightful even if you know nothing whatsoever about Shakespeare or Elizabethan drama or anything else except winter? Think of Sappho's "Hesperus": Does anyone, to enjoy that, have to know anything other than what is in the poem itself? That unity arises from the fact that as Francis Palgrave put it, in the Preface to his Golden Treasury, the lyric turns on some single thought, feeling, or situation" There may be a number of subsidiary ideas, but there is always a dominant central idea. 

Thus in Herbert's poem "Virtue", the first two lines of the first stanza present a lovely impression of a spring day "Sweet day, so cocl, so calm, so bright! 

The bridal of the earth and sky, a day that is doomed, according to the last two lines: 

(67)

The Lyric-III- 

"The dew shall weep thy fall tonight, For thou must die.

The second stanza turns on a rose 

"Sweet rose, whose hue, angry and brave, Bids the rash gazer wipe his eye," a rose that also is doomed: 'Thy root is ever in its grave, And thou must die.

The third stanza gives us the season of spring by and large "Sweet spring, full of sweet days and roses,

A box whore sweets compacted lie, a season that must inevitably pass: "My music shows ye have your closes, And all must die.

Three stanzas, three different images. But with a common denominator of death. Or, to say it figuratively, with one solemn chord constantly recurring is the bass. And still we have but the ancillary images and idea. For it takes the fourth stanza 

"Only a sweet and virtuous soul, Like seasoned timber, never gives;

But though the whole world turn to coal, Then chiefly lives. 11 it takes that stanza to make the contrast, to present the excellent image of seasoned timber and the central idea of the deathlessness of virtue, and thereby to realize fully the title of the whole.

SO I Perhaps there is in your mind now a half-formed question something like this: "If lyrics in general are characterized by unity, and if the sonnet is a special form of the lyric, isn't a sonnet-cycle a self-contradictory term?" My first answer to that must be diplomatic, one that will keep the question open for a while; respond, "Well, yes, and no!' "Yes" because sonnets are lyrics and are therefore self-complete. But also "No", because one can have a number of emotional responses to the same object or person. 

Don't you agree that a man who is in love could write a sonnet to his sweetheart's eyes today, to her lips tomorrow, to her smile the next day, and se on? Fach sonnet, you see, would be complete in itself. That is, it would not be like a stanza in a large poem of many stanzas. 

But the series of sonnets would have unity as a group as well as individually. And so it is that a sonnet-sequence" is not really as self-contradictory a term as at first it appears to be. 

Boing the embodiment of a "single thought, feeling, or situation," the lyric is, in the third place, usually quite short. It conconveys some thing to us through both our minds and our emotions. But what caused it to issue from the poet was some stimulus to his mind and his feelings; something aroused in him an emotional response and (68)

The Lyric-III-; 

he then took that response and wrote it out in vcrse. Personal fouling, you see, is the core of the matter. But all omotions are in themselvos short-lived. Tt's something like this: You are putting down a strip of carpet. You crack down on your thumb with the tack-hammer. Your first response to put it mildly - is a loud and lusty "Ouch!" Someone rushes in to you to find out what the matter is. There you sit, your face contorted with pain, cithor shaking the thumb vigorously or else squeezing it with the other hand, mumbling incoherent subsidiary "ouches" But a week later? You merely look at the blackened nail with annoyance. 

It's ore like this: Someone meanly crosses you. You flare up in righteous indignation, nay, anger, and speak your mind. That's your emotional response at its height. An hour lcter, a little calmor, you are still indignant. But your emotional response is subsiding. 

And then a day later, you wax philosophical. You are back on en cvon kcol again; your emotional responso is gone, has been replaced by an intellectual response.

So, and for precisely the same reason, the lyric is usually short. Emotions, like the prins of smashed thumbs, burn themselves out within relatively short periods of time. It was our own Edgar 

Allan Poc, you know, who first pointed this out, in his Essay on the 

Poetic Principle. I hold, declared Poc, "that a long pcom does not exist. That degree of excitement, which would entitle a pocm to be so called at all, cannot bo sustained through a composition of any great length. After the lapso of half an hour at the very utmost," he concluded, "it flags and then the poom ... is no longer such.

Yes, the lyric is usually short, but it is not always so, and nood not always be so. Poo was thinking, perhaps, of one of Wordsworth's two famous definitions of poetry, and momentarily forgot the other one. The first of thoso definitions is, that poetry is the spontaneous overflow of powerful feelings the spontaneous overflow of powerful feelings". That's a very stimulating and provocative definition. But so also is the other one to the effect that poetry is "emotion recollected in tranquillity", "emotion recollected in tranquillity. It is this latter sort of emotion that we see at its best, perhaps, in the elegy and in the ode, two special forms of the lyric that we shall scrutinize hereafter. Or, if you wish to be off to determine this for yourselves at once, read Wordworth's Ode on the Intimations of Immortality in Early Childhood, and then say whether a long lyric is, or is not, possible. THE LYRIC IV. 

We didn't seem to cover mich ground yesterday, but that appearance need not bother us in the least, for it was rather difficult ground and it was very important ground. 

On the day before we had concluded that the first and foremost characteristic of the lyric is its subjectivity, and then we had pried into the meaning of the term subjectivity; so yesterday I returned to the subject long enough to remark that subjectivity, like its opposite, objectivity, is not absolute but only relative and that sometimes subjectivity creeps into modern artistic ballads and makes lyrical ballads of them, and that occasionally, too, subjectivity intrudes into plays and so renders them lyrical dramas. This led me to make a generalization about literary rules, a generalization to the effect that they are not precepts but records, that they are not strict and unchangeable like the laws of the old Medes and Persians but just as flexible as genius compels them to be by its successful innovations. I intended that generalization to warn you against ruleworship and against form-worship. 

Then we went on to find the second chief characteristic of the 11 lyric to be its unity, and we defined unity as oneness. In this connection we recalled the self-completeness of Shakespeare's song "When icicles hang by the wall" and of Sappho's "Hesperus" We took Palgrave's dictum, that the lyric turns "In some single thought, feeling, or situation' and verified it by applying it successfully to George Herbert's "Virtue" observing that while there are several lesser images and ideas in the poem there is a central and dominant idea that rúles all the others. And lest you be confused by an apparent contradiction, I digressed for a moment to explain the synonymous terms sonnet-cycle, sonnet-sequence, and sonnet-series as being not really self-contradictory, inasmuch as there may be a large, over-all unity embracing a considerable number of pieces each of which possesses full and unimpeachable individual unity. 

Next we found that the lyric is usually short because it embodies a single emotion and because simple emotions, as Poe pointed out long ago, are short-lived. We found a rough analogy for lyric impulse and lyric expression and lyric structure in one's reactions to a smashed thumb, and a better analogy in one's reactions in a fit of anger.

Poe went so far as to declare that " a long poem does not exist" and this statement we found to tally with Wordsworth's definition of poetry as "the spontaneous overflow of powerful feelings". 

We observed further, however, that Poe's dogmatic assertion is in conflict with Wordsworth's other definition of poetry, of poetry as "emotion recollected in tranquillity", and we looked forward to finding lyrics informed with this gentler, calmer sort of emotion in the special lyric forms of the ode and the elegy day!

So I should say that we covered quite a bit of ground yester- 

And today, after some preliminary considerations, we shall begin to survey the history of the lyric in English. (71)

You will recall the definition with which we began last Monday, namely, that the lyric originally was a peem composed to be sung by a single voice to the accompaniment of a lyre. Originally, then, two arts cooperated in the lyric. But that, as you know, is no longer generally true; most modern lyrics are composed to be recited or read without instrumental accompaniment. Are we therefore to assume that the form is now divorced from music? Not at all! 

Listen to the first stanza of Poe's "The Bells": "Hear the sledges with the bells 

Silver bells! 

What a world of merriment their melody foretells! 

How they tinkle, tinkle, tinkle, In the icy air of night! 

While the stars that oversprinkle 

All the heavens seem to twinkle 

With a crystalline delight; 

Keeping time, time, time, In a sort of Rumic rhyme •

To the tintuabulation that so musically swells From the bells, bells, bells, bells 

Bells, bells, bells, From the jingling and the tinkling of the bells.

No; it is obvious that there has been no loss of music in the modern lyric. The lyric has simply absorbed into itself the music that used to be external to it. (That absorption has of course been a gradual process, but not a constant one. In some ages it has operated scarcely at all, has gone on slowly, or perhaps stopped altogether; in others it has proceeded with startling rapidity.) 

And it is with this measuring-stick of the independence of the lyric from external accessories that one great analyst divides the history of the English lyric into four periods: first, the period in which the subject-matter of the lyric, and its language, were far less important than the musical accompaniment, were little more, indeed, than an excuse for the exhibition of vocal and instrumental ability; second, the period in which the subject-matter remained conventional but in which much more artistry was expended upon the language and the formalities involved; third, the period in which the subject-matter became of greater moment than either the language or the music; end fourth, the period in which subject-matter and form were more or less perfectly blended.

Perhaps some diagrams will assist you in mastering these points thoroughly. Turn to a fresh page in your notebook and prepare. to draw four balances, each one of them to be represented by a fouror five-inch line with a large dot or small circle in the middle of the line to indicate the axis of the balance. 

Draw the first balance as if it were tipped around its axis heavily, very heavily, to the right. On the right side, above the line, write musical accompaniment" with "vocal and instrumental" in parentheses; on the left side, write "subject matter" above the line, and "language" below it. That diagram represents the first period (v2)

in the history of the English lyric, 

the one in which "the subjectmatter of the lyri, and its language, were far less important than the musical accompaniment, were little more, indeed, than an excuse for the exhibition of vocal and instrumental ability. 1Draw the second balance tipped only very slightly to the right. On the right side, above the line write "music"; on the left side, write "language and form" above the line and "subject matter" below the line. That diagram represents the second period, the one in which the "subject matter remained conventional but in which much more artistry was expended upon the language and formalities involved.

For the third period, draw the balance tipped heavily to the left, and on the left write subject matter" above the line, and then on the right, above the line, write "music -- (dash) form.

In this period, you see, subject-matter became of greater moment. 

For your last diagram, draw your line straight across the page; that is, draw it as perfectly level. On the left, above the line, write subject", and below the line write "langunge On the right, above the line, write "form", and below it write "music".

In this the fourth and latest period, subject and form are in perfect balance. (Diagram on page 76.) 

Now number the diagrams, one, two three, and four.

In the first one, put an asterisk or star above "musical accompaniment" on the right side. In the second, put an asterisk before "language and form" on the left side. In the third, put an astrisk before subject-matter" on the left side. In the fourth, put an asterisk over the point of balance. 11 -G 

Is it driven in now? I hope so. To drive it in, was the only purpose of, and the only justification for, what we've just been doing. And please don't mislead anyone by saying that we're studying the lyric by means of diagrams. We're not. For the moment we've been studying not the lyric, but the history of the lyric, and that's a horse of quite a different color. 

These four periods, during which the lyric was developing, now along this line, now along that, until at last it evolved itself to that point of perfection which we know so familiarly today, these four periods coincide rather nicely with the great periods or divisions of English literature as a whole: the pre-Renaissance, the (73)

Renaissance, the Neo-Classical, and the Romantic periods. 

The first of these, the pre-Renaissance period, embraces all of Anglo-Saxon and Middle English poetry, and runs well up into the fifteen-hundreds, that is, into the sixteenth century. But the se eight or nine centuries were comparatively crude, and the lyric, that literary orchid, presupposes refinement and sensibility; so the lyrical verse of this period what there is of it is of interest chiefly to specialists and scholars. 

Then, much like a burst of sunshine after a dark and heavy storm, came the glorious and magnificent literary age of the English.

Renaissance, an are enduring for two -- nearly for three generations, and coming up into the sixtecn-hundreds, into the seventeenth century, and lasting even after the death of Queen Elizabeth. We have already noted in this period the importation of the Italian sonnets and the development of the English form. New lyric forms there were too, such, for example, as the songs in the plays.

But for some strange reason the poets were content to cling for the most part to mercly one of the great themes, to, love. many of them, 

The third period, the neo-Classical, takes in the later sixtecn-hundreds and almost all of the seventeen-hundreds. This was the age of Dryden and Swift and Pope and Dr. Johnson, and age of prose and reason, and an age as barren in the lyric generally as we saw it was in the sonnet specifically. 

The fourth and last age, the Romantic, began at the turn from the eighteenth to the nineteenth century, and may be said to have lasted up to the Great War. This is the age which, beginning with the Lyrical Ballads of Wordsworth and Coleridge in 1798, saw the lyric developed to its utmost perfection. 

Listen; here's Bryan W. Proctor's poem A song of the Sea". 

Never heard of Procter? Well, he's a relatively unknown figure of the nineteenth century. But here's how even a very minor poet managed the lyric in the Romantic period: 

The Sea! the Sea! the open Sea! 

The Blue, the fresh, the ever free!

Without a mark, without a bound, It runneth the earth's wide rcgions round; 

It plays with the clouds; it mocks the skies; 

Or like a cradled creature lics. 

I'm on the Sca! I'm on the Ses! 

I am where I would ever be; 

With the blue above end the blue below, And silence wheresoe'er I go; 

If a storm should come and awake the decp, What matter? I shall ride and sloop. 

I love O how I love to ride 

On the fierce, foaming, bursting tide, When every mad wave drowns the moon, Or whistles aloft his tempost tunc, (74)

And tells how goeth the world below, And why the south-west blasts do blow.

I never was on the dull, tame shore, But I loved the great Sea more and more, And backwards flew to her billowy breast, Like a bird that seeketh its mother's nest; 

And a mother she was and is to me; 

For I was born on the open Sea!

The waves were white, and red the morn, In the noisy hour when I was born; 

And the whale it whistled, the porpoise rolled, And the dolphins bared their backs of gold:

And never was heard such an outcry wild 

As welcomed to life the Ocean-child!

I've lived since then, in calm. and strife, Full fifty summers a sailor's life, With wealth to spend, and a power to range, But never have sought, nor sighed for change; 

And Death, whenever he come to me, Shall come on the wide unbounded Sea! 

One parting word: Keep on reading lyrics! (75)

THE LYRIC 

V. 

Now let's consolidate the new positions we took up yesterday. 

We observed that in the beginning the lyric was largely dependent upon vocal and instrumental accompaniment, but that gradually, during the centuries, it has absorbed into itself the music that used to be external to it. As an example of a modern lyric exhibiting specially remarkable verbal music, we read the first stanza of Poe's "The Bells": "How they tinkle, tinkle, tinkle, In the icy air of night! first,

Then by measuring the degree of musical self-sufficiency of the lyric we divided its English history into four periods: the pre-Renaissance period, extending from the beginnings of English literature in Anglo-Saxon times down into the sixteenth century, a period in which the musical accompaniment of an instrumental and a singing voice was of dominant importance; second, the Renaissance period, embracing much of the sixteenth century and s one of the seventeenth, and during which poets greatly developed the lyrical possibilities of language and form; third, the neo-Classical period, covering the later seventeenth century and most of the eighteenth, and during which most emphasis was placed on subject-matter; and fourth, the Romantic era, running from the very beginning of the nineteenth century down to the Great War, and stressing the perfection of the lyric in the achievement of balance between its content and the whole mode of in the wedding of subtle idea and exquisite its expression, verbal music. 

For what I believed to be, and still believe to be, a very good reason, we began our study together this Quarter with consideration of a very special sub-form of the lyric, namely, the sonnet. Then, last week, we backed up, as it were, to consider the general characteristics of the lyric as a large, parent form. 

And hereafter, before going on to entirely fresh fields and pastures new, we shall consider some of the other sub-forms of the lyric, such as the song-lyric, the idyll, the pastoral, the ode, and the elegy.

But don't you think it well, before we go on at all, to review the general characteristics of the lyric, and also such other matters as arose incidentally? Prepare then, if you will for a series of questions. And again I counsel you not to try to write out the questions, but simply to listen to them and then to answer them either mentally or with a written word or two. I shall proceed more slowly with the questions than I did before, but not slowly enough for you to write them out in longhand. If you wish a copy of the questions, the headquarters of the Radio College will be glad to mail you cne upon request by mail or phone. Now, however, listen to each question, answer it for yourself, and then, after I have given the correct response, make a nete as to whether your response was right or wrong. And in case of doubt, give yourself the benefit of it, for I should do so if I were marking your paper unless there were too many such cases! 

Then let's be off!

THE SONG-LYRIC 

All last week we talked about the lyric as a large, general, parent form. Today we begin to sharpen our focus on the song-lyric in particular.

The song-lyric was indeed the very first form of the lyric to take shape. Originally, we saw, the lyric, the song-lyric, was composed in Greece and sung by a single voice to the accompaniment of the lyre. We observed, however, that the modern English lyric is generally written to be read, not sung, and has absorbed into itself the music that once was external to it. 

Now we may go another step and remark that to some extent there were these two varieties of the song-lyric from its recorded beginnings in Greek literature, the one variety in which the poetry was simple and but half or less of the whole performance, and the other variety in which the poetry was less simple and indeed of such significance and beauty as to be complete in itself without instrumental accompaniment. You didn't feel - did you? that Sappho's "Hesperus was over-simple without its accompaniment? Certainly not!

But remember: Sappho was the greatest of all Greek lyric poets, and indeed has been called (though you and I will be more temperate) "the very greatest poet that ever lived" The song-lyrics of other and lesser Greek lyric poets stood much more in need of the bolstering of the sweet chords of their lyres, The beginnings of the song-lyric in English are not se gloriously perfect as are those of the Greek. In English poetry, as I have already remarked, the attainment of perfection was a long, slow, gradual process, and one that can reasonably as well as conveniently be marked off into periods. And yet, even in early 

English song-lyrics, there is occasionally a flash of utter perfection. I have said that the "lyrics" of this period are "of interest chiefly to specialists and scholars Perhaps, on the whole, you will agree with that dictum when you have heard me through, you are at least beginning scholars, aren't you? 

but Then listen, if you will, to the very oldest lyric in the English language, a lyric perhaps twelve centuries old.. In Anglo- 

Saxon, the language from which modern English is descended, through Middle English, in Anglo-Saxon the first strophe or "stanza" sounds like this Ge 

Weland hinbe wurman wraeces cunnade 

Anhydig earl earfotha dreag 

Haefde him to gesiththe sorge and langath

Wintercealde wraece wean oft onfond 

Siththan hine Nithhad on nede legde 

Swoncre cono-bende on syllan monn 

Thaet fere de, siththes swa maeg. (79)

(09.

The Song-lyric-I 

Here is that same strophe as translated in the original metre by Francis B. Gummere (capital G-u-m-m-e-r-e), Francis B. Gummere: 

"Wayland learned bitterly banishments ways, earl right resolute; ills endured; had for comrades Care and Longing, winter-cold wanderings; woe oft suffered when Nithhad forged the fetters on him, bending bonds on a better man 

That he surmounted: so this may I!"

That's the refrain all through the poem, at the end of every strophe: 

That he (or she, or they) surmounted: so this may I!

Here's another translation, or, rather, a free paraphrase 

of it by Charlton M. Lewis, Chariton M. Lewis (capital L-e-w-i-s), and this time I'll read the version entire: 

Weland, the constant-hearted, knew well what sorrow meant, When woman's vengeance laid him low and woke his long lament. 

Grief was his life-long comrade; he felt her cold caress 

When Nithhad lamed his limbs and on him laid that long distress.

Yet he strove on, and overcame; nor shall my strength be less. 

And on the breast of Beadohild her brothers' murder lay

Less heavy than her own soul's grief, when on that hateful day 

Her heart first knew what burden her outraged body bore, Awaking from that hideous dream, her woe for evermore. 

Yet she strove on, and overcame; shall I my griefs deplore? 

All know Hild's shameful fortune and that god's unfathomed sorrow 

Who through love's wakeful years of night watched weeping for the morrow;

All know how King Theodoric, by that accursed spell, Kept, motionless, for thirty years, the Maerings' citadel.

Yet these strove on, and overcame; I can endure as well. 

And all have heard of Eormanric, king of the wolfish heart,

Who scourged the Gothic kingdoms, and made his subjects smart; 

And many a man of sorrows, with sudden woe oppressed, Cried bitterly for vengeance as he beat his stricken breast.

Yet these strove on, and overcame; shall I not stand the test? (Outcast, and sorrowing in his soul, The aged harper chants his dole, And, o'er the strings his gray head bending, Sees in his heart but woe unending. 

Let him remember, and take cheer; 

The good Lord God of heaven draws near, Who for our sowing gives just reaping, And makes an end of this world's weeping.) (80)

The Song-lyric-I- 

I, Deor of the Heodenings, was dear to my good lord,

And did long minstrel service, nor missed my due reward; 

Till now this mightier minstrel thrusts my lord and me apart,

And wins my lands and living with the wiles of his high art. 

He has his day; he overcame; but peace! break not, my heart!

Now if you are a scholar, and particularly if you are a specialist in all of the old Germanic languages as well as in Anglo- Saxon, you can talk long, and with a sort of fascination, about Wayland and Nithhad and Beadohild and Hild and Theodoric and all the rest. But, just as you are, and just listening to these translations and paraphrases, don't you understand that some dear old minstrel has lost his job at some chieftan's court and is singing a pathetic sort of swan-song to himself? Over and over it's "Yet these strove on, and overcame; I can endure as well" until that heart-touching last line about his successful rival, "He had his day; he overcame; but peace! break not, my heart!" 

There is "a flash of utter perfection" in that, isn't there? 

If you wish to go further into old English lyrics, I suggest that you read in a volume published by Ginn and Company in 1902; the book is entitled Select Translations from Old English Poetry and it is edited by Albert S. Cook (capital C-o-o-k) and Chauncey B. Tinker (capital T-i-n-k-e-r), two Yale scholars of wide renown. 

Albert S. Cook, Chauncey B. Tinker,

Before we go on, let's return to the matter of the two varieties of the song-lyric and see if we can't settle upon some terms to distinguish them. Let's call the song-lyric composed primarily to be sung to music a vocal song-lyric, and let's call the song-lyric intended to be complete in itself a literary song-lyric, As I see it, there is much to be said about both of these, but more to be said about the literary song-lyric. NOTE ON THE READING OF MIDDLE ENGLISH 

She

I have here today a very pleasant letter from Miss Anna E. 

Black of Route 4, Circleville, Ohio. Miss Black is apparently much interested in good old Geoffrey Chaucer, - so much interested, indeed, that she is reading him in his native Middle English. is somewhat puzzled, however, by the final -e's, as beginning students inevitably are, and wishes to know whether they should be pronounced.

Others of you may be similarly puzzled, so here ar the pertinent rules in a nutshell: (1) Final -e is always pronounced at the end of a line. (2) Within a verse, finale is practically always elided (that is, omitted in reading), first, before an initial vowel (that is, before a vowel beginning with a, e, i, o, or u), and, second, before an h which is either silent Tas in the word honour h-o-n-o-u-r) or only slightly pronounced (as in the words he, h-e, him, h-i-m, hadde, h-a-d-d-e, and so on). (3) Within a verse, too, final -e is sometimes slurred or altogether apocopated before an initial consonant, though specific rules cannot be laid down; one's ear must be his guide. (4) And I might remark finally that within a word e is sometimes syncopated, though here again specific rules can hardly be laid down. 

I'll repeat those four rules, three of them regarding final -e, one of the regarding internal e.

These rules are of help, however, with only a small number of the difficulties involved in reading Middle English verse so that it really sounds like Middle English. The vowel and consonant sounds are quite different, in many cases, from those in Modern English. For instance, the first personal pronoun, I, in Middle 

English is pronounced eye. Again, the word night in Middle 

English is pronounced nick-t. So you have much yet to learn, Miss 

Black and you other venturesome spirits; but I assure you it's worth learning, and I heartily wish you godspeed. 

Miss Black enclosed in her letter a transcript of the song of the birds at the close of Chaucer's Parlement of Foules (that is, "Parliament of Birds"), with the request that I read it.

I'm very happy to accede to the request, though you may observe, Miss Black, that I've revised the text according to Robinson's version of it. 

Now welcome somer, with thy sonne softe,

That hast this wintres wedres overshake, And driven away the longe nyghtes blake!

Saynt Valentyn, that art ful hy on-lofte, Thus syngen smale foules for thy sake: 

Now welcome, somer, with thy sonne softe,

That hast this wintres wedres overshake. (82)

Note-Reading Middle English 

Wel han they cause for to gladen ofte, Sith ech of hem recovered hath hys make,

Ful blisful mowe they synge whan they wake: 

Now welcome, somer, with thy sonne softe,

That hast this wintres wedres overshake; 

And driven awey the longe nightes blake! 

In that song there is, you hear, a soft, a soft, final -e at the end of every line. There is also, in welcome, an example of apocope, for the word in Middle English is spelled w-e-1-c-o-m-e and normally would be pronounced as trisyllabic, but here the final -e is apocopated before the s of somer, spelled s-o-m-e-r but pronounced as if it were spelled s-u-m-e-r↓ Elision is commoner than either apocope or syncope in Middle English, yet curiously there is not an example of it in all these thirteen lines.

Let's start off today with a lyric from the pen of Geoffrey Chaucer (capital C-h-a-u-c-e-r), Geoffrey Chaucer, who died the very last year of the fourteenth century, -- aged about sixty. Chaucer's is the greatest figure in English literature up to the time of Elizabeth. Very little of the tremendous bulk of his work is in the lyric, English mediaeval master of the tale; certainly you've all heard of his Canterbury Tales; but the few lyrics he did compose are the best of their time. 

ale vide he is the

All of his work is of course in what we call Middle English. 

Middle English, as I remarked yesterday, is descended directly from Anglo-Saxon, and most of us would say it is much simpler. 

At the same time, however, it is quite different from Modern English, and doubtless most of you would say it is much more difficult. Well, perhaps it is; but, believe me, it is not nearly so difficult as it seems to be. No; don't believe me: believe your own ears. Here's one translation into Modern

English of "The Complaint of Chaucer to His Empty Purse" (that's the title: "The Complaint of Chaucer to His Empty Purse"):

COMPLAINT OF CHAUCER TO HIS EMPTY PURSE 

To you, my purse, and to none other wight 

Lament I, for you are my lady dear. 

I am so sorry now that you are light! 

Certes, unless you make me heavier cheer,

I may as well be laid upon my bier. 

Wherefore unto your mercy thus I cry- Be heavy again, else certes I must die. 

Vouchsafe the day, ere ever it be night, That I the blessed chink of you may hear 

Or see your color like the sunshine bright, That never yet for yellowness had peer. 

You are my life, mine heart you only steer,

Queen of content and of good company. 

Be heavy again, else certes I must die. 

Now, purse, who are to me my life's one light,

My life's one saviour, down in this world here, Help me out of this city through your might, Since you refuse to be my treasurer.

For I am clipped like priest or monk austere. 

But yet I pray you of your courtesy

Be heavy again, else certes I must die. (84)

Now that wasn't too difficult to follow, was it? And wasn't your ear pleased by the softer sounds of the Middle English, and by the extra sounds at the ends of the lines sounds that have disappeared in Modern English? And you now can read Middle 

English just as I did, if you will really apply yourselves to the study and practice of its sounds and rhythms for a few months. 

And don't you agree that here again in this pre-Renaissance period of the song-lyric is "a flash of utter perfection"? Can't we all, especially in these long hard days and months and year s of bitter Depression and slow Recovery, can't we all sympathize fully with such a "complaint" to an "empty purse"? And Chaucer meant it, too, for all his levity: he was fifty-nine or so, an old age for those days; in the last years of his life; and very nearly dead broke. No laughing matter, that, for most people.

But Chaucer laughed, with half his face at least, and here we are, five hundred and thirty-odd years later, laughing with him, laughing as he laughed, half through a mist of tears. 

If Chaucer much engages you, you'll find other good things of his in the books I've already suggested to you: for instance,. his "Envoy" to his friend Bukton, another piece in humorous vein, addressed to that gentleman when he was about to be married; or, in totally different vein, his "Lack of Steadfastness"; or, finally, his incomparable "Words unto Adam, His Owne Scriveyn" or copyist, warning him that hereafter he'd better copy more carefully and accurately. All of these, except perhaps the last, are lyrics, excellent literary song-lyrics in an age predominately of inferior vocal song-lyrics.

It is no difficult task to point out to you the best lyrics in Anglo-Saxon and Middle English, the two great subdivisions of the pre-Renaissance period; but as we now come somewhat formally into the period of the Renaissance itself the riches around us are really embarrassing. Let us, then, stick to songs by those song-intent Elizabethan dramatists of whom I have already spoken. 

We've already read Shakespeare's "When Icicles Hang by the Wall" from Love's Labours Lost, but do you know this "Fairy 

Lullaby" from A Midsummer Night's Dream?

HYMN TO DIANA 

Queen and Huntress, chaste and fair, Now the sun is laid to sleep, Seated in the silver chair, State in wonted manner keep:

Space to breathe, how short soever: 

Thou mak'st a day of night, Goddess excellently bright!
