THE SONG-LYRIC III.. 

We have already differentiated two varieties of the songlyric, the literary song-lyric and the vocal song-lyric. Now "vocal song-lyric" seems to me to be an awkward term; so, from now on, let's simplify "vocal song-lyric" to the familiar word "song".

And it is to the song that we shall address ourselves during most of this class-period, for I believe you will enjoy an analysis of the peculiar problems that the successful songwriter has to solve. 

His first problem is to determine which words he may use and which he may not. Of course, he can use any words, but he's wiser if he uses as many as possible that call for the mouth to be well open in singing them. 

In

Consider what this means in terms of vowel-sounds. Write the word beam (b-e-a-m). Now, prolonging it in order to observe how you hold your mouth, speak the word, say beam. Write the word tin (t-i-n). Now, again, prolonging it, say the word aloud, Write the word train (t-r-a-i-n), and try that too. every case, you see, your teeth are brought comparatively close together, your lips are drawn rather tightly over them, and your mouth is only partially opened. Obviously, then, a song-writer should avoid the close vowel-sounds in so far as he can. tin.

Now write the word father (f-a-t-h-e-r). Now speak the word, drawing it out and observing how you hold your mouth while you are producing the ah-sound, father. Notice how open and relaxed your mouth was? Well, the a in father is what we call an "open" vowel. Write hot (h-o-t), and call (c-a-1-1), and try them too, hot, call. Those vowels too are open. And again the point is obvious: the best words for songs are those with open vowelsounds. 

GD Similarly, with regard to songs, there are good and bad consonants. Pronounce the letter f; don't name it, don't add a vowel and say ef; pronounce f by itself, as if you were starting to say the word first (f-i-r-s-t), but couldn't get beyond the first sound, f. Now pronounce v by itself, v as in van, V. 

I might extend the list and give you p and b, and s and z, and many others.

All of these, you observe, almost close the mouth. 

Is the song-writer therefore to avoid them? No, not that; indeed he couldn't, even if he tried to. But he can, to a surprising degree, find words in which such consonants are followed immediately by open vowels, vowels that at once open the mouth widely. 

Thus, after f, say, the open o in foam (f-o-a-m), as Tennyson has it in "Crossing the Bar", "Too full for sound and foam"

On the other hand, the musical. possibilities of a few consonants are almost limitless. Listen to another of Tennyson's lines, - "The murmuring of bees in immemorial elms". 

How's that for verbal music? (89)

Song-Lyric-III 

"The murmuring of bees in immemorial elms a!!

Write that line out and look at it carefully, "The murmuring of bees in immemorial elms". 

There are twenty consonants in that line I've just counted them! Of these twenty, six are m's, two are n's, three are r's, and two are 1's; thirteen-twentieths, then, or sixty-five per cent, to lapse for a moment into statistics, four consonants m, n, 1, and r -- are, you know, called the liquids. And isn't the name fitting, 

are liquids. "liquids"? "The murmuring of bees in immemorial elms" These 

They do flow, don't they? And good lyrists make much use of words that possess them.

The second problem of the song-writer is one concerning metre. 

I've not yet taken any of our all-too-short class periods to go very carefully into metre, though that matter, pedestrian as it is, should not be postponed indefinitely. But I did remark, some weeks ago, that poets regularly play variations on their metrical patterns. I was thinking then of literary song-lyrics. For that is precisely what the song-writer may not do, play variations on his metrical pattern. 

He must, in fact, hold quite regularly to the normal and formal beat of the line. 

Listen, for example, to the first stanza of one of Robert 

Burns's songs, read first as poetry: "O my Luve's like a red, red rose 

That's newly sprung in June: 

O my Luve's like the melodie 

That's sweetly play'd in tune.

Now listen, if you will, to the same stanza put on the disection table of reading for scansion: "O my Luve's like a red, red rose 

That's new-ly sprung in June: 

O my Luve's like the me-lo-die 

That's sweet-ly play'd in tune. 

As regular, you see, as the beat of a metronome:

Tum-tum, tum-tum, tum-tum tum-tum, tum-tum, Tum-tum, tum-tum, tum-tum. 

That same regularity goes right through the stanzas. 

Here's the second one, unhappily mistreated, but treated so only momentarily and for a sufficient purpose: "As fair thou art, my bon-nie lass 

So deep inluve am I: 

And I will luve thee still, my dear, Till a'the seas gang dry: ་་ (90)

Song-Lyric-III 

Contrast with that, the metre of Shelley's literary songlyric, "To Night", the first stanza: "Swiftly walk o'er the western wave, Spirit of Night! 

Out of the misty eastern cave 

Where, all the long and lone daylight, Thou wovest dreams of joy and fear 

Which make thee terrible and dear,-- 

Swift be thy flight!"

I read that, of course, as poetry. I can't read it otherwise; I can't read it so that it will scan regularly, unless I wrench the accent of swift-ly to swift-ly, of spi-rit to spi-rit, and so on, and that, as you know, may not be done, In short, the metre of a literary lyric is inspired by variety, but the metre of a song is best when it is smooth and firm and regular.

Moreover, if the song is written to fit a melody, as many of Burns's songs were, and Tom Moore's too, open vowels and long notes should come together. The third problem of the song-writer focuses on the subjectmatter of the song. If the song is composed of two or more stanza as most songs are, the poet must see to it that the subject-matter of each stanza runs quite closely parallel to the subject-matter of each of the other stanzas, for usually they are all to be sung to one and the same melody, and there is, at least ideally, a close connection between the melody and the burden of idea and sentiment.

If that statement seems to be involved, perhaps an illustration will make the meaning of it more clear. Take that exquisite song of F. W. Bourdillon (capital B-o-u-r-d-1-1-1-o-n), "The 

Night Has a Thousand Eyes": 

"The night has a thous and eyes, And the day but one; 

Yet the light of the bright world dies 

With the dying sun. "The mind has a thousand eyes, And the heart but one;

Yet the light of a whole life dies 

When love is done."

Don't you perceive in that the parallel between the two images? 

Just as sunset brings darkness over the world, so the death of love brings darkness over human life. Whether this song has been set to music, I don't know; but I'm sure that a melody fitting for the first stanza would be equally fitting for the second. 

And no matter whether his song is in several stanzas or in none, the poet must see to it that the subject-matter is relatively simple in itself and quite simple în its presentation. 

The place for elaborate imagery, for delicate feeling, and for subtle (91)

Song-Lyric-III 

thought, is in the literary song-lyric, That may be read over and over and over again. But a song must reveal itself at only one hearing, and must indeed reveal itself through less than half of the hearer's attention, for of course his chief attention is given, and should properly be given, to the music, and dominant art in the combination. 

I venture I can lose you easily enough in reading you an intricate literary song-lyric, but if anyone can lose you in reading you a song you may depend upon it that that song, as a song, is a failure. Let's try the experiment. Here's Shelley's "Music, when soft voices die": "Music, when soft voices die,

Vibrates in the memory 

Odors, when sweet violets sicken, Live within the sense they quicken. "Rose leaves, when the rose is dead, Are heaped for the beloved's bed; 

And so thy thoughts, when thou art gone, Love itself shall slumber on. 

You perceived that as lovely, yes; and certainly, as you heard it, you understood it in part; but wouldn't you prefer, before being examined on it, if such an examination were possible, wouldn't you prefer to go over it by yourself? a literary song-lyric. 

Here's a delightful song-lyric, "Sally in Our Alley",

Henry Carey (capital C-a-r-e-y), the first two stanzas: 

Of all the girls that are so sweet 

There's none like pretty Sally; 

She is the darling of my heart, And she lives in our alley. 

There is no lady in the land 

Is half so sweet as Sally: 

She is the darling of my heart, And she lives in our alley. 1em; 

Her father he makes cabbage-nets 

And through the streets does cry 

Her mother she sells laces long 

To such as please to buy 'em; 

But sure such folks could ne'er beget 

Sa sweet a girl as Sally! 

She is the darling of my heart, And she lives in our alley. 

There are five more stanzas, know! 

That's by and Sally's a girl you ought to (92)

Song-Lyric-III 

You may have had the idea that vocal song-lyrics, even good songs, are easy to compose, and easier by far than literary song-lyrics.. If you did have that idea, I hope I have succeeded at least in modifying it considerably. 

And I do believe that if you will ponder and apply the principles I have set forth today you will find yourselves able to determine whether any given song-lyric is a vocal song-lyric or a literary song-lyric. (93)

THE ODE 

1 I. 

The rainbow comes and goes, And lovely is the Rose,

The Moon doth with delight 

Look round her when the heavens are bare; 

Waters on a starry night 

Are beautiful and fair; 

The sunshine is a glorious birth; 

But yet I know, where'er I go,

That there hath passed away a glory from the earth. 

Of course you recognize that: it's the second strophe from

Wordsworth's ode on "Intimations of Immortality from Recollec tions of Early Childhood", the ode that I suggested you read some time ago. The title has always seemed to me to be a bit too cumbrous and catalogish, but at the same time the poem as a whole seems to me to be one of the greatest in English literature; 

The Moon doth with delight 

Look round her when the heavens are bare; 

Waters on a starry night 

Are beautiful and fair; glorious in detached images and lines, like those, and even more glorious in its entirety. We'll come back to it, perhaps by the end of this period, and at least start to go through it carefully together; but let's first sketch in some general background of understanding for the ode as a lyric form. 

The English word ode (spelled o-d-e) is derived ultimately from the Greek, and it was in Greek poetry that the ode began. 

Greatest of the Greeks in writing odes was Pindar (spelled capital P-i-n-d-a-r), whose life spanned the seventy-four years between 522 B. C. and 448 B. C. As a youth, Pindar was apprenticed as a musician. Later he began writing poems on public occasions. And it was to celebrate the winners of the games held in honor of the gods that he worked out his structurally elaborate and complex odes. In the Reading List I have suggested that you read a translation of his first Olympian ode, celebrating Hiero (spelled capital H-i-e-r-o), Hiero the Syracusian, victor in the horse race.

If you will analyze that ode you will find it is composed of a strophe and an antistrophe in identical form and of an epode of contrasting form. Strophe is spelled s-t-r-o-p-h-e and in the Greek choral dance was the movement of the chorus while turning from the right to the left of the orchestra. For you must understand that the Greek ode, a variety of the Greek lyric, was not only sung to instrumental accompaniment, but was also furnished with the accompaniment of a dance on a great public stage. The word strophe, then, is used as a term both for the movement of the chorus from the right to the left and for the group of lines of poetry that the chorus sang while making that movement. Antistrophe is spelled a-n-t-i-s-t-r-o-p-h- (99)

Ode-I-, and was the counterturn of the chorus from the left to the right, and of course it was also the corresponding part of the choral song. Epode is spelled e-p-o-d-e, and was the stand of the chorus, as well as that part of the lyric ode which followed the strophe and antistrophe. 

Three groups of lines, then, the strophe, or turn; the antistrophe, or counterturn; and the epode, or stand, or any multiple of the three groups, composed the true Pindaric ode, which obviously was, as I have already remarked "structurally elaborate and complex" so complex, indeed, that it is hardly fully understood by scholars even today. 

In the Reading List I have suggested that you read only two or three examples of what may be called the Anglo-Pindaric ode, that is, of English odes that follow the pattern and form of the true Pindaric ode. One is Ben Jonson's ode "To the Immortal 

Memory and Friendship of that Noble Fair, Sir Lucius Cary and Sir H. Morison" a product of the Renaissance; and another is 

Thomas Gray's "The Progress of Poetry", a product of the eighteenth century. Why, you may ask, 'so few? 'Because, I answer, "there are only a very few Anglo-Pindaric odes in English litorature." 

11 11 ?? - G perhaps

Both Jonson and Gray were extremely learned men, not so learned as some of our great modern scholars, but nearly So, and quite learned, as was the general thing in England up through the eighteenth century, in the classics, both Greek and Roman. And the fact is that only remarkably close students of the classics could reproduce correctly even the form of Pindaric odes, let alone their informing spirit, for the form is really far more intricate than I have indicated. 

If I were you, I would concentrate on Gray's "The Progress of Poesy". I would study it in an annotated edition (Gray's own notes will do). I would read it slowly and thoughtfully many times, until I understood it both in minute detail and as a comprehensive whole, until I could see what a tremendous accomplishment it was, and also until I could see that the form, lacking the aids of singing voices, of plucked lyres, and of choral dances, is really alien to English literature. 

But don't, I pray you, don't give up; don't finally cease your study of the Anglo-Pindaric ode until such lines as these reveal their riches to you, lines about Albion, the poet's name for England, and about Shakespeare, and about Nature: 

Far from the sun and summer-gale,

In thy green lap was Nature's Darling laid, What time, time, where lucid Avon strayed, To Him the mighty Mother did unveil

Her awful face: The Dauntless Child 

Stretched forth his little arms, and smiled.

This pencil take (she said) whose colors clear 

Richly paint the vernal year: 

Thine too the se vernal keys, immortal Boy! 

This can unlock the gates of Joy; 

Of Horror that, and thrilling Fears,

Or ope the sacred source of sympathetic Tears. (100)

Ode-I- I cannot be sorry the t, reading it to you over the radio, I cannot make that strophe appear to be more readily meaningful; 

I know, as you will learn if you do not already know, that the deepest joys and sorrows and intellectual and spiritual experiences of life are intensely personal and individual: they cannot really or honestly be sugar-coated and made into pills that can be swallowed easily and quickly. 11 One of rare Ben Jonson's followers, and forty-five years Ben's junior, was Abraham Cowley (spelled C-o-w-l-e-y). Cowley was a competent Latin scholar, but his Greek -- well, his "Pindarics", Felix Schelling says, "in their slovenly disregard of the rules of the form so flaunted in their titles, had much to do with the abuse of the torm, ode, in later English poetry to signify almost any poem of a more or less serious intent, written in irregular metre. Schelling (spelled capital 

S-c-h-e-1-1-1-n-g) puts it mildly in the phrase "slovenly disregard of the rules of form"; other critics say bluntly that 

Cowley was ignorant of them, so ignorant that he misurderstood the intricate irregularity of Pindar's ode to be "highly irregular, with changes of line and stanza to suit every change of mood. Hence the term "false Pindaric ode,' some times softened to "free ode." !! 11 Most of the odes written à la Cowley have been utterly worthless, yet some are of undisputable greatness. One of these latter is John Dryden's "Alexander's Feast, or, The Power of 

Music; an Ode in Honor of St. Cecilia's Day" written in 1697, and thrice set to music the third time, in 1736, by Handel. 

In a letter to a friend, shortly after the ode was published as a pamphlet, Dryden wrote: "I am glad to hear from all hands, that my Ode is esteem'd the best of all my poetry, by all the town: I thought so myself when I writ it; but being old (Dryden was then sixty-six), I mistrusted my own judgment.

No need to mistrust that judgment, John; we're all agreed it's a great ode, a great poem. And many who have never read it have somewhere picked up the fourth and last line of the first chorus, which goes like this: 

Happy, happy, happy pair! 

None but the brave, None but the brave, None but the brave deserves the fair.

Only, I'm afraid, those who know the line indirectly, at second hand, usually misquote it as 

None but the brave deserve the fair.

Another of the great free odes in English poetry is Wordsworth's on his "Intimations of Immortality", from which I quoted at the beginning of the period. It's only a little longer than "Alexander's Feast", if you go by the number of lines: but if you go by its content, it's immeasurably longer. Dryden narrates for a while, pauses for a chorus, tells some more, pauses again, and so on. Wordsworth, however, is descriptive and reflective (101)

Ode-I- and philosophical and mystical all the way through, and Wordsworth's descriptions and reflections and philosophy and mysticism may well give any reader pause. 

Let's start through it together and go as far as we can before the close of the period: There was a time when meadow, grove, and stream, The earth, and every common sight, To me did seem 

Apparelled in celestial light, The glory and the freshness of a dream.

It is not now as it hath been of yore;-- 

Turn wheresoe'er I may, By night or day,

The things which I have seen I now can see no more. II 

The Rainbow comes and goes, And lovely is the Rose; 

The Moon doth with delight 

Look round her when the heavens are bare; 

Waters on a starry night 

Are beautiful and fair; 

The sunshine is a glorious birth; 

But yet I know, Where'er I go, That there hath passed away a glory from the earth. Now

III while the Birds thus sing a joyous song, And while the young lambs bound 

As to the tabor's sound, To me alone there came a thought of grief:

A timely utterance gave that thought relief, And I again am strong

The Cataracts blow their trumpets from the steep; 

No more shall grief of mine the season wrong; 

I hear the Echoes through the mountains throng,

The Winds come to me from the fields of sleep, And all the earth is gay; 

Land and sea 

Give themselves up to jollity, And with the heart of May 

Doth every beast keep holiday; 

Thou child of joy, Shout round me, let me hear thy shouts, thou happy Shepherd-boy! (102)

Ode-I- 

IV 

Ye blessed Creatures, I have heard the call 

Ye to each other make; I see 

The heavens laugh with you in your jubilee; 

My heart is at your festival, My head hath its coronal, The fulness of your bliss, I feel 31 

I feel it all. 

O evil day! if I were sullen

While the Earth herself is adorning 

This sweet May-morning, And the children are pulling 

On every side,

In a thousand valleys far and wide, Fresh flowers; while the sun shines warm, And the babe leaps up on his mother's arm: 

I hear, I hear, with joy I hear!

But there's a Tree, of many one, A single Field which I have looked upon, Both of them speak of something that is gone; 

The Pansy at my feet 

Doth the same tale repeat: 

Whither is fled the visionary gleam? 

Where is it now, the glory and the dream?

These questions echoed for many months in Wordsworth's heart and mind: 

Whither is fled the visionary gleam? 

Where is it now, the glory and the dream? (103)

THE ODE 

II. 9. 

Yesterday we broke off our reading of Wordsworth's ode on the "Intimations of Immortality from Recollections of Early 

Childhood" at the end of the four first strophes. or stanzas as Wordsworth himself rather inaccurately calls them. That break of ours was well planned, for Wordsworth says in his prefatory note to the poem that "Two years at least passed between the writing of the first four stanzas and the remaining part". 

What else Wordsworth says in the prefatory note is well worth our reading together, I think, because many of you may be reading the Ode in an edition that does not give that note. 

The note goes on as follows: "To the attentive and competent reader the whole sufficiently explains itself; but there may be no harm in adverting here to particular feelings or experiences of my own mind on which the structure of the poem partly rests. 

Nothing was more difficult for me in childhood than to admit the notion of death as a state applicable to my own being. I have said elsewhere 'A simple child 

That lightly draws its breath 

And feels its life in every limb 

What should it know of death!' 

But it was not so much from feelings of animal vivacity that my difficulty came as from a sense of the indomitableness of the Spirit within me. I used to brood over the stories of Enoch and 

Elijah, and almost to rersuade myself that, whatever might become of others, I should be translated, in something of the same.way, to heaven. With a feeling congenial to this, I was often unable to think of external things as having external existence, and I communed with all that I saw as but 

Something not apart from, inherent in, my own immaterial nature. Many times while going to school have I grasped at a wall or tree to recall myself from this abyss of idealism to the reality. At that time I was afraid of such processes. In later periods of life I have deplored, as we have all reason to do, a subjugation of an opposite character, and have rejoiced over the remembrances, as expressed in the lines 

'Obstinate questionings 

Of sense and outward things, Fallings from, us, vanishings;' etc. 

To that dream-like vividness and splendour which invest objects of sight in childhood, every one, I believe, if he would look back, could hear testimony, and I need not dwell upon it here: but having in the poem regarded it as presumptive evidence of a prior state of existence, I think it right to protest against a conclusion, which has giver pain to some good and pious persons, that I meant to that I meant to inculcate such a belief. It is far too shadowy a notion to be recommended to faith, as more than an element in our instincts of immortality. But let us bear in mind that, though the idea is not advanced in revelation, there is nothing there to contradict it, and the fall of Man presents. (104)

Ode-II

an analogy in its favour. Accordingly, a pre-existent state has entered into the popular creeds of many nations; and, among all persons acquainted with classic literature, is known as an ingredient in Platonic philosophy. Archimedes said that he could move the world if he had a point whereon to rest his machine. 

Who has not felt the same aspirations as regards the world of his own mind? Having to wield some of its elements when I was impelled to write this poem on the 'Immortality of the Soul, I took hold of the notion of pre-existence as having sufficient foundation in humanity for authorising me to make for my purpose the best use of it I could as a poet. 'The Child is Father of the Man; 

And I could wish my days to be 

Bound each to each by natural piety.'" 

(Cambridge Edition of Wordsworth's Complete Poetical Works, p.353). 

That illuminating long note leads up directly to the next two strophes, the fifth and sixth, both of which are concerned with this idea of a prenatal existence, the memory of which gradually fados as we pass from infancy to manhood: 

Our birth is but a sleep and a forgetting:

The Soul that rises with us, our life's Star Hath had elsewhere its setting, And cometh from afar: 

Not in entire forgetfulness, And not in utter nakedness, But trailing clouds of glory do we come 

From God, who is our home: 

Heaven lies about us in our infancy!

Shades of the prison-house begin to close 

Upon the growing Boy,

But he beholds the light, and whence it flows, He sees it in his joy;

The Youth, who daily farther from the East 

Must travel, still is Nature's Priest, And by the vision splendid 

Is on his way attended;

At length the Man perceives it die away, And fade into the light of common day.

VI Earth fills her lap with pleasures of her own; 

Yearnings she hath in her own natural kind,

And, even with something of a mother's mind, And no unworthy aim,

The homely Nurse doth all she can 

To make her foster-child, her inmate, Man, Forget the glories he hath known, And that imperial palace whence he came. (105)

Then follows the seventh strophe, a favorite of mine for many years, and an even greater favorite now that I have the privilege and pleasure of observing the development of my young son:

VII 

Behold the Child among his new-born blisses, A six years' darling of a pigmy size! 

See, whore 'mid work of his own hand he lies, Fretted by sallies of his mother's kisses 

With light upon him from his father's eyes! 

See, at his feet, some little plan or chart,

Some fragmont from his dream of human life, Shaped by himself with newly-loarned art; 

A wedding or a festival, A mourning or a funeral; 

And this hath now his heart, And unto this he frames his song: 

Thon will he fit his tongue 

To dialogues of business, love, or strife; 

But it will not be long

Ero this be thrown aside, And with now joy and prido

The little actor cons another part; 

Filling from time to tire is "humorous stage"

With all the persons, down to palsied Age, That Life brings with her in her equipage;

As if his whole vocation 

Were endloss imitation. (106)

Ode-II- 

Aren't those marvelously descriptive and compressed lines? 

That's poetry. 

Fretted by sallies of his mother's kisses, With light upon him from his father's eyes!

Then comes, in the next strophe, Wordsworth's magnificent apostrophe to the Child, to the Child with a capital 

VIII c: 

Thou, whose exterior semblance doth belie 

Thy soul's immensity; 

Thou best philosopher, who yet dost keep 

Thy heritage, thou eye among the blind, That, deaf and silent, read st the eternal deep, Haunted for ever by the eternal Mind,- 

Mighty Prophet! Seer blest! 

On whom those truths do rest

Which we are toiling all our lives to find, In darkness lost, the darkness of the grave; 

Thou over whom thy immortality 

Broods like the day, a master o'er a slave,

A Presence which is not to be put by; 

Thou little Child, yet glorious in the might 

Of heaven-born freedom on thy being's height 

Why with such earnest pains doth thou provoke 

The years to bring the inevitable yoke; 

Thus blindly with thy blessedness at strife? 

Full soon thy soul shall have her earthly freight,

And custom lie upon thee with a weight 

Heavy as frost, and deep almost as life!

Who that is grown will not echo, as if he were alone and by himself the whole mighty chorus on a world-stage, those last lines? 

Why with such earnest pains dost thou provoke

The years to bring the inevitable yoke, Thus blindly with thy blessedness at strife? 

Full soon thy soul shal have her earthly freight,

And custom lie upon thee with a weight, Heavy as frost, and deep almost as life! 

That's it: 

Heavy as frost, and deep almost as life! (107)

THE ODE III. 

That there was a break in time after the fourth strophe of Wordsworth's greatest Ode, we know from the poet himself. And one of his best editors points out that also after that strophe there is a break in the treatment of the subject, just as there is another such break after the eighth strophe. The subject of the 

Ode, says this editor, Andrew J. George (spelled capital 

G-e-o-r-g-e), is "the origin, development, and destiny of the human soul". The first four stanzas those we read together 

Monday set forth our common experience in growing up, and at last epitomize it in a question, 

"Where is it now, the glory and the dream?" 

The next review the that is, where is the "visionary gleam" of childhood? four stanzas those we read together yesterday history of the soul: show it "trailing clouds of glory" as it comes "from afar"; trace it through infancy, boyhood, youth, and manhood; and close with another question, this one addressed to the child: "Why with such earnest pains dost thou provoke 

The years to bring the inevitable yoke, Thus blindly with thy blessedness at strife?

So far in the Ode we have already come. 

The last three stanzas point the way to peace, or at least to the way in which Wordsworth found peace: the cherishing of old memories, the enjoyment of présent life in the light of those memories, and the attainment of compassion. IX 

O joy that in our embers 

Is something that doth live, That nature yet remembers 

What was so fugitive!

The thought of our past years in me doth breed 

Perpetual benediction: 

not indeed

For that which is most worthy to be blest, Delight and liberty, the simple creed 

Of Childhood, whether busy or at rest, With new-fledged hope still fluttering in his breast:- 

Not for these I raise 

The song of thanks and praise;

But for those obstinate questionings 

Of sense and outward things 

Fallings from us, vanishings,

Moving about in worlds not realised, High instincts before which our mortal nature 

Did tremble like a guilty thing surprised: 

But for those first affections, Those shadowy recollections 

Which, be they what they may, Are yet the fountain light of all our day 

Are yet a master light of all our seeing; (108)

Ode-III 

Uphold us, cherish, and have power to make Our noisy years seem moments in the being 

Of the eternal Silence: truths that wake, To perish never; 

Which neither listlessness, nor mad endeavour, Nor Man nor Boy,

Nor all that is at enmity with joy, Can utterly abolish or destroy!

Hence, in a season of calm weather, Though inland far we be, Our souls have sight of that immortal sea 

Which brought us hither; 

Can in a moment travel thither 

And see the children sport upon the shore, And hear the mighty waters rolling evermore.

Then sing, ye Birds, sing, sing a joyous song! 

And let the young Lambs bound 

As to the tabor's sound! 

We, in thought, will join your throng, Ye that pipe and ye that play, Ye that through your hearts to-day 

Feel the gladness of the May! 

What though the radiance which was once so bright 

Be now for ever taken from my sight,

Though nothing can bring back the hour 

Of splendour in the grass, of glory in the flower; 

We will grieve not, rather find 

Strength in what remains behind;

In the primal sympathy 

Which having been must ever be; 

In the soothing thoughts that spring 

Out of human suffering; 

In the faith that looks through death;

In years that bring the philosophic mind. ΧΙ 

And 0, ye Fountains, Meadows, Hills, and Groves 

Think not of any severing of our loves! 

Yet in my heart of hearts I feel your might;

I only have relinquished one delight 

To live beneath your more habitual sway: 

I love the Brooks which down their channels fret, Even more than when I tripped lightly as they; 

The innocent brightness of a new-born Day 

Is lovely yet; 

The Clouds that gather round the setting sun 

Do take a sober colouring from an eye 

That hath kept watch o'er man's mortality; 

Another race hath been, and other palms are won. 

Thanks to the human heart by which we live, Thanks to its tenderness, its joys, and fears, To me the meanest flower that blows can give 

Thoughts that do often lie too deep for tears. (109)

Ode-III 

Observe the great final lines of each of these three stanzas? "And hear the mighty waters rolling evermore." And "In years that bring the philosophic mind. And "Thoughts that do often lie too deep for tears.

This ode of Wordsworth's, like most of Wordsworth's poetry, is not candy for infants. No really thoughtful poetry is. If. you would master this ode, you must really study it, must read it over and over and over again. He who is materially-minded will get little from it even then. But he who has eyes and ears and heart for the things of the spirit, for those intangible things which are after all the greatest goods knowable by man, for him, be he young or old, this ode will prove itself a veritably inexhaustibe treasure all the days of his life. 

Nothing is ever said with utter finality, you know, until a poet says it. For many, many of your richest experiences you will find that Wordsworth offers the only adequate word-expres sions. Many of these word-expressions are in this ode: Haven't you found some already? How about this: "Waters on a starry night 

Are beautiful and fair"? 

Simple? Certainly! But aren't the very greatest things, the greatest men and women, invariably and unalterably simple? How about "the inevitable yoke"? No? You've not felt any such yoke? 

Don't be impatient: you will. 

And then, it may be, rereading 

Wordsworth, you'll come across that phrase with all the delight and pride of an original discoverer, and we who have long known it will say to ourselves to ourselves, mind you; not to you! why should we cloud the title to your treasure? "I knew it!" 

we'll say, You will

As the third example of the free ode I've suggested 

Tennyson's "Ode on the Death of the Duke of Wellington". of course recall that it was the Duke of Wellington who commanded the allied forces that defeated the armies of Napoleon at the Battle of Waterloo in 1815, and you may know further that after that battle the Duke served his country long and well as statesman and counsellor. Tennyson's Ode was the noble expression of the

English nation's bereavement at his death; and it yet lives on into the twentieth century, as relatively little of Tennyson does live on, unfaded. When, for instance, during the Great War, Lord Kitchener was drowned somewhere in the northern seas, the English recalled this Ode of Tennyson's as the only adequate expression of their grief. (110)

Ode-III 

I will not read it in its entirety, but there are three passages of it that I will read. The first is a short passage. in the fourth strophe in which Tennyson is setting forth the element of the Duke's character. He was, says the poet, "Rich in saving common-sense, And, as the greatest only are, In his simplicity sublime. 11 Can you think of a higher tribute to pay a leader? 

The second passage occurs about the middle of the eight strophe: "Not once or twice in our rough island-story

The path of duty was the path to glory. 

He that walks it, only thirsting ? 

For the right, and learns to deaden 

Love of self, before his journey closes

He shall find the stubborn thistle bursting 

Into glossy purples, which out-redden 

All voluptuous garden-roses:

Not once or twice in our fair island-story The path of duty was the path to glory. 

He, that ever following her commands,

On with toil of heart and knees and hands, Thro' the long gorge to the far light has won 

His path upward, and prevail'd, Shall find the topping crags of Duty scaled 

Are close upon the shining table-lands 

To which our God himself is moon and sun. 

Such was he."

The third and last passage is the loftiest of them all, and is as timeless and as eternally appropriate as great poetry always is: "For tho the Giant Ages heave the hill 

And break the shore, and evermore 

Make and break, and work their will 

Tho' world on world in myriad myriads roll 

Round us, each with different powers, And other forms of life than ours, What know we greater than the soul? 

On God and Godlike men we build our trust."

Aren't those noble thoughts and lines to throw into the faces and teeth of those little thinkers and pseudo-scientists who, because they low some geology and some astronomy, would upset the normal man's straight thinking by their wild conjectures and even wilder talk of the soul, of which they know no more or, indeed, less 

than others? Listen again:

Ode-III 

"For tho' the Giant Ages heave the hill 

And break the shore, and evermore 

Make and break, and work their will, Tho' world on world in myriad myriads roll 

Round us, each with different powers, And other forms of life than ours, What know we greater than the soul? 

On God and Godlike men we build our trust."

I have no intention whatever of turning this class in English literature into a milk-and-water Sunday-school class, or this microphone into a conventionally moralistic pulpit; but the plain fact is that the connection between literature and life is so close that great literature not informed by high ethical principle is simply impossible. It isn't that literature comes into being didactically to inculcate morality; it's merely that great literature always is ultimately moral. - Lam haven't I? 

I've already remarked that "nothing is ever said finally until a poet says it". Well, here's a poet saying what I've just said, only infinitely better: "Beauty is truth, truth beauty, that is all 

Ye know on earth, and all ye need to know. 

11 

That's John Keats speaking, in the last two lines of his "Ode on a Grecian Urn". I've named that Ode on the Reading List, among the so-called Anglo-Horatian odes, odes that are composed not on the model of Pindar's, and not on the model of Cowley's "free" odes, but in regular stanzas that, in odes, suggest the regular structure of Horace's odes. Such also are Keats's "To Autumn and "To a Nightingale", and Shelley's "To a Skylark" and "To the 

Westwind", and our American Bryant's "To a Waterfowl". I'd like to read these with you, and to go back to Milton's ode "On the 

Morning of Christ's Nativity" and to Collins's "Ode to Evening", and even to Gray's burlesque ode "On the Death of a Favourite Cat" ; but, you know, tempus fugit, and there is much more for us to do; and, besides, I undertook to be only your guide, philosopher and friend, while you undertook to read and read and READ! THE ELEGY 

I. 

The special lyrical forms that are to be found in the idyll (spelled i-d-y-1-1), the pastoral (spelled p-a-s-t-o-r-a-1) and the elegy (spelled e-l-e-g-y), are very closely interrelated, that therefore it is only with considerable difficulty that they can be separated and spoken of as separate types. Let us hegin, however, by looking into the elegy, and by reading together the most famous elegy in English literature, Gray's "Elegy written in a Country Churchyard".

Thomas Gray, whose life spanned the fifty-five years from 1716 to 1771, is one of the great figures in English poetry of the eighteenth century, and a considerable figure in English poetry of all centuries. Gray was a shy, scholarly, retiring man who, despite his learning and his genius, preferred to live not in the society of the metropolis but in the quiet seclusion of the country. In poetry his taste was fastidiousness itself, and it is, therefore, chiefly, that he wrote comparatively little, and that he polished that little to the utmost perfection. We have already glanced at his admirable Anglo-Pindaric odes. Now we turn to his very greatest poem, his "Elegy written in a Country Churchyard", published in 1781, and a piece that we know he worked on for four years and have reason to believe he worked on for eight years. The New International Dictionary defines elegy as "a mournful or plaintive poem; a funeral song; a poem of lamentation" That of course, you knew before, but it is well, I believe you will agree, as certainly a butcher would, to sharpen even familiar and muchused tools before using them anew. An elegy, then, is a lament. 

Usually the lament of an elegy is for an individual or a group of individuals, real or imagined, as we shall see. Gray's lament, however, is occasioned not by the death of an individual, but by his contemplation of death generally, of death as it is to be seen and thought of in a country churchyard. Gra had a special churchyard in mind, that of Stoke Poges (spelled capital S-t-o-k-e, capital P-o-g-e-s), which you may still go to see, if you will; but any English country churchyard would do equally well (there's no mention of Stoke Poges in the title of the elegy); indeed, any country churchyard will do. 

You are familiar, aren't you, with churchyards used as graveyards, as cemeteries, as burial places? The custom of regularly burying the dead in churchyards is rapidly dying out in America, but there are many such churchyards still in occasional use, and you may see a number of them in Ohio. 

And sometime, if you've never done so, get out of your car and wander leisurely through a churchyard that has long been used as a graveyard. Read, if you can, the pious sentiments cut on the headstones. Mark you can't help it the weathering and decay of those stones, many of them falen askew, more of them scaled and splintered and illegible. And then try you'll not be successful; but you can try, if you want to, try not to think of what mothers 

(119)

38

Elegy-I and fathers and youths and maidens and old men and infants lie sleeping there; try not to think of death, death with a capital "d", as an universal and humbling power.

Gray didn't try not to think that way. He was by nature reflective, philosophical, melancholy, and I have no doubt that he was one of those strange souls who enjoy the atmosphere of a graveyard. I don't, not even now that Death has struck close to me and carried off all that was mortal of one I loved most dearly. 

Yet, reading Gray's "Elegy", I willingly go with him into a country churchyard, as I have gone by myself into famous American churchyards in Boston and Cambridge and Baltimore, and think with him his mournful thoughts on death. For they are not merely plaintive thoughts; they are also humbling thoughts, thoughts that recall one from any false pride that may have momentarily seduced him, thoughts that cleanse and refresh and strengthen the spirit. There are references and allusions in the poem that are not natively familiar to us. Few of us can recall the days of the curfew; I don't know of any yew-trees in America; the hunter's horn, save in the chases of a few ultra-smart and artificial hunting clubs, is unknown to us; we have few churches of longdrawn aisle and fretted vault', as have the English, in which we bury our notables; Hampden was an English patriot, as was 

Cromwell; and so on. And further, we aren't much used to personifications in poetry. such as Ambition, spelled with a capital "a" and thought of as a person, or Grandeur with a capital "g", Memory, and, so on. Yet, a moment ago, I spoke naturally of "Death with a capital d'" and you felt no shock, did you? And isn't most old literature crowded with strange knowledge that we first pick up in reading it? or I think we can now read the poem with understanding and sympathy. Let me, however, make one remark about the proper tempo in which to read it. You read all poetry reasonably slowly; read elegies quite slowly; make the difference in tempo comparable to the difference in tempo between an ordinary military march on the one hand, and, on the other, let's say Chopin's Funeral March. 

And now here's Gray's "Elegy":

The Curfew tolls the knell of parting day, The lowing herd wind slowly o'er the lea The plowman homeward plods his weary way, And leaves the world to darkness and to me.

Now fades the glimmering landscape on the sight, And all the air a solemn stillness holds, Save where the beetle wheels his droning flight, And drowsy tinklings lull the distant folds;

Save that from yonder ivy-mantled tow'r 

The mopeing owl does to the moon complain 

Of such, as wand'ring near her secret bow'r, Molest her ancient solitary reign. (120)

Elegy 

Beneath those rugged elms, that yew-troe's shade,

Where heaves the turf in many a mould'ring heap, Each in his narrow cell for ever laid, The rude Forefathers of the hamlet sleep. 

The breezy call of incense-breathing Morn, The swallow twitt'ring from the straw-built shed, The cock's shrill clarion, or the echoing horn,

No more shall rouse them from their lowly bed. 

For them no more the blazing hearth shall burn, Or busy housewife ply her evening care; 

No children run to lisp their sire's return, Or climb his knees the envied kiss to share.

Oft did the harvest to their sickle yield, Their furrow oft the stubborn glebe has broke; 

How jocund did they drive their team afield! 

How bow'd the woods beneath their sturdy stroke! 

Let not Ambition mock their useful toil,

Their homely joys, and destiny obscure: 

Nor Grandeur hear with a disdainful smile,

The short and simple annals of the poor. 

The bosst of heraldry, the pomp of pow'r, And all that beauty, all that wealth e'r gave, Awaits alike th' inevitable hour: 

The paths of glory lead but to the grave. 

Nor you, ye Proud, impute to These the fault, If Mom'ry o'er their Tomb no Trophies raise,

Where thro' the long-drawn isle and fretted vault 

The pealing anthem swells the note of praise. 

Can storied urn or animated bust 

Back to its mansion call the fleeting breath? 

Can Honour's voice provoke the silent dust, Or Flatt ry sooth the dull cold ear of Death?

Perhaps in this neglected spot is laid 

Some heart once pregnant with celestial fire;

Hands, that the rod of empire might have sway'd, Or wak'd to extasy the living lyre. 

But Knowledge to their eyes her ample page,

Rich with the spoils of time did no'er unróll; 

Chill Penury repress'd their noble rage, And froze the genial current of the soul. 

Full many a gem of purost ray serene, The dark unfathom'd caves of ocean bear: 

Full many a flower is born to blush unseen, And waste its swestness on the desert air. (121)

Elogy-I 

Some village-Hampden, that with dauntless breast The little Tyrant of his fields withstood; 

Some mute inglorious Milton here may rest, Some Cromwell guiltless of his country's blood. 

Th' applause of list'ning senates to command,

The threats of main and ruin to despise, To scatter plenty o'er a smiling land, And read their hist'ry in a nation's eyes, Their lot forbad: nor circumscrib'd alone 

Their growing virtues, but their crimes confin'd; 

Forbad to wade through slaughter to a throne 

And shut the gates of mercy on mankind,

The struggling pangs of conscious truth to hide, To quench the blushes of ingenuous shame, Or heap the shrine of Luxury and Pride

With incense kindled at the Muse's flame. 

Far from the madding crowd's ignoble strife, Their sober wishes never learn'd to stray; 

Along the cool sequester'd vale of life

They kept the noiseless tenor of their way. 

Yet cv'n these bones from insult to protect Some frail memorial still erected nigh, With uncouth rhimes and shapeless sculpture deck'd, Implores the passing tribute of a sigh. 

Their name, their years, spelt by th' unletter'd muse, The place of fame and elegy supply: 

And many a holy text around she strews, That teach the rustic moralist to die.. 

For who to dumb Forgetfulness a prey, This pleasing anxious being e'er resigned,

Left the warm precincts of the chearful day, Nor cast one longing ling'ring look behind?

On some fond breat the parting soul rolies, Some pious drops the closing eye requires;

Ev'n from the tomb the voice of Nature cries, Evin in our Ashes live their wonted Fires. 

For thee, who mindful of th' unhonour'd Dead

Dost in these lines their artless tale relate; 

If chance, by lonlely contemplation led, Some kindred Spirit shall inquire thy fate, Haply some hoary-headed Swain may say, 'Oft have we seen him at the peep of dawn 'Burshing with hasty steps the dews away

THE ELEGY 

II. 

I've been reading and rereading Gray's "Elegy" for so many years that I have unconsciously learned it nearly by heart, and could, I suppose, if put to it, recall nearly every verse of it. 

Yet I never cease to be surprised to observe how many remarkably familiar lines there are in it, - lines so familiar that it seems 

I've always known them. Isn't that your experience too? Hadn't you picked up many phrases, even whole lines, and perhaps a stanza or so, before you ever read the "Elegy" in its entirety? "Parting day scape ... • • weary way to darkness and to me solemn stillness incense-breathing morn glimmering landnarrow cell rugged elms neglected spot ... celestial fire": aren't you really at a loss to remember the time when those phrases weren't familiar to you? And even longer ones: "The short and simple annals of the poor. "The boast of heraldry, the pomp of pow'r. "Full many a gem of purest ray serene, "And waste its sweetness on the desert air."

And what do you suggest for the most famous single line? hear your answers, you know; yet I'm pretty confident of your choice: it's this, isn't it? "The paths of glory lead but to the grave. 

Just one more question like these and I'll go on to fresh woods and pastures new: Which is the one stanza that you catch yourself repeating, and painlessly memorizing, because well, perhaps because of its solemn nostalgic quality? What is that nearly self-memorizing quatrain? I'll venture it's this: "Far from the madding crowd's ignoble strife, Their sober wishes never learn'd to stray; 

Along the cool sequester'd vale of life

They kept the noiseless tenor of their way. 

Isn't that it?

You'll find such precious lines remarkably numerous in the elegies you're reading. I've recently reread all of those on the list I prepared for you, - all of those once or twice, and some others, too, - to refresh myself before writing these lectures. 

And it was almost with a start that I found one of my favorite phrases in "Lycidas". Of course I knew, when I used the phrase, that I was quoting, quoting something that can well stand quotation, repeated quotation; but I'd forgotten the exact source. 

I've already used the phrase today. Did you catch it? "to fresh woods and pastures new." It's from the last line of Lycidas (spelled capital L-y-c-i-d-a-s), Milton's great elegy from Edward King (spelled capital K-i-n-g). Sometimes, I'm afraid, I misquote

One of the greatest of Hebrew elegies is to be found in the the Second Book of Samuel, Chapter I, verses 19 to 27. 

This is the lament of David over Jonathan and Saul, as translated about the time of Shakespeare in the King James Version: 

The beauty of Israel is slain upon thy high places: 

How are the mighty fallen! 

Tell it not in Gath, Publish it not in the streets of Askelon; 

Lest the daughters of the Philistines rejoice, Lest the daughters of the uncircumcised triumph.

Ye mountains of Gilboa, let there be no dew, Neither let there be rain among you, Nor fields of offerings:

For there the shield of the mighty is vilely cast away, The shield of Saul,

As though he had not been anointed with oil. 

From the blood of the slain, From the fat of the mighty, The bow of Jonathan turned not back, And the sword of Saul returned not empty.

Saul and Jonathan were lovely in their lives, And in their death they were not divided.

They were swifter than eagles, They were stronger than lions, Ye daughters of Israel, Weep over Saul, Who clothed you in scarlet with other delights, Who put on ornaments of gold upon your apparel. 

How are the mighty fallen 

In the midst of battle! 

O Jonathan, thou wast slain in thine high places. 

I am distressed for thee, my brother Jonathan: 

Very pleasant hast thou been unto me: 

Thy love to me was wonderful,' 

Passing the love of women. 

How are the mighty fallen 

And the weapons of war perished! 

That's a mighty dirge indeed.

As a literary form, however, the elegy first took shape in Greece. It began there in the period following the period of the epic, and it continued to be cultivated there after all other forms of poetry had fallen into decay. And no wonder: for the elegy in Greek was not confined to songs of lamentation, but was used also for martial poetry -- war songs and marching songs, for gnomic and sententious verses, and even for erotic themes. (That word gnomic is spelled g-n-o-m-i-c.) There is, just one common denominator, so to speak, for all Greek elegies: they are (125)

Elegy-II 

invariably composed in the elegiac measure, in the elegiac distich. (Elegiac is spelled e-l-e-g-i-a-c, and may be pronounced either elegiac or elegíac. Distich is spelled d-i-s-t-i-c-h.) And the elegiac distich, if you insist, is a couplet composed of a dactyllic hexameter and a dactyllic pentameter: (Dactyllic is spelled d-a-c-t-y-1-1-i-c; hexameter is spelled h-e-x-a-m-e-t-e-r; and pentameter is spelled p-e-n-t-a-m-e-t-e-r. One of these days I will take time out to put you straight on the ABC's of English versification, in case any of you are not already essentially grounded in it.)

Now all of that about the Greek elegy may or may not be of interest to you. Certainly it is of no value to you so far as the 

English elegy is concerned. For, as you saw yesterday, the English elegy is confined almost exclusively to songs of lamentation. And today you may learn, if you've not already made the observation for yourselves in the course of your reading, that the English elegy may be in any measure the poet chooses. Thus Spenser, in "Dido 

Dead", employs an elaborate stanza; Gray employs a simple one; 

Pope writes in closed couplets; and so ? on.

There is more to be said, however, on this score of English elegiac measures. The chief point is this, that the measure should either be simple or else be so easily handled that the reader doesn't become aware of its complexity. In other words, an elegy is no proper form in which to flaunt one's technical dexterity: any such exhibition immediately calls into question the sincerity of the writer. - and an elegy not wholly and utterly sincere is well, I seem to have set up a straw man to knock down such an elegy is unthinkable. Matthew Arnold's "Thyrsis" (spelled capital T-h-y-r-s-i-s), that inexpressibly beautiful elegy to Arthur Hugh Clough (spelled capital C-1-o-u-g-h), is in highlywrought stanzas; yet as you read it you are aware only of its loveliness, and that is the proof both of Arnold's metrical mastery and also of his pervading sincerity. 

you know, you

Listen to two of those stanzas. They were written. in 1866, when Arnold was forty-four years old. "Not old, say? Well, maybe not "old", but at least well beyond the half-way mark of our allotted three-score-and-ten, and certainly not young! 

Doubtless the thoughts and feelings that accompany that age still lie outside the experience of most of you who hear me; yet listen, anyway. Arnold has just been enumerating some of the experiences that he and Clough used to enjoy together, and has ended with a cry, "They all are gone, and thou art gone as well!' ??

Then follow the two stanzas I've been leading up to. (The "night" is of course Death.) 

Yes, thou art gone! and round me too the night 

In ever-nearing circles weaves her shade. 

I see her veil draw soft across the day; 

I feel her slowly chilling breath invade

The cheek grown thin, the brown hair spread with 

I feel her finger light

Laid pause fully upon life's headlong train; gray; (126)

Elegy-II 

The foot less prompt to meet the morning dew, The heart less bounding at emotion new, And hope, once crush'd, less quick to spring again.

And long the way appears, which seem'd so short 

To the less practised eye of sanguine youth;

And high the mountain-tops, in cloudy air, The mountain-tops where is the throne of Truth, Tops in life's morning-sun so bright and brave! 

Unbreachable the fort 

Of the long-batter'd world uplifts its wall;

And strange and vain the earthly turmoil grows, And near and real the charm of thy repose, And night as welcomes as a friend would fall.

I challenge any one who has a heart in him, and a brain, and a love of beauty, to read those lines and not be moved, not feel another cubit added to both his spiritual and aesthetic stature. 

Usually, however, a simpler measure is a better medium for an elegy. Did you observe Gray's stanza? Four verses of iambic pentameter rhyming abab, or, as it is usually called now, the "elegiac quatrain" perhaps the most representative English stanzaform. How appropriate that is, in view of the fact that "the essential ground-note of English poetry", from its beginnings down to our own day, is the elegiac tone. 

As to the structure of the English elegy, no rules can be laid down. Quite often, however, an elegy will be seen to fall into three general parts, the first part given over to mourning, the second to questioning, and the third to reconciliation or solace. Largely what you'd expect, isn't it? For the elegy is, you will remember, a form of the lyric, and in all lyrics there is a similar general structure arising naturally out of the rise and fall of emotion.

You will find this general structure, for instance, in Shelley's Adonais (spelled capital A-d-o-n-a-i-s), that glorious lament for young John Keats (spelled capital K-e-a-t-s). Here's how it begins, with mourning: 

I weep for Adonais - he is dead! 

Oh, weep for Adonais! though our tears 

Thaw not the frost which binds so dear a head! 

And thou, sad Hour, selected from all years 

To mourn our loss, rouse thy obscure compeers, And teach them thine own sorrow! Say: With me

Died Adonais; till the Future dares 

Forget the Past, his fate and fame shall be 

An echo and a light unto eternity!!

Here are two other stanzas of lamentation, the eighth and ninth: (127)

Elegy-II 

He will awake no more, oh, never more!

Within the twilight chamber spreads apace 

The shadow of white Death, and at the door 

Invisible Corruption waits to trace

His extreme way to her dim dwelling-place; 

The eternal Hunger sits, but pity and awe 

Soothe her pale rage, nor dares she to deface

So fair a prey, till darkness and the law 

Of change shall o'er his sleep the mortal curtain draw. 

Oh, weep for Adonais! -The quick Dreams,

The passion-winged ministers of thought, Who were his flocks, whom near the living streams Of his young spirit he fed, and whom he taught 

The love which was its music, wander not,

Wander no more, from kindling brain to brain, But droop there, whence they sprung; and mourn their lot 

Round the cold heart, where, after their sweet pain,

They ne'er will gather strength, or find a home again. 

Here is a portion of the questioning, the twenty-first strophe: 

Alas! that all we loved of him should be, But for our grief, as if it had not been, And grief itself be mortal! Woe is me! 

Whence are we, and why are we? Or what scene

Meet massed in death, who lends what life must borrowi

Peace, peace! he is not dead, he doth not sleep 

He hath awakened from the dream of life 

Tis we, who, lost in stormy visions, keep 

With phantoms an unprofitable strife, And in mad trance strike with our spirit's knife

Invulnerable nothings. We decay 

Like corpses in a charnel; fear and grief 

Convulse us and consume us day by day, And cold hopes swarm like worms within our living clay.

XL

From the contagion of the world's slow stain

Shall be its earthly doom, the dead live there 

And move like winds of light on dark and stormy air.

I.

There is no inevitable connection between a knowledge of versification on the one hand and the enjoyment of poetry on the other. That is, I will grant you that one can heartily enjoy poetry without possessing much technical information about versification, just as one one can enjoy double-stopped harmonics in a violin solo and not be able to tell just how they are produced. 

I will grant you also that one can know a great deal about versification and yet have no real appreciation of poetry; during some twenty years as a student I had the misfortune to suffer at the hands of a number of teachers who thus saw and talked about many trees but who never glimpsed or pointed out the forests. 1 Nevertheless, I know positively, from extended observation, that a knowledge of at least the essentials of versification is valuable in studying poetry, and I sincerely believe, from long personal experience, that such knowledge is conducive to increased enjoyment of poetry. It is of value in the study of poetry if only as a time-saver: if instructor and student share a body of knowledge, they can confidently employ in their discussions the brief technical terms which sum it up. And it is conducive to increased enjoyment of poetry because it furnishes the means whereby one can detect subtle dexterities and beauties that otherwise would remain specifically unperceived. ,

So let's take time out, today, for that succinct account of 

English versification which I've been promising you, or threatening you with, you decide which word is more appropriate for lo, these many moons. A more highfalutin' word for versification, by the way, is prosody (spelled p-r-o-s-o-d-y), which the dictionary defines as that part of grammar treating of the quantity of syllables, of accent, and of the laws of versification or metrical composition".

Let's first establish our understanding of verse, speaking generally, and of a verse, speaking specifically. 

I believe I have already pointed out to you that the opposite of prose is not poetry but verse. Poetry, as we have seen, has often been defined tentatively. Thus Poe, you remember, defined it as "the rhythmical creation of beauty"; Wordsworth, on one occasion, defined it as "emotion recollected in tranquillity", and, on another occasion, as "the spontaneous overflow of powerful feelings" These definitions, and many others that we might rehearse, are valuable indeed: each one of them is true as far as it goes; each one of them makes a point that ought to be made, and memorably made, about poetry. But no one of these definitions of poetry encompasses the whole truth about it: no one of them makes all of the points that should be made about poetry. They are, then, stimulating and suggestive, but not final. Apparently it isn't possible to define poetry with any degree of finality. 

verse can be satisfac- Happily it is otherwise with verse: torily defined. Verse, speaking generally, is simply measured language, - measured Tanguage. A verse, however, a verse, speaking specifically, is "a line consisting of a Certain number and disposition of metrical feet". (134)

Eng. Versification-I 

Doubtless you have heard some people use the word verse as if it were synonymous with stanza. Don't you imitate them, for it isn't. A stanza is a special arrangement of two or more verses, whereas a verse is a single metrical line. That distinction is, I grant you, not a tremendously important one in itself: it is, indeed a comparatively fine one, nevertheless, it may justly be said of all persons, may specially truly be said of students; "By the distinctions they make, and by the distinctions they fail to make, shall ye rightly know them". then, will not misuse verse and stanza as if they were interchangeable. If you mean, for example, to speak of the first quatrain of Gray's "Elegy", you will speak not of the "first verse" but of the "first stanza" So much for that. You,

Now let us see how the patterns of verse differ in different languages. 

In Hebrew verse the structural basis is repetition of the thought. Anyone can hear this repetition if he will read aloud the psalms of David, and he can see it typographically in modern editions of the Bible. Listen again to the beginning of 

David's lamentation over Jonathan and Saul, in the first chapter of the second book of Samuel:. "The beauty of Israel is slain upon thy high places: 

How are the mighty fallen! 

Tell it not in Gath, Publish it not in the streets of Askelon; 

Lest the daughters of the Philistines rejoice, Lest the daughters of the uncircumcised triumph.

Ye mountains of Gilboa, let there be no dew, Neither let there be rain among you, Nor fields of offerings: How the 

How are the mighty fallen. 

In the midst of battle!' 

Hear the repetition?

The verse patterns in Greek and Latin are based on time, on the long and short quantity of the syllables of the words, on the intervals naturally required to pronounce the different syllables. 

Recall, for example, the first verse of Virgil's Aneid (Virgil is spelled either capital V-i-r-g-i-1 or capital V-e-r-g-i-1; 

Aneid is spelled capital A-n-e-i-d), 

I hope you stuck with your

Latin long enough to read Virgil in the original: 

Arma Vi/rumque ca/no Tro/jae qui/primus ab/oris/ 11 

Do you wish to write out that line? I'll spell the words to you:

Arma, capital A-r-m-a; virumque, v-i-r-u-m-q-u-e; qui, q-u-i; primus, p-r-i-m-u-s; ab, a-b; oris, o-r-i-s. Translated word by word as it stands the line reads: Arma, "arms"; virumque, (the) hero and"; cano, "I sing"; Trojae, "of Troy"; qui, "who"; primus "first"; ab, "from"; oris, (the) coasts", Reordering the words, you get this: "I sing arms, and the hero who first from the coasts of Troy". (135)

Eng. Versification-I 

- The pattern of the verse is dactyllic hexameter, the regular measure for the classical epic. That is, there are six feet in the line six dactylls and spondees. But those feet are not measured by the number of the syllables in them. Neither are they measured by the stresses of the syllables. They are measured by the quantities of the syllables, that is, by the length of time required to pronounce the syllables. Take the fifth foot, "primus ab": The first syllable, pri-, is long by its nature; the second, -mus, is short by virtue of its position before a vowel; and the monosyllable ab is short; hence a perfect Latin dactyll a long syllable followed by two short ones. If, however, primus had been placed before a word beginning with a consonant, both of its syllables would have been long, because it takes longer distinctly to pronounce adjacent consonants in different words; and the word itself would scan as a spondee - two long syllables. The one essential point in all of this is, that the verse-patterns. of 

Greek and Latin are based on quantity, on time. www G

In French the verse-patterns are based on the number of syllables in a line. Look, for example, in the Oxford Book of 

French Verse, at a poem by Theophile Gautier (capital T-h-e-o-p-h- 

1-1-e, Theophile, with an acute accent over the first "e"; capital G-a-u-t-i-e-r, Gautier). The title of the poem is Pendant la Tempetre (capital P-e-n-d-a-n-t, Pendant; l-a, la; capital 

T-e-m-p-e-t-r-e, with a circumflex accent over the second "e"), a title which may be Englished as "During the Tempest". Here's the first line; take it down word for word as I dictate it: La, capital L-a; barque, b-a-r-q-u-e; est, e-s-t; petite, p-e-t-i-t-e; et, e-t; la, l-a; mer, m-e-r; immense, i-m-m-e-n-s-e, (This is one of these lessons that are unusually slow-paced and troublesome because we do not have the assistance of a large blackboard on which to write out strange names and words.) Let's check that line: La, capital L-a, capitalized because it's the first word of a verse; barque, b-a-r-q-u-e; est, e-s-t; petite, p-e-t-i-t-e; et, e-t, sounds like the other word, but is a different one; la, l-a; mer, m-e-r; immense, i-m-m-e-n-s-e. There you are: 

La barque est petite//et la mer immense. 

You can come pretty near a translation of that line whether you know any French or not: "The barque is small and the sea immense".

In the French the final -ue of barque is not pronounced and is therefore no syllable; the final -e's of petite and immense are likewise silent and not syllables. Ten syllables remain. Is the verse therefore iambic pentameter? Not at all. It can be read so, of course, if one strains at it hard enough; but no Frenchman, would read it so. A Frenchman would read it in two phrases, as it were, two phrases with a fairly strong accent at the end of each, and the two phrases separated by a pause or caesura. - in this case after the fourth syllable: 

"La barque est petite // et la mer immense". (136)

Eng. Versification-I 

And a Frenchman would describe this verse simply as decasyllabic, simply as ten-syllabled. (These points on French versification 

I've verified with an expert, my friend Professor Don Demorest of the Department of Romance Languages; so, you may depend upon them.) .

Our time is up; so, that's all for today. continue with versification.
