II. 

Yesterday, after an apologia for it (and an apologia, spelled a-p-o-1-0-g-i-a, is not an apology!), I began a brief account of 

English versification, of English prosody. First we recalled that poetry, often and brilliantly defined tentatively, is not finally definable. Next we agreed that verse is measured language, that a verse is a line of such language, and that verse and stanza are not interchangeable terms - a stanza being composed of two or more verses. Then we looked into the verse-patterns of different languages, observing: that the pattern of Hebrew verse is based upon repetition of the thought; that the patterns of 

Greek and Latin verse are based upon quantity, upon the time naturally required to pronounce the different syllables; and that the patterns of French verse are based upon the number of syllables in a line. And at that point, right in the midst of a line of thought, we found that the time for the class-period was up. Let us, then, resume the line of thought where we dropped it: I was just about to speak of the verse patterns in Old English, or, as it is more commonly called, Anglo-Saxon. 

there is only the one 

In Anglo-Saxon verse the pattern. is based on three elements, namely, stress, caesura, and alliteration. Do you recall that bit of Deor's Lament" that I read to you in Gummere's translation, a translation which, as I remarked at the time, holds closely to the original metre? Take it down as I read it again: "Wayland (Wayland is spelled capital W-a-yl-a-n-d.) learned bitterly (At this point draw two parallel upright lines, to indicate the caesura or pause.) banishments ways, (Make a comma and begin a new line.) earl right resolute; (Make a semicolon, and indicate the caesura as you did before. ills endured (Put an acute accent over the second, the last e in endured to indicate that that e, ordinarily silent, is here pronounced..) " And there we'll stop.

Wayland learned bitterly // banishments ways, Earl right resolute; // ills enduréd The caesura, you see, in every case divides the whole line into two half-lines. In each half-line, no matter how many the syllables or words in it, in each half-line there are two heavy accents, - no more and no less. The alliterated words always fall under the accent. Other words, not alliterated, also fall under the accent sometimes, but the alliterated words always do. (Alliteration, by the way, spelled a-1-1-1-t-e-r-a-t-i-o-n, is similarly of initial sounds, just a rhyme is identity of final sounds.) The clue to the alliterated sound is furnished by the first important word in the second half-line. Thus, after the caesura in the first line, comes banishment; therefore, b is the alliterated sound, found also in bitterly in the first half-line. 

The accents, then, fall, in the first half-line, on Wayland and bitterly, and, in the second half-line, on banishments and ways. Listen:

Wayland learned bitterly // banishments ways. (138)

r.i tek

Eng. Versification-II 

The second line you've taken down examples not alliteration of a consonant sound but a sort of alliteration of vowel sounds. Listen: earl right resolute; // ills enduréd;

And so, you see, a verse-pattern built on the bases of stress, caesura, and alliteration.

On Now what about the patterns of modern English verse? what are they built? Those two questions, which are really one, take us, strange as it may seem, into the very midst of a contest that still rages. Some say that the basis of English verse is stress, stress alone; some say, like George Saintsbury (Saintsbury is spelled capital S-a-i-n-t-s-b-u-r-y), that it is stress and quantity taken together.

We shall not now enter into that controversy, but shall content ourselves with this safe statement, that the smallest recurring group of stressed and unstressed syllables in English verse is called a foot. Please get that straight: a foot is the smallest recurring group of stressed and unstressed syllables in a verse. That, of course, is a general definition, one that needs the bolstering of specific definitions. 

Specifically, then, there are four chief kinds of feet in English verse, and two lesser kinds, six altogether. 

By far the most frequently used foot in English verse is the iamb (spelled i-a-m-b). Sometimes this foot is called an spelled i-a-m-b-u-s); more rarely it is called an iambic (spelled i-a-m-b-i-c). iamb, Iambus 

Write the English word emit (spelled e-m-i-t). Pronounce it: e-mit. Of two syllables, you see, with the accent on the second syllable. There you have an English iamb, an unaccented syllable followed by an accented one. 

as in Mark that I said "an English iamb" for in Greek and Latin verse an iamb is a short syllable followed by a long one the Latin word amans (spelled a-m-a-n-s) meaning "loving", in which the first "a" is short while the second "a" is long, darents. According to the rules of classical prosody, "emit" despite the accent, would be not an iamb but a trochee (spelled t-r-o-c-h-e-e), a long syllable followed by a short one. (I've checked these points with an expert, my friend Professor John B. Titchener of the Department of Classical Languages, and so am sure of them.) 

Write the English word awry (spelled a-w-r-y). Pronounce it: a-wry. Of two syllables, again, with the accent on the second syllable. An English iamb, then . And if the word were in

Greek or Latin it would be a good classical iamb, too, because the second syllable is truly long in quantity, really takes longer to pronounce than the short first syllable. (139)

Jami

Eng. Versification-II 

I said we wouldn't enter the controversy as to whether the basis of English verse is stress alone or stress and quantity, but you see we've not been able to keep out of it. It's deep water, I concede; but its's really fun to get out of the shallows, isn't it? And surely you've got this straight, - haven't you? that primarily the basis of English verse-patterns is accentual: secondarily that basis may in part be quantitative, but primarily it is accentual. 

Now, well above the a of awry, make a mark like the bottom half, so to speak, of a circle, or like a slice of canteloupe viewed from the side - a slice represented by a single line. 

That's the regular symbol for a short syllable in English verse. 

Well above the syllable -wry, make a mark like a hyphen or a dash; make it on a line with the other mark. That's the regular symbol for a long syllable in classical verse, for an accented or stressed syllable in English verse. And there you have the graphical representation of an iamb, whether classical or English.

I've already spoken of another kind of foot frequently employed in English verse, the trochee (spelled, to repeat, t-r-o-c-h-e-e). The word trochee is itself a trochee, according to the definition suggested a moment ago, - a stressed syllable followed by one not stressed. Diagram a trochee, a short dash followed by a crescent mom lying on its back. 

Just the reverse, you see, of an iamb.

Less frequent than iambs and trochees are anapests and dactyls. An anapest (spelled a-n-a-p-e-s-t) is composed of two unstressed syllables followed by a stressed syllable, as in the word engineer (spelled e-n-g-i-n-e-e-r). Make an anapest with the symbols I've suggested. A dactyl (spelled d-a-c-t-y-1) comprises an accented syllable followed by two unaccented ones, as in the word metrical (spelled m-e-t-r-i-c-a-1). Diagram a dactyl.

These four chief feet of English verse the iamb, the trochee, the anapest, and the dactyl you must get clearly in mind. That may appear to you to be an order easy to obey, and 

I truly hope that you will find little difficulty in carrying it out; but I can well recall how these terms used to become confused in my mind after I was sure they were pigeon-holed for good.

You will find two or three other kinds of feet in English verse, feet that practically never are used by themselves as the basis meter of verse, but that occur occasionally. One, the spondee (spelled s-p-o-n-d-e-e, composed of two accented syllables, as, say, in the phrase "bell-boy", b-e-1-1-hyphen-b-o-y, if you give the two words equal accent, "bell-boy". Another is the pyrrhic, p-y-r-r-h-i-c, composed of two unaccented syllables, as, perhaps, ordinarily, in the phrase "by the", b-y, "by". t-h-e, "the" "by the Another that is sometimes urged is the amphibrach (spelled a-m-p-h-i-b-r-a-c-h), composed of three syllables, the first one short, the second one long, and the 

(140)

sei

Eng. Versification II, third one short again, as, for example, in the word alarming (spelled a-l-a-r-m-i-n-g), or in the word prophetic (spelled p-r-o-p-h-e-t-i-c). 

Such are the names and structures of the feet to be found in English verse. And of course each noun has its corresponding adjective. Thus you have, from iamb, "ambic" (spelled i-a-mb-i-c); from trochee, "trochaic" (spelled t-r-o-c-h-a-i-c); from anapest, "anapestic" (spelled a-n-a-p-e-s-t-i-c); from dactyl, "dactyllic" dactyllic" (spelled d-a-c-t-y-1-1-1-c); and from spondee, spondaic" (spelled s-p-o-n-d-a-i-c). Pyrrhic is both noun and adjective, and I believe there is no adjectival form of amphibrach. 1 There are special terms, too, to indicate the number of feet in a verse. If there is only one foot to the verse you have monometer (spelled m-o-n-o-m-e-t-e-r). There are very few English poems in monometer, but I trust you will be amused by this one in trochaic monometer; it's entitled "Fleas" and is complete in one couplet, as follows: "Adam 

Had 'em!"

Two feet to the line give dimeter (spelled d-i-m-e-t-e-r), three give trimeter (t-r-i-m-e-t-e-r), four give tetrameter (t-e-t-r-a-m-e-t-e-r), five give pentameter (p-e-n-t-a-m-e-t-e-r), six give hexameter (h-e-x-a-m-e-t-e-r), seven give heptameter (h-e-p-t-a-m-e-t-e-r), and eight give octameter (o-c-t-a-m-e-t-e-r), The vast majority of English verses are written in tetrameter and pentameter. (Just a little more of this highly concentrated barrage of technical points and then we'll turn to playing a sort of game together.) In In

So far I've been speaking quite casually of stress or accent, as if the matter were simple indeed; but in truth it is not. 

English verse there are three accents that every word has or may have. First, of course, a word has its ordinary accent, the accent we regularly give it in speech, the accent that, in case we are uncertain about it, we can book up in a dictionary. good verse no word is ever given an accent at variance with its dictionary accent. Second, a word in verse falls under s ome metrical accent; the stress may be light or it may be heavy, but some stress there always is. Then, third, a word in verse, like a word in prose or in' speech, may be subjected to a rhetorical accent, may properly be emphasized in order clearly to bring out the meaning. The most important words in a verse should bear all three accents: the dictionary accent, the metrical accent, and the rhetorical accent. That, for the moment, enough about accent. Now let's begin that game. 

Write out the first line of Gray's "Elegy": is (141)

Eng. Versification-II ... 

"The curfew tolls the knell of parting day". 

What's the rhythm of it, the movement: dactyllic, anapestic, iambic, or trochaic? Further, what sort of a line is it: tetrameter, pentameter, hexameter, or what? In short, how do you scan it? (Scan is spelled s-c-a-n.) That is, how do you reveal and describe its metrical structure? 

Well, you probably don't need any help, but I'll go through this one with you anyway; you can do the next one by yourselves. 

First I say to myself, "Obviously unimportant words, such as articles and prepositions, are not likely to be accented"; so, I put a little slice of canteloupe over the two the's and over of.

Next I say, curfew and parting are both of them accented on the first syllable"; so, I put dash-like marks over cur- and part- and put more canteloupe over few and -ing. 

Then I say, tolls is the verb of the sentence, and knell is the object, and certainly these are accented"; so, I put those dash-like symbols over tolls and knell. Now nine of the ten syllables are disposed of, leaving only day; and when I perceive the groups of light and heavy stresses leading up to it, I'm no longer in question about day: I put a dash over it too. Finally, I mark off the recurring groups of accents: I draw a single upright line down between the r and f of curfew, between tolls and the between knell and of, and between the t an i in parting. There you are: each foot an iamb, therefore iambic rhythm; five feet, therefore a pentameter. Q.E.D. Isn't that a fascinating game?

Now you try this line and we'll talk about it tomorrow: "Sleep the sleep that knows not breaking". (142)

III.

At our last previous class meeting we continued to pry into English versification. In Anglo-Saxon we saw a verse-pattern based on accent, pause, and alliteration; this concluded a survey begun in an earlier class period. Then we observed the versepatterns of Modern English to be based primarily on accent, or stress. A foot in that verse we defined as the smallest recurring group of stressed and unstressed syllables, and then we went on to consider the seven kinds of feet there are: the iamb (we paused to differentiate the English from the classical iamb), the trochee, the anapest, the dactyl, the spondee, the pyrrhic, and the amphibrach. After this we learned the names of verses according to the number of feet in them: monometer, dimeter, trimeter, tetrameter, pentameter, hexameter, heptameter, and octameter. 

Finally, before playing a kind of game, we pondered the three sorts of accents that words bear English verse: the dictionary accent, the metrical accent, and the rhetorical accent. The game" consisted in applying some of this technical knowledge in the scansion of the first line of Gray's "Elegy", "The curfew tolls the knell of parting day", a line that we concluded was a perfect iambic pentameter. For this class period you were to scan the line, "Sleep the sleep that knows not breaking". 

What did you conclude it to be? I hope you decided that the prevailing rhythm is trochaic, and that there are four feet, that, in short, the verse is a trochaic tetrameter. 

That, however, is only part of the correct answer. The remainder of that answer affords three more questions. First: 

Are all four feet trochees? The answer is, No. Second: Which foot is not a trochee? The answer is, The third. Finally: 

Then what is the third foot? And the answer is, A spondee.

I suppose almost all of you are right so far as the trochaic tetrameter is concerned, but I daresay that many of you did not detect the spondee. But don't be discouraged, for believe me when I tell you that many of you do your reading in one way and your scansion in another; that your reading, which is quite important, is right; and that only your scansion, which is much less important, is wrong. That discrepancy must not, of course, continue indefinitely; and, of course, you won't permit it to. 

Here's how you went wrong, if you are interested in hearing my analysis of your mistake: In your reading you did detect the spondee; you did emphasize that important little word "not"; you read the verse this way: "Sleep the sleep that knows not breaking". 

That is, in reading the line you remembered the very first rule 

I gave you in the lecture on How to Read Poetry, the rule that 

I then phrased as follows: "As you read, place most emphasis on the words that carry the meaning of the poet. Emphasize his meaning just as you would emphasize your own if you were speaking (143)

Eng. Versification-III 

for yourself." For certainly you, like all the rest of us, emphasize that negative adverb not when you speak for yourself. 

You don't say: "I will not do it!" You say: "I will not do it!" 

But in your scansion, in your marking the syllables as stressed or as unstressed, and in your drawing these single vertical lines. that divide one foot from another, you soon perceived the versepattern, rapidly ran through the line to see if the tum-ti of the trochee would fit in all cases, found that it would, and then carelessly let it go at that. 

I spoke a moment ago as if I deemed scansion to be unimportant. 

And, merely as scansion, I most certainly do deem it to be unimportant. But scansion can be, and ought to be, much more than merely scansion. It ought to be a clear, effective, well nigh unmistakable means of registering your reading of verse, of registering it for your own later consideration, or of registering it so that others, far removed from you in space, may consider it, possibly for examination purposes! Of course, before you can go very deeply into scansion, you'll have to have a few more symbols to use, and a clear understanding of what the symbols signify; and I'll try to see to it that you get both. But even with the symbols you have those for the feet, that for the divisions between feet (a single upright line) and that for the caesura (two parallel upright lines) you can do a great deal. 

Do you wish a scansion problem or two? Then take down these verses: "Nuns fret not at their convent's narrow room; 

And. hermits are contented with their cells;

And students with their pensive citadels; 

Maids at the wheel, the weaver at his loom". 

That quatrain is one of Wordsworth's. 

Here's one of Shakespeare's: "Shall I compare thee to a summer's day? 

Thou art more lovely and more temperate: 

Rough winds do shake the darling buds of May, And summer's lease hath all too short a date"

Those are good problems, believe me! Now do be sure, in solving them, to keep the poetry uppermost, not the pattern. The patterns are quite easily determined, but the substituted feet are fairly difficult to be sure of.

I think I shall make a confession to you, because I believe it will do you good. In explaining how I scanned that line of 

Gray's "Elegy" the other day I misrepresented the facts. I did not say to myself first this, then that, then the other thing. 

All of that was specious rationalizing. You know what rationalizing is, don't you? It's explaining, afterwards, why you did something; or it's giving, afterwards, your reasons for saying something; when, in sober truth, at the time you said it you hadn't done any reasoning; or, at the time you did it you did it wholly impulsively. It's a very common fault, as you know as well. (144)

Eng. Versification-III. 

as I. And people who are intellectually honest aren't ordinarily guilty of it:

The truth, then, and now, is this, that I scan without looking at the verse and without looking at the sheet of paper in which I'm jotting down the conventional symbols. First I get the verse in mind, Then I close my eyes and say it over once or twice or three times. Gradually I become conscious of the syllables that I'm tapping, as it were - of the syllables that I'm naturally emphasizing. Then, still with my eyes closed, I repeat the line again, only this time I move my pencil across a clean sheet of scratch-paper, making the chicken-tracks of scansion as I go. Often I do this a second time, and sometimes some a third. Then, and not until then, I open my eyes, look at the scrawly symbols, determine the basic pattern and the variations, mark off the feet, and indicate the caesura. 

If you've been having difficulty in scanning you might try this semi-trancolike personal method of mine. But if you're now getting along satisfactorily by some other means, don't bother to try the experiment. It isn't the method, you see: it's the accomplishment that counts. And in any case I wish you success. (145)

IV. 

At our last class last week we went over the scansion of the verse "Sleep the sleep that knows not breaking" and observed that the third foot, which can be read as an iamb, is better read and marked as a spondee. For those of you who failed correctly to mark it, I ventured an explanation of your error, and again counselled you to be sure to scan verses not as they can be read but as you really do read them. Next, after an apologia for scansion, I set you two new problems, and then, in some detail, revealed to you my own method in handling such problems. 

What was your success in scanning the first quatrain of

Wordsworth's sonnet on the sonnet? Iambic pentameter, of course; but what about the variations from that pattern? 

I read and scan the first line like this: "Nuns fret/not at/their con/vent's nar/row room;/" that is, the first foot a spondee, the second a trochee, the third, fourth, and fifth, iambs. 

The second line: "And her/mits are/conten/ted with/their cells;/" - iambs in the first, third, and fifth feet; pyrrhics in the second and fourth. 

"And stu/dents with/their pen/sive cit/adels;/" iambs in the first, third, and fourth feet; pyrrhics in the second and fifth. "Maids at/the wheel,/the wea/ver at/his loom" -iambs in the second, third, and fifth feet, a trochee in the first, and a pyrrhic in the fourth. 

In the four lines, where you might expect twenty iambs, if you didn't know better, you have, instead, twelve iambs, five pyrrhics, two trochees, and a spondee, regularity in six out of ten feet, variety in four out of ten. Poets do, quite literally, "play variations on their patterns", don't they?

Remember the verses you and I used to write when we were youngsters?

We fixed upon a certain metrical pattern and then hewed to the line of it with a vengeance: not a single extra syllable, not a single substituted foot. We, you see, were the victims of our patterns, not their masters. And that was just one of the differences between us and real poets! (146)

Now to that quatrain from one of Shakespeare's sonnets: "Shall I compare thee to a summer's day? 

Thou art more lovely and more temperate: 

Rough winds do shake the darling buds of May 

And summer's lease hath all too short a date". two substituted feet. 

Second verse: trochee,

I read it like this. First verse: spondee, iamb, trochee, iamb, iamb, spondee, pyrrhic, spondee, pyrrhic, 

not a normal foot in the line! Third verse: the first foot a spondee, all the rest iambs. Fourth verse: all iambs save for the spondee in the fourth foot.

In these four lines nine substituted feet, 

five spondees, two pyrrhics, two trochees: almost as many substituted feet as normal feet. That is indeed rich variety, isn't it? 11 

You've observed, I'm sure, that in going over these scansion problems with you I've left a wide margin of safety both for you and for me. In all cases I've said, "I read it like this. In no case have I said, "This is the only correct way to read it." 

Why have I followed this course? Because, in the first. place, any approach to tyranny of any kind is abhorrent to me. 

And in the second place, he who would legislate dogmatically on matters of taste is an unmitigated fool. There are many, many verses that can be well read in a number of different ways. Of course I will defend my judgment in any case where I have made it, and similarly I should encourage you to defend yours if we could go over troublesome lines in conference together. 11 Occasionally in verse you will find lightly stressed syllables omitted. The symbol for such an omitted syllable is the caret (spelled c-a-r-e-t), which looks much like a v" upside down. The omission of syllables at the beginning of a is called truncation (spelled t-r-u-n-c-a-t-i-o-n). omission at the end of a verse is termed catalexis (spelled c-a-t-a-1-e-x-i-s). verse Such 

And on the other hand you will sometimes find extra lightly stressed syllables added in verse. Such addition at the beginning of a verse constitutes anacrusis (spelled a-n-a-c-r-us-i-s). At the end of a verse such addition gives what is called a weak, or feminine, ending. Such extra syllables are indicated by the regular symbol of the bottom half of a circle, but they are placed outside of, or beyond, the upright lines with which one indicates the divisions between feet.

The terms, as such, are unimportant, but I do wish you to be able to handle situations in which the principles are involved and hence in which the symbols are needed; so, here are some problems and examples. But first here's a poem, one that all of you know almost by heart:

Break, break, break, On thy cold gray stones, 0 Sea!

The thoughts that arise in me.

Will never come back to me. No matter how great your present enjoyment of that elegy's solemn beauty, it will be deepened, I sincerely believe, by a study of its unusual and successful metrical features. Four stanzas, the last two somewhat different from the first two. 

The first two in anapestic trimeter, rhyming xaya a formula in which "x" and "y" indicate unrhymed lines. The last two also in anapestic trimeter, save for the third lines in anapestic tetrameter, a happy variation.

When I embark; 

For tho' from out our bourn of Time and Place The flood may bear me far, I hope to see my Pilot face to face 

When I have crost the bar.

You are not, with Tennyson, in the company of a Homer or a Shakespeare or a Milton or a supremely great poet, but you are in the presence of an accomplished master. (150)

Measured language.

A combination of two or more verses.

No.

Identity of final sounds.

Saintsbury, George Saintsbury.

Yes.

Iambus and iambic.

THE ENGLISH AND SCOTTISH POPULAR BALLADS 

I have undertaken to speak today of the old English and Scottish popular ballads. Today, therefore, is one of those unusual days when I wish that I were somebody else; for, to be quite frank with you, I don't care much for the popular ballads, and it would obviously be far better for you to hear of them from some one of their many enthusiastic admirers. as There are people, you know, who wax as enthusiastic about ballads as they do abrut well, let's say fresh huckleberry pie.

Some of these people go so far as to trapse off into the mountains of western Virginia, of Tennessee, and of Kentucky, in order they put it, to "collect" ballads directly from the lips of the weatherbeaten old mountaineers who live there in a seclusion almost unbelievable, - ballads that those mountaineers have sung and passed on by word of mouth for many, many generations, ballads that often closely parallel the ballads known heretofore only in remote places in northern England or southern Scotland, ballads that sometimes are unique.

Others of these ballad enthusiasts go to great pains to insinuate themselves into the society of cowboys, then to beguile. those unsuspecting gentlemen into singing their special ballads, and then finally to record those ballads, both the tunes and the words. With these latter enthusiasts I have, I confess, some sympathy. I have long admired, for instance, the catchy words of "Ragtime Cowboy Joe", and one of my unfulfilled ambitions is to be able to sing it and accorpany it with the right chords on the ukelele. Know where I first heard it? In a dormitory for graduate students in Harvard University! Know whom I first heard sing it?

My good friend Rudolf P. Berle, then an assiduous student in the 

Law School, now a highly successful New York attorney and one of the less-publicized members of the New Deal's "Brain Trust" That's as close as I've been to cowboys and to ballads in the making. 

Yet those enthusiasts who go collecting in the mountains are the real, simon-pure ballad lovers. They are not misled by the lure of what is somewhat novel and largely picturesque. No dilute, modern ballads for them. Give them the real thing a poem "meant for singing, quite impersonal in manner, narative in material, probably connected in its origins with the communal dance, but submitted to a process of oral tradition among people who are free from literary influences and fairly homogeneous in character". 

Get that? You should, for that's the definition of an expert in balladry, the definition made by Francis B. Gummere (spelled capital G-u-m-m-e-r-e) in his book entitled The Populer Ballad and published by the Houghton Mifflin Company in 1907. I'll repeat it: 'The popular ballad is a poem meant for singing, quite impersonal in manner (m-a-n-n-e-r), narrative in material, probably connected in its origins with the communal dance (c-o-m-m-u-n-a-1, communal), but submitted to a process of oral tradition (o-r-a-1, oral) among people who are free from literary influences and fairly homogeneous in character (h-o-m-o-g-e-n-e-o-u-s, homogeneous) List the key-words and phrases in that definition: singing "impersonal", "narrative", "dance", "oral tradition" 'free from 11 (156)

Eng. & Scot. Popular Ballads literary influences", "homogeneous in character". 

I shall not lead you into the mazes of the question and the controversy as to whether the popular ballads were composed communally or by individuals. The older view of scholars you will find in Gummere's book that I've just spoken of, in Gummere's chapter on the ballads in the second volume of the Cambridge History of English Literature, and in the Introduction of my teacher, Professor George Lyman Kittredge (spelled capital L-ym-a-n, capital K-i-t-t-r-e-d-g-e), to the Cambridge edition of English and Scottish Popular Ballads published by the Houghton Mifflin Company in 1904, the best inclusive collection short of the monumental collection of Francis J. Child (spelled capital 

C-h-i-1-d), published by the same company in five large volumes from 1882 to 1898. The newer view you will find in the fourteenth edition of the Encyclopaedia Britannica in an article by G. 

Gregory Smith (spelled capital G-r-e-g-o-r-y, capital S-m-i-t-h) and Louise Pound (spelled capital P-o-u-n-d). 

11 The oldest ballad that has been preserved is. "Judas' extant in a manuscript from the thirteenth century, that is, from the twelve hundreds. The newest popular ballad that I know of at least it's a popular ballad of a sort is one composed 

communally a few years ago in Florida by a band of negroes who were working together mopping up, as it were, after a hurricane. 

Most of the ballads, however, date from the sixteenth, seventeenth, and eighteenth centuries. Of these older and indisputable popular ballads there are just three hundred and five, though for many of these three hundred and five there are numerous versions. as it

There are several reasons why this ballad poetry is important to students of literature. In the first place, as I've suggested, the popular ballads are the focal point of the controversy as to whether any poetry of any sort was ever composed by a group of persons; for, if any poetry ever was so composed, the ballads are that poetry. In the second place, since their "discovery" were, in the eighteenth century, the ballads have exercised a considerable influence on subsequent poetry, on the artistic ballad, especially, but on narrative and lyric poetry generally, end most notably during what we denominate as the Romantic Movement. And in the third place, a considerable number of the ballads are of lasting worth in their own right. 

Their very survival, by word of mouth alone, for generation after generation after generation, is eloquent testimony as to their intrinsic worth. Ninety, to be exact, ninety of the old English and Scottish popular ballads have been rediscovered, still alive and sung among and by the people, in remote mountain districts of our United States. Sir Philip Sidney Renaissance gentleman par excellence, felt this charm and wrote of it briefly in his Defence of Poetry in the late fifteen-hundreds. Joseph Addison felt it too, and wrote of it in some numbers of the Spectator early in the seventeon-hundreds. 

If you go in for exact dates, one you should have down pat is 1765, for it was in that year that Bishop Percy published, from a manuscript of about 1650, his famous ballad collection called Reliques (157)

Eng. & Scot. Popular Ballads of Ancient English Poetry (Reliques here is spelled r-e-l-i-q-u-e-s pieces that Dr. Johnson scoffed at and ridiculed in a parody, but pieces nevertheless of really signal importance in freshening and in brightening again the sluggish stream of eighteenth century English poetry. Then, in 1802 and 1803, Sir Walter Scott published his collection of the Minstrelsy of the Scottish Border (that's the title of the collection: Minstrelsy of the Scottish Border) and gave added impetus to the forces leading to the modern artistic ballad as we see it in Coleridge's "Rime of the Ancient Mariner" 

Keats's "La Belle Dame sans "erci" Rosetti's "he Blessed Damozel",

Yeats's "The Ballad of Moll Magee", and many others. What is it in the popular ballad that Sidney and Addison and Scott and so many others have admired? What, in short, are the special qualities of the ballad?

Outstanding, I think, is the simplicity of the ballad both in materials and in technique. The narrative materials are of great variety, but they are invariably simple. There are narratives of love, of jealousy, of fairies, of demons, of Christ, of the saints, of Robin Hood and his outlaws, of skirmishes and battles on the Border, but all of them simple narratives. The simplicity of the materials is matched by the simplicity of their presentation. That presentation is rarely artful, though unquestionably it is artful in "Edward" and in "Sir Patrick Spens"; typically the presentation is forthright, direct, in the common, quickly-recognized coin of convention, and ornamented only with conventional refrains and repetitions and such. 

For, whether, they were composed by individuals or by groups, the ballads were the poetry of the people, and the people hoth lowered and exalted them to their level. They couldn't read; they couldn't write; they weren't given to meditating upon the past, or to speculation as to the future; they lived realistically and animalistically and altogether naturally in the present; and they entertained themselves like children in the deeds and adventures and experiences of others. Sounds pastoral and somewhat utopian, doesn't it? And so it is, as we see it. To them, however, it was, as I have said, altogether natural.

That, for the present at least, is all you need to know about 

Now let's read some ballads. ballads. 

Here's one that you already know, "Sir Patrick Spens":

The king sits in Dumferling toune, Drinking the blude-reid wine: 

O whar will I get guid sailor, To sail this schip of mine?'

Up and spak an eldern knicht, Sat at the kings richt kne: 'Sir Patrick Spence is the best sailor, That sails upon the se." (158)

Eng. & Scot. Popular Ballads 

The king has written a braid letter, And signd it wi his hand, And sent it to Sir Patrick Spence, Was walking on the sand.

The first line that Sir Patrick red, A loud lauch lauched he; 

The next line that Sir Patrick red, The teir blinded his ee. 'O wha is this has don this deid, This ill deid don to me, To send me out this time o' the yeir, To sail upon the se! 

Mak hast, mak haste, my mirry men all, Our guid schip sails the morne': 'O say na sae, my master dir, For I feir a deadlie storme. 'Late late yestreen I saw the new moone, Wi the aul moone in hir arme, And I feir, I feir, my deir master, That we will cum to harme.1

O our Scots nobles were richt laith 

To weet their cork-heild schoone;

Bot lan owere a' the play were playd, Thair hats they swam aboone.

O lang, lang may their ladies sit, Wi thair fans into their hand; 

Or eir they se Sir Patrick Spence 

Cum sailing to the land.

O lang, lang may the ladies stend, Wi thair gold kems in their hair, Waiting for thair ain deir lords, For they'll se thame na mair.

Haf owre, haf owre to Aberdour, It's fiftie fadom deip, And thair lies guid Sir Patrick Spence,

Wi the Scots lords at his feit. 

There's the regular ballad measure, -. quatrains of alternating iambic tetrameter and iambic trimeter, rhyming xaya. 

And observe how naively, too, the metrical accent often dominates the rhetorical accent, for the ballads were sung, you remember, and consequently the beat of the time was regular, no matter what such regularity might do to the poetry that is, to what we think of as the poetry, but what to the original singers was not so much poetry as simply "the words".

Here's another famous old ballad, "The Wife of Usher's Well" telling of a mother who entertained the ghosts of her three dead sons. (We know they are dead when we hear that "their hats were o the birk", for in the ballads the birch ("birk" in Scots dialect) is a tree that grows in the land of the dead.) 

There lived a wife at Usher's Well, And a wealthy wife was she;

She had three stout and stalwart sons, And sent them o'er the sea.

They hadna been a week from her 

A week but barely ane,

When word came to the carline wife 

That her three sons were gane.

They hadna been a week from her, A week but barely three, When word came to the carlin wife 

That her sons she'd never see. 

I wish the wind may never cease, Nor fashes in the flood, Till my three sons come hame to me 

In earthly flesh and blood.' 

It fell about the Martinmass "

When nichts are lang and mirk, The carlin wife's three sons came hame, And their hats were o the birk. 

It neither grew in syke nor ditch, Nor yet in ony sheugh; 

But at the gates o Paradise ? 

That birk grew fair eneugh. 'Blow up the fire, my maidens! 

Bring water from the well! 

For a my house shall feast this night, Since my three sons are well." 

And she has made to them a bed,

She's made it large and wide, And she's talen her mantle her about,

Sat down at the bed-side. 

Up then crew the red, red cock, And up and crew the gray; 

The eldest to the youngest said, Tis time we were away.' (160)

QUESTIONS ON THE POPULAR BALLAD· 

1. Are popular ballads still collected? - Yes! 

2. Where in America are versions of old English and Scottish popular ballads still being recorded? In the mountains of western Virginia, of Tennessee, and of Kentucky. 

3. What environmental factor makes for the survival of ballads among mountaineers? - The seclusion of the mountaineers. 

4. How are ballads passed from generation to generation among mountaineers and other natural ballad singers? mouth.

9. Then how are cowboy ballads ever recorded? - By special investigators who go for a while to live among the cowboys.

They show that the first and third lines are unrhymed.

THE EPIC 

I. 

"What though the field be lost? 

All is not lost! the unconquerable will, And study of revenge, immortal hate, And courage never to submit or yield: 

And what is else not to be overcome?"

Isn't that a magnificent expression of the spirit of heroic defiance? Whose defiance? Why, the defiance of one who, "above the rest In shape and gesture proudly eminent, Stood like a tower; his form had not yet lost 

All her original brightness, nor appeared

Less than Archangel ruined, and the excess 

Of glory obscured: as when the sun new-risen

Looks through the horizontal misty air 

Shorn of his beams, or from behind the moon, In dim eclipse, diastrous twilight sheds

On half the nations, and with fear of change 

Perplexes monarchs. Darkened so, yet shon 

Above them all the Archangel; but his face

Deep scars of thunder had intrenched, and care 

Sat on his faded cheek, but under brows 

Of dauntless courage, and considerate pride 

Waiting revenge. Cruel his eye, but cast 

Signs of remorse and passion, to behold

The fellows of his crime, the followers rather (Far other once beheld in bliss), condemned 

Forever now to have their lot in pain; 

Millions of spirits for his fault amerced 

Of Heaven, and from eternal splendours flung 

For his revolt; yet faithful how they stood, Their glory withered: as, where heaven's fire

Hath scathed the forest oaks or mountain pines, With singed top their stately growth, though bare, Stands on the blasted heath.

As surely you know, there's just one poet in English literature who writes like that John Milton; and there's just one poem in which he consistently writes like that Paradise Lost; and there's just one character of whom he writes in heroic vein quite like that - Satan. 

My impulse, now that we've come at last to the epic in English, is not only to begin with a passage from the greater of Milton's, and with a comment on it, but to continue with a host of other passages from the same source, and with endless observations on them; for it seems to me that, Shakespeare apart, Milton is to

English poetry, and particularly English poetry, what Wagner is to 

Continental opera its crowning glory. That course, however, would be selfish on my part and unjust to you, for it would ignore many fundamental considerations, would pass by a number of questions 

(173)

6.

You Ard 12 107 TU Is

JA

Epic-I 

whose answers provide the minimum background essential to reading an epic intelligently and with full pleasure. What, for instance, is an epic? What called epics into being, and where, and when?

Has the epic developed since its origin, and how? What are the great epics, and who wrote them? And particularly, what are the chief English epics, and what circumstances surrounded their composition? - Those are just a few of the questions that even a casual student of the epic should be able to answer readily. you see, there is much ground for us to cover. But, if you will follow me, I promise you, at the end of the rainbow, an unusually large crock of gold. So 

You've all heard, I know, of the Iliad (spelled capital

I-l-i-a-d; one "1", mind you) and the Odyssey (spelled capital 

O-d-y-s-s-e-y) of Homer (spelled capital H-o-m-e-r), the greatest of all Greek poets, who flourished, as nearly as we can determine now, about the middle of the ninth century before Christ, that is, about 850 B.C. Those two epics, the Iliad and the Odyssey, are "the earliest literary monuments, not of Greece alone, but of Europe." (I quote that repeated passage from W. C. Wright (spelled capital W-r-i-g-h-t), whose Short History of Greek Literature, first published by the American Book Company in 1907, is an excellent little reference book.) I'm sure you've read those poems; but, if they've grown dim in your memory, read them again. They're really classics, you know, and will bear rereading, in almost any translation you'll find.

Since the epic had its origin in Greece, let's begin with a definition that will set up clearly in our minds the Greek conception of it. An epic, to the Greeks, was a narrative poem treating a theme of action in heroic proportions and style, deriving its unity from a central hero whose exploits or fortunes constitute the thread of the discourse, which is intended for recitation. 

now we're far 

Let's go back over that definition bit by bit. A "narrative poem" that is, a poem that unfolds a story, as the ballad does, as the tale does too. Treating a "theme of action" - not concerned primarily, then, with emotion, as is the lyric, but, instead, with action. In "heroic proportions and style" away. from the tale, the ballad, and the lyric, bút quite near to tragedy, a dramatic form we've not yet studied together: "heroic" that is, far above the ordinary; "proportions and style" that is, in outline and structure, and also in the statement and expression. 

As for "unity", every literary type in its ideal exemplification is informed by unity; the particular point here is that the unity of the epic derives from "a central hero": a" hero, you will observe, not several; and a "central" hero, too, not an eccentric one, one off to one side. "Exploits or fortunes" those words are, of course, not synonymous: a successful individual combat, for example, is an exploit, whereas a shipwreck is simply a misfortune. "The thread of the discourse" "the thread", you will mark; "thread" in the singular, not in the plural. hose exploits or fortunes constitute the thread of the discourse" - not scattered, detached, unrelated exploits or fortunes, but exploits or fortunes in an organic series, like a number of separate pearls or beads, as 11 (1.74)

Epic-I 

it.were, unified into a single object, a necklace, by being strung together on a single stout silk thread. And, finally, "intended for recitation' not intended for singing, as a lyric is, and not. intended to be accompanied by imitative action on a stage, as a play is. 

And so, to put Humpty Dumpty together again, an epic, in the Greek view, was a narrative poem treating a theme of action in heroic proportions and style, deriving its unity from a central hero whose exploits or fortunes constitute the thread of the discourse, and intended for recitation. Is that clear? As you will recall from your.reading, that definition is analytical and descriptive of Homer's actual practice in his epics. 

Two centuries after Homer the Greek epic period came to a close. Four centuries after Homer came Europe's first normal treatise on poetry, the Poetics of Aristotle. Always, you see, critics come after poets and theory comes after practice. 

Aristotle (spelled capital A-r-i-s-t-o-t-1-e), whose exact dates, if you wish them, are from 384 to 322 B.C., gave first place in his Poetics (spelled capital P-o-e-t-i-c-s) to tragedy; so, when we come to study the drama, we'll consult him again. of the epic, to which he gave second place, he says the essentials are dignified theme, organic unity, and orderly progress of action. You aren't to take Aristotle as gospek, ever, and you are to be particularly on guard when he discourses on poetry, for he had little profound sympathy with, and understanding of, the aims and achievements of literary artists, and almost no prophetic vision. in matters aesthetic, but, on the other hand, it is always well to know what Aristotle says on a given point and to ponder it, for Aristotle is one of your hard-headed, practical-minded critics. (If you'd like to look into the Poetics, I suggest that you read Aristotle on the Art of Poetry, an amplified version with supplementary Illustrations for students of English, by Lane Cooper (spelled capital L-a-n-e, Lane; capital C-o-o-p-e-r, Cooper) of Cornell 

University, a version first published by Ginn and Company in 1913.) ..

Now we are brought face to face with a question that unfolds itself some thing like this: If the Iliad and the Odyssey are the earliest literary monuments of Europe, are we to infer that Greek poetry began with the epic? Did Homer, all by himself, singlehanded, with no background of other similar poetry, or indeed of any poetry whatsoever, simply or magically produce art of whole cloth, as it were, those great epics of his? Any one can answer that question summarily with an emphatic "No", but a summary answer leaves too many related questions unanswered. 

Approach it this way: First there is a primitive nation. 

That nation engages in a war with another nation. It emerges from the conflict victorious. Consequently its leaders and specially distinguished warriors become popular heroes. The common people talk about them. Alert minstrels versify their exploits and sing them. Another generation grows up. It accepts the stories of the national heroes and repeats them. Then another generation grows up. (175)

4444SUNN

Epic-I The heroes of old grow more and more legendary; the stories about them increase in number and detail. Other generations grow up: the tales of the old, old war and its heroes now amount to a saga (spelled s-a-g-a), or, to use the precise term, the tales now constitute an epos (spelled e-n-o-s), that is, "a series of poems dealing with a connected theme but not formally united". 

Now approach more closely: There was a primitive nation of 

Hellenes, or Greeks. Across the blue Aegean in Asia Minor there was another primitive nation, whose capital city was Troy (or, in Greek, Ilion, spelled capital I-l-i-o-n, and hence Iliad), a city that was located and excavated by the great archaeologist Schlieman (spelled capital S-c-h-l-i-e-m-a-n-n) about 1870, and dated by another great archaeologist, Dorpfeld (spelled capital D-o-(that is, umlaut "o")-r-r-f-e-l-d (final "d" pronounced "t" as "t".as always in German)), sometime between 1500 and 1000 B.C. From that conflict, one of a long series between the forces of the East with a capital "e" and of the West with a capital "w" a series later marked in Greek history by the crucial battle of Marathon, the Greeks emerged victorious. Subsequently the Trojan legend became in Greece "the most deeply and permanently dramatic theme" in all the store of its saga. 2 It was from this epos that Homer wrought the epopee of the Iliad. Epopee (spelled e-p-o-p-e-e) is a term opposed to epos. 

An epos, we saw, is "a series of poems dealing with a connected theme but not formally united". An epopee, on the other hand, is "the united and unifying epic narrative formed from these" tales not formally connected hitherto, A saga, an epos, you see, develops among the people and reflects certain historical or legendary events. Of the epos of the Iliad, no separate bit is preserved, but scholars are almost universally agreed that there was such an epos. Scholars, indeed, can find in the Iliad echoes of numerous other popular heroic sagas, but into such special problems we shall not pry. Suffice it for us that the Iliad was forged by a supremely masterful literary artist from a vast mass of popular lore and tradition. 

Observe the adjective: "popular" lore and tradition"

Because they were distilled from the lore and tradition of the people, generally, Homer's epics are properly to be designated folk-epics. But almost every great nation on earth has its folkepic, or at least its folk-epos, a saga developed largely among the people, but shaped finally by some single conscious literary artist, an artist either wholly unknown or as dimly and as uncertainly known as old blind omer. (176)

tras

THE EPIC 

II. 

Yesterday, after a deep and sustaining draught from Milton's

Paradise Lost, we addressed ourselves to some thoughtful study of the epic as a type in poetry. We refreshed our memories concerning a few facts about Homer, and learned that his Iliad and Odyssey are "the earliest literary monuments, not of Greece alone, but of Europe". We set up a definition of the epic, according to the 

Greek conecption: "a narrative poem treating a theme of action in heroic proportions and style, deriving its unity from a central hero whose exploits or fortunes constitute the thread of the discourse, which is intended for recitation." From this we turned to Aristotle's Poetics and observed his stress on dignified theme, organic unity, and orderly progress of action. Next we saw in general how, among primitive peoples, history eventually becomes legend and saga, and then, sharpening our focus, we traced the rise of the Trojan epos, wrought finally into epopee by the greatest of all Greek poets, wrought into the supreme folk-epic by Homer.

I remarked at the close of the class period that "almost every great nation on earth has its folk-epic, or at least its folk epos, a saga developed largely among the people, but shaped finally by some single conscious literary artist, an artist either wholly unknown or as dimly and uncertainly known as old blind Homer" and I was about to add that epics thus embody, each in its degree, the culture of a race or a civilization. 

Let me enumerate some of the chief folk-epics of Western Europe. France has its anonymous Chanson de Roland (spelled capital C-h-a-n-s-o-n, space, d-o, space, capital R-o-1-a-n-d), or Song of Roland, written about 1100 A. D., clebrating Roland,, nephew of 

Charlemagne, and his heroic rearguard in battle with the Saracens in the pass at Roncevalle, - an epic that epitomizes "those early struggles of the Franks that checked the onrush of the Moors, and finally saved Europe from Mohammedan domination. It's worth learning old French to read the Song of Roland with complete satisfaction in the original, but there's a good translation of it in English prose by Isabel Butler (spelled capital B-u-t-1-e-r), first published by the Houghton Mifflin Company in the Riverside 

Literature Series in 1904. 

Germany has its equally anonymous Niebelungenlied (spelled capital N-i-e-b-e-l-u-n-g-e-n-l-i-e-d) or Song of the Niebelungs written out in the twelfth century A. D., but going back historically to the total defeat of the Burgundians by the Huns in 437, - a noble old pagan epic celebrating chiefly Siegfried (spelled capital S-i-c-g-f-r-i-e-d), and arising out of the migration of the nations that wrecked the Roman Empire and laid the foundations of European civilzation. If, like Francis 

Thompson, you can't read the crabbed mediaeval German" read, if you will, the transalation into English prose made by Margaret Armour (spelled capital A-r-m-o-u-r) and available in the Everyman's 

Library edition since 1908. 

(177)

Epic-II- 

For the Scandinavian peoples, particularly the Icelanders, there are the Eddas (edda is spelled e-d-d-a), the Younger Edda and the Elder Edda, both written down in the thirteenth century, but preserving the mythology and legends and history of centuries. 

Avaliable in the Everyman's Libray edition are The Olaf Sagas 

(Olaf is spelled capital 0-1-a-f), chronicled by Snorre Sturlas on (spelled capital S-t-u-r-1-a-s-o-n) and translated into English by Samuel Laing (spelled capital L-a-i-n-g), and also The Saga of Grettir the Strong (Grettir is spelled capital G-r-e-t-t-i-r), translated by George A. Hight (spelled capital H-i-g-h-t); but these, if you'll take my word for them and I've recently gone over them again, these are pretty hard reading and not now worth your while. 

And then finally for our purposes among the folk-opics there is the hardy Anglo-Saxon or Old English Beowulf (spelled capital B-e-o-w-u-1-f), of which I shall have something to say another day. Let's 

Before we go ahead, however, let's go back for a while. return to Homer's Iliad, in order surely to determine some, points in the technique of the epic. And let's not get bogged down in mere abstractions; let's go directly to the poem itself. 

not the

Here's how it begins, according to the eighteenth-century translation of Alexander Pope (spelled capital P-o-p-e), best translation, by any means, but certainly the most famous and most easily obtainable: 

Achilles' wrath, to Greece the direful spring 

Of woes unmember'd, heavenly goddess, sing! 

That wrath which hurled to Pluto's glowing reign 

The souls of mighty chiefs untimely slain;

Whose limbs unburied on the naked shore, Devouring dogs and hungry vultures tore: 

Since great Achilles and Atrides strove,

Such was the sovereign doom, and such the will of Jove! 

The very first words announce the theme: "Achilles' wrath".

Similarly, the opening lines of the Odyssey present the theme of the wanderings of Odysseus, or, as we more commonly call him, Ulysses. And so usually in epics: the theme is announced briefly in the first few lines.

In the Iliad the theme is a martial one: above all else is celebrated the military prowess of the hero. In the romantic epics of a much later date an impassioned love element often figures prominently, and sometimes there is also a glorification of the true faith; but in the epic as Homer wrote it these elements are absent entirely. 

In the second line you hear the poet invoke the muse: "heavenly goddess, sing! There is a similar invocation at the beginning of the Odyssey. Such invocation later became a convention in epic composition, and not seldom was made by poets to whom the Muses were not beings who might hear and answer but were instead mere literary ornaments. (178)

Epic-II- 

As we begin the, seventeenth line - only the seventeenth line, mind you we are already in the midst of the action: 

Suppliant the venerable father stands; 

Apollo's awful ensigns grace his hands:

By these he begs; and lowly bending down, Extends, the scepter and the laurel crown. 

He sued to all, but chief implored for grace 

The brother-kings, of Atreus' royal race. His 

Whose father? the father of the captive maiden Chryseis. name? Chryses. Sued to all of whom? To all the Greek kings and warriors. Who are the brother-kings? Menelaus and Agememnon. 

Where is this scene enacted? In the Greek camp before besieged

Troy. Such questions and answers might be prolonged indefinitely, but never fear: they won't be! But they do serve, don't they, to bring out the fact that here at the outset we are not at the beginning of the action but in the midst of it, or, in the Latin phrase, in medias res (spelled i-n, in, m-e-d-i-a-s, medias, r-e-s, res). 

Likewise in medias res begin most other epics that have been shaped finally by literary artists. 

Let's hear what Chryses has to say: "Ye kings and warriors! may your vows be crowned 

And Troy's proud walls be level with the ground. 

May Jove restore you when your toils are o'er 

Safe to the pleasures of your native shore. 

But, oh! relieve a wretched parent's pain, Ang give Chryseis to these arms again; 

If mercy fail, yet let my presents move, And dread avenging Phoebus, son of Jove: There you are: quite a long formal speech; not a terse "Please give my daughter back to me but an elaborate appeal that seemed to Homer befitting for a priest of Apollo. And again, following

Homer, such formal speeches became conventional. 

Most of the Greeks are willing to release the capitve maiden, but not so Atrides: "Hence on thy life, and fly these hostile plains, Nor ask, presumptuous, what the king detains: 

Hence, with thy laurel crown, and golden rod; 

Nor trust too, for those ensigns of thy god. 

Mine is thy daughter, priest, and shall remain; 

And prayers, and tears, and bribes, shall plead in vain 

Till time shall rifle every youthful grace, And age dismiss her from my cold embrace.

In daily labors of the loom enploy'd 

Or doom'd to deck the bed she once enjoyed.

That gives us our first impression of Atrides. favorable one, to be sure, but it's a strong one. And while we may deem him proud, vain, cruel, and much else that is bad, we must yet concede that he's cast in a large mould, that he's in that sense heroic. So, indeed, are all the chief human characters of 

Homer's Iliad: of high birth and station, exampling ordinary human nature enlarged and intensified, beings of a stature fit for supernatural guidance, and sometimes of some degree of divine origin themselves; or, from another point of view, beings magnified to proportions suitable for actors in momentous actions on a vast international stage. 

The narrative goes on:

The trembling priest along the shore return'd, And in the anguish of a father mourn'd. 

Disconsolate, not daring to complain, Silent he wandered by the silent main; 

Till, safe at distance, to his god he prays, 

"If e'er with wreaths I hang thy sacred fane,

Or fed the flames with fat of oxen slain; 

God of the silver bow! thy shafts employ, Evenge thy servant, and the Greeks destroy. 

Thus Chryees pray'd: the favoring power attends, And from Olympus' lofty top descends. 

Bent was his bow, the Grecian hearts to wound; 

Fierce as he moved, his silver shafts resound. 

Breathing revenge, a sudden night he spread, And gloomy darkness roll'd about his head. 

The fleet in view, he twang'd his deadly bow, And hissing fly the feather'd fates below.

And therein we see another feature of the Homeric epic: the direct intervention in the action of the divinities, Most epics, as a matter of fact, feature some sort of supernatural intervention or interest or purpose, and all that we can reasonably demand is that the poet write sincerely according to the theology of his time, as Homer did.

Another feature of style that you will observe in the Iliad and the Odyssey, though less in Pope's translation than in closer ones, is the epic epithet, usually "a highly expressive compound term applied over and over again to the same person or thing", such as "Apollo, bearer-of-the-silver-bow" or "the loud-sounding sea".

A more remarkable feature of the style is the epic simile, that is, an elaborate comparison in which description becomes an end in itself. Here's one, a comparatively short one of four lines, from Book II of the Iliad: (180)

The monarch spoke: and straight a murmur rose. 

Loud as the surges when the tempest blows, That dash'd on broken rocks tumultuous roar,

And foam and thunder on the stony shore.

Not less their number than the embodied cranes, Or milk-white swans in Asius' water plains, That, o'er the windings of Cayster's springs, Stretch their long necks, and clap their rustling wings,

Now tower aloft, and course in airy rounds, Now light with noise; with noise the field res ounds. 

Thus numerous and confused, extending wide, The legions crowd Scamander's flowery side; 

With rushing troops the plains are carried o'er, And thundering footsteps shake the sounding shore.

I believe the epic simile will become a substantial delight to you, that it will be with great pleasure that you will discover for yourselves such similes as this one in Matthew Arnold's epic-like Sohrab and Rustum (capital S-o-h-r-a-b, Sohrab and capital R-u-s-t-u-m, Rustum), one of the high-spots in 

English poetry of the last century:

For we are all, like swimmers in a sea, Poised on the top of a huge wave of Fate, Which hangs uncertain to which side to fall.

THE EPIC III. 

At our last meeting, the second devoted to the epic, I began, after the customary brief review, with the statement that epics, especially folk-epics, "embody, each in its degree, the culture of a race or civilization". I then proceeded to speak of some of the chief folk-epics of Western Europe: the French Song of 

Roland, epitomizing the struggle of the Franks and the Moors; the 

German Song of the Niebelungs, arising out of the migrations of the Northern nations that wrecked the Roman Empire; the Scandinavian Eddas; and the Anglo-Saxon Beowulf. Then, returning to 

Homer's Iliad, in Pope's transaltion, I pointed out to you a number of the conventions of the Greek epic: the announcement of the theme at the outset; the emphasis on martial exploits; the invocation of the muse; the beginning of the narrative in medias res; the formal speeches, the heroic cast of the chief characters; the intervention of the gods; the epic epithet; and, finally, the epic simile.

There were epic poets in Greece for two centuries after Homer, but their contributions are comparatively negligible and we shall therefore pass them by. Later, about six centuries and more after Homer, there was an outburst of epic poetry at Alexandria (spelled capital A-l-e-x-a-n-d-r-i-a). is First among the Alexandrians stands Appolonius (spelled capital A-p-p-o-1-o-n-i-u-s) who, though born in Alexandria, usually spoken of as Appolonius of Rhodes (spelled capital R-h-o-d-e-s), because it was at Rhodes, his adopted home, that he was first acclaimed for his Argonautica (spelled capital A-r-g-o-na-u-t-i-c-a), or Tales of the Argonauts, in four books. Dealing with the highly popular theme of Jason and the Golden Fleece, Appolonius wrote so effectively that he was influential on a number of the greater Roman poets, including Virgil, Ovid (spelled capital 0-v-i-d), and Catullus. Appolonius died about 190 B.C. Six hundred years after Appolonius, that is, in the fourth century of our era, came Quintus (spelled capital Q-u-i-n-t-u-s) of Smyrna (spelled capital S-m-y-r-n-a), with his Sequel to the Iliad, an abridgment, in fourteen books and some nine thousand verses, of all of the cyclic epics on the Trojan saga. 

Then, in the fifth century, there was Nonnus (spelled capital N-o-n-n-u-s), a Hellenized Egyptian, who wrote Dionysiaca (spelled capital D-i-o-n-y-s-i-a-c-a) or Story of Dionysus. This work, in forty-eight books and more verses than the Iliad and Odyssey put together, attempts to follow the phraseology of Homer as closely as did the Sequel of Quintus, but succeeds only in violating every known canon of epic proportion and composition.

These three, as I scarcely need point out, are not folk-epics but art-epics. An art-epic may be dfined, simply enough, as the creation of a known individual poet writing in accordance with ideals and principles of technique that are more or less definitely (182)

Epic-III

formulated. Shall I repeat that? An art-epic is "the creation of a known individual poet writing in accordance with ideals and principles of technique that are more or less definitely formulated". Homer, greatest of folk-epicists, is certainly not "a known individual poet", whereas Virgil, greatest of the artepicists of the ancient world, certainly is. 1 Virgil, or, to give him his full name, Publius Virgilius Maro, who lived the fity-one years between 70 and 19 B.C., towers as mightily over other Roman poets as Homer does over other Greek poets. Also, you will be interested to know, his is the greatest single classical influence on the literature of Western Europe in the Dark Ages, in the Middle Ages, in the Renaissance, and since. Of particular interest to us is the further fact that 

Virgil's is unquestionably the greatest of all classical influences on English literature. The first true Homerist in Europe was 

Boccaccio (spelled capital B-o-c-c-a-c-c-i-o), who first tasted the true Homer in direct translation in the thirteen-sixties. 

Since then, of course, Homer's influence on English literature, on all modern literatures, has been inestimably great. But the world's knowledge of Homr was, and hence Virgil's influence has been constant for nearly two thousand years now. One of my colleagues, for instance, Dr. Tom B. Haber (spelled capital 

H-a-b-e-r), has argued admirably, in his doctoral dissertation, that the unknown poet of the Beowulf knew Virgil's Aeneid. Chaucer coming several centuries later, knew Virgil intimately, as anyone can prove. Then, early in the English Renaissance, the Earl of 

Surrey translated the Aeneid into blank verse, that is, into unrhymed iambic pentameter, and, incidentally, Surrey's was the first use of blank verse in English. Milton, too, as we shall see, had Virgil as clearly in mind as he had the Bible. And finally, to come down nearly to our own day, Tennyson spoko of Virgil and his Latin hexameters in this glorious line, Wielder of the mightiest measure ever moulded by the lips of man.

The theme of the Aeneid is concerned with the wanderings and fortunes of Aeneas (spelled capital A-e-n-e-a-s, the first a-e being a digraph, spelled d-i-g-r-a-p-h, - a word you ought to look up in the dictionary if it isn't entirely familiar to you), with Aeneas, a refugee from Troy, and with the founding of Rome and the Latin race. This theme is worked out so admirably that the 

Aeneid became the model for the art-epic down to our own day. 

Virgil, however, did not live to complete the work to his own satisfaction; he wished to spend three more years on it, revising and polishing it; and when he saw that death was to interrupt his labors, he gave orders to his literary executors to destroy it; but they, happily decided otherwise and proceeded to publish it unfinished verses and all, just as Virgil left it. 

This epic, which, as I have already remarked, became the model for the art-epic down to our own day, itself closely followed a model, the model of the Homeric epic. The Iliad deals chiefly with martial exploits, the Odyssey with adventures; in the Aeneid, (183)

Epic-III

strife and wanderings are interwoven. In the Homeric epic the gods and goddesses play important parts in the action: so do they in the Virgilian; and herein, likewise, the human characters, while sufficiently heroic, are at the same time largely the puppets of the powers above. Further like Homer, Virgil begins with the statement of his theme and with an invocation to the muse, and then plunges into the midst of the action. The earlier action Aeneas relates to Dido (spelled capital D-i-d-o), to Dido, queen of Carthage, just as Ulysses relates his wanderings at the court of Antinous.

There are parallels, too, mutatis mutandis, in matters of substance: the shield of Achilles, made by Vulcan at the request of Thetis, described in the Iliad, Book XVIII, is matched in the Aeneid, Book VIII, by the shield of Aeneas, procured for him by Venus; the funeral games that Achilles orders for his friend 

Patrochus in Book XXIII of the Iliad, have their counterpart in the Aeneid, Book V, in the games commanded by Aeneas in honor of his father, Anchises. And these, as perhaps you have observed for yourselves, these are but a few of the parallels in matters of substance.

Virgil followed Homer in many points of style, too. Thus there are in the Aeneid long formal speeches; there are recurring epic epithets; and there are elaborate epic similes. Aeneas, for an instance of epic epithet, is always spoken of as "pious Aeneas" a phrase wherein pious is to be understood as meaning "loyal to one's family line", in this case the Trojan line. for an example of the epic simile, listen to this from the Fourth Book (Queen Dido of Carthage has sent her sister Anna to plead with Aeneas not to sail away): ?

As, when the winds their airy quarrel try, Justing from ev'ry quarter of the sky,

This way and that the mountain oak they bend, His boughs they shatter, and his branches rend; 

With leaves and falling mast they spread the ground; 

The hollow valleys echo to the sound:

Unmov'd, the royal plant their fury mocks, Or, shaken, clings more closely to the rocks; 

Far as he shoots his tow'ring strength on high, So deep in earth his fix'd foundations lie. 

No less a storm the Trojan hero bears; 

Thick messages and loud complaints he hears, And bandied words, still beating on his ears. 

Sighs, groans, and tears proclaim his inward pains; 

But the firm purpose of his heart remains. And

That's from the translation by John Dryden, an excellent translation made in the sixteen-nineties, and ever since then acknowledged by general consent as the standard translation. 

By all means, read Dryden's dedicatory essay. Dryden, you know, besides being a great original poet and a great translator, was also, and indeed remains, a great literary critic. I am one of those who maintain that literary criticism is as much a creative (184)

};

Epis-III

art as is the writing of poems or essays or novels, and I hope that you too, after you've filled your minds and hearts with the traditionally good things in English literature, I hope that you will then cultivate literary criticism too. One matter that Dryden discusses we too must take note of. 

The moral of the Iliad, says Dryden, is that the effects of discord. are bad and that the effects of unity are gond. The moral of the 

Aeneid, he continues, is different, as befitted a different time, and is that the people should respect their prince, Augustus Caesar, should be obedient to him, and in their obedience should be happy. Perhaps so. Yet, could we question him today, I am sure that Dryden would agree to the proposition that all works of art, with no exceptions, and hence including the epic, works of art should first of all afford pleasure to those for whom they are intended. No work of art, great in the highest degree, will ever be found at variance with a sound morality. 

That is to say, morality will take care of itself. The drive of the artist must ever be toward the citadel of Beauty. Let him capture that and he will find, as we also find, that he has captured Truth as. well.. 

that all (185)

THE EPIC IV. 

Yesterday we glanced at the epic poets of Alexandria: Appolonius of Rhodes, Quintus of Smyrna, and Nonnus the Egyptian. Their epics, we saw, were not folk epics, as were Homer's, but art epics and art epics we defined as the creations of known individual poets writing in accordance with ideals and principles of technique that are more less definitely formulated. Marching at the head of artepicists we beheld Virgil, greatest of all classical influences on European literature, and greatest of all classical influences on English literature. Enlarging on this last point we noted Virgil's influence on the Anglo-Saxon Beowulf, on mediaeval Geoffrey Chaucer, in the Renaissance through Surrey's translation into blank verse, on the two outstanding Johns of the seventeenth century - John Milton and John Dryden, and, simply to end the list, on Alfred, Lord Tennyson. Then we turned back to the Aeneid, stated its theme, and remarked that as Virgil worked it out it became the model for most subsequent art-epios, even though the poet did not live finally to polish it. Next, at some length, we noted how closely Virgil followed the model of Homer: in broad similarity of theme, in characters supernatural and human; in beginning the action in the middle; in such matters of substance as ornamented shields, funeral games, and so on; and even in style, in such matters as formal speeches, epic epithets, and epic similes. One such simile we read together, in Dryden's noble translation, and I heartily recommended to you

Dryden's dedicatory essay. We closed with some consideration, again. of that old, old problem, the problem as to whether the prime aim of art is moral instruction or pleasure, and I cast my vote, as usual, for pleasure. I've taken longer for this review of yesterday's work than is my wont, but I saw no way to be more concise, for we really did cover ground yesterday, didn't we? 

After Virgil, and before the shadows of the so-called Dark Ages settled over the ruins of Rome, bright white capital of the last great empire of the ancient world, there were composed many other epics. There was, to name one, the Pharsalia (spelled capital 

P-h-a-r-ś-a-l-i-a) of Lucan (spelled capital L-u-c-a-n), dealing with the Civil Wars and celebrating Cato (spelled capital C-a-t-o). 

There was also, to name another one, a later one, and a better one, the Thebaid (spelled capital T-h-e-b-a-i-d) of Statius (spelled capital S-t-a-t-i-u-s) a great favorite with English poets in the eighteenth century: both Pope and Gray, for example, admired it and translated portions of it into English verse. There were others too, that followed after Virgil, and not only in time, in method and style as well; but in method and style they followed at such a great distance that we may ignore even their names. 

for Then came the barbarians out of the North, the fall of Rome, and the long, long night of the Dark Ages and the Middle Ages. 

Through those ages it was the Church - your church and my church, then there was but the one Church, through those ages it was the church that preserved as much of civilization as was preserved. For the epic epitomizing that mediaeval Christianity, you must read the Commedia (Divina), the (Divine) Comedy, of Dante Alighieri, whose life spanned the sixty five years between 1265 and 1331; for it is 

Dante (spelled capital D-a-n-t-e), the Italian Dante, who stands at the end of the Middle Ages, devoutly presents its theology, and so (186)
