eloquently expounds his allegory of the pilgrimage of the human soul that critics, sometimes speak of him as "the first and in some respects greatest figure in the literature of modern Europe". Time forbids that I dwell on this epic, but someday you must read it, perhaps in the translation of our own Henry Wadsworth Longfellow. 

Do you recall that sonnet of Longfellow's that we read together, one that he wrote in the 'disconsolate time' of our American Civil War, on the subject of the Divine Comedy, while he was translating it? It's well worth our rereading:

Oft have I seen at some cathedral door A laborer, pausing in the dust and heat, Lay down his burden, and with reverent feet 

Enter, and cross himself, and on the floor 

Kneel to repeat his paternoster o'er;

Far off the noises of the world retreat; 

The loud vociferations of the street 

Become an undistinguishable roar.

So, as I enter here from day to day, And leave my burden at this minster gate, Kneeling in prayer, and not ashamed to pray,

The tumult of the time disconsolate 

To inarticulate murmurs dies away, While the eternal ages watch and wait.

A noble tribute, isn't it? And so we too, when we've a mind to, can experience Dante.

A hundred and one years after Dante died, Luigi Pulci (spelled capital P-u-1-c-i) was born. When Dante died, the long night of the 

Middle Ages in Italy was almost done; when Pulci was born, the sun was almost at the zenith in the bright day of the Italian Renaissance: science, philosophy, art, literature, and men too, were experiencing a rebirth. 

I named Pulci because his is the honor, through his Morgante. 

Maggiore (apelled capital M-o-r-g-a-n-t-e, capital M-a-g-g-i-o-r-e), or The Larger Morgante", Pulci's is the honor of being the first composer of the romance-epic poem as a separate kind of Italian versc Morgante was the giant whom Roland captured and tamed, or Orlando, as he is called in Italian (spelled Capital 0-r-1-a-n-d-o). Pulci, you see, worked over the materials of the French epos, or, as it is usually termed, "the matter of France". 

So also did Matteo Boiardo (spelled capital B-o-i-a-r-d-o), one year Pulci's junior, in his Orlando Innamorato (spelled capital I-nn-a-m-o-r-a-t-o), or Roland in Love. To Boiardo belongs the special honor of being the first to invent the machinery of the romance-epic. and, even more important, of being the first to invent the romantic heroine, in the person of Anglelica, daughter of Gallaphrone, King of Cathay, - a lady known to John Milton, as you will see by looking into his second epic, Paradise Regained, Book III, lines 338 and following. (187)

action

Epic IV Continuing and completing Boiardo's romance-epic (romance-epic is a compound word and a single term) there is the Orlando Furioso (spelled capital F-u-r-i-o-s-o), or Orlando, Mad, of Ludovico Ariosto (spelled capital -r-i-o-s-t-o), a captivating medley with no other design at all than that of affording pleasure to its readers, and this even though Spenser believed he found a moral allegory in it. 

shop Now let's pause for a moment consolidate our new positions (as a military commander might say), and make sure of our communications with the rear. Here in Pulci, in Boiardo, and in Ariosto, we've encountered something new. Are these works of theirs, of which I've spoken so summarily, really epics? And if they aren't epics, what are they? Well, strictly speaking, ey are not epics; they are romance-epics. And the most obvibus difference between the classical epic and the romance-epic lies in the presence of the love-motive in the romance-epic. What drove Orlando mad, for instance, in Ariosto's romance-epic, was love, love for the false 

Angelica. Did such love figure in Homer's epics? No: in the Iliad not at all, and in the Odyssey not notably. Did such love figure in 

Virgil's Aeneid? (Now we're coming to the heart of the matter.) 

Not such love, no; but love, yes! In the Aeneid there is a great deal, you will remember, about Dido's passion for Aeneas, there's much less about Aeneas's for Dido, and despite her entreaties, and despite those of her sister Anna, he sails away to the shores of Latium. Unquestionably, however, Virgil fathered the increase of the love element in the epic, but I doubt that he foresaw how far it would be carried by his followers in the Renaissance, and I am sure that his shade would vanish into a thin wisp of blue smoke if he could see the crimes that Hollywood movies commit in the name of love.

There's just one other epic of the Italian Renaissance that I wish to speak of briefly, and then we'll leave foreign shores and "come home" to England. This other Italian epic was written by 

Torquato Tasso (spelled capital T-a-s-s-o), who was born in 1544 and died in 1595. Tasso was a critic as well as a poet, and in his Discourses on Poetry (Discorsi dell' Arte Poetica) Tasso urged a reconciliation between the Virgilian epic, on the one hand, and the popular romance-epic, on the other. And so, as you'd expect, when he came to write his Gerusalemme Liberata (spelled capital G-e-r-u-s-a- 1-e-m-m-e, capital L-i-b-e-r-a-t-a), or Jerusalem Delivered, he did not follow the example of Ariosto and aim primarily at pleasure, but, instead, like Dante, emphasized moral instruction and celebrated the true faith. Writing of the great and colorful period of the 

Crusades in the Holy Land, Tasso's theme, says Hallam is "not of a single people, but of Europe", of all Europe. And a host of Europe's great literary men, including Spenser, Milton, Voltaire, and Goethe, to name but a few, have loudly sung Tasso's praises. 

Now, dutifully, I have sketched for you what I have taken to be the minimum background necessary for your pleasurable and intelligent reading of epics. I hope you have survived these comparatively heavy seas thus far, for I believe I discern smoother waters ahead English epics, English romance-epics, and also English epic episodes, a heartening prospect for all of us! (188)

THE EPIC 

V. 

At our last meeting we glanced at the chief epic poets of Rome after Virgil, Lucan and his Pharsalia, and Satius and his Thebaid. 

Then, coming apace down the stream of time, we saw Dante summing up the Middle Ages, particularly in their theology, in his Commedia, so reverently translated by our own Longfellow. Next we ran over the writers of romance-epios in the Italian Renaissance, Pulci, the first of them with his Morgante Maggiore; Boiardo, the inventor of the romance heroine in his Orlando Innamorato, known to Milton; madcap Ariosto and his Orlando Furioso; and, finally, devout Tasso and his Gerusalemme Liberata, almost universally known and applauded. 

In many ways, such as in the wildness of the adventures of the heroes and heroines, but particularly in the increased love element, we judged these romance-epics to be quite, quite different both from the finished folk epic as Homer wrote it and from the polished art. epic as composed by Virgil. to

Now we turn to England, to England before it was England, England when it was Angle-lond (spelled capital A-n-g-l-e-hyphen- 1-o-n-d) or "Land of the Angles", the Angles being one of the several Germanic tribes that came from the Continent to the island in 449 and thereafter, first as the allies of the hitherto native Celts and then shortly afterwards as their conquerors, conquerors who thereafter smugly enough, referred to the Celts as "foreigners"!..

Among the stories that the Anglés brought with them from the Continent, stories sung by their minstrels or scops (scop is spelled s-c-o-p), was the story of Beowulf (spelled capital B-e-o-w-u-1-f), the mighty Beowulf, who in his handclasp had the strength of thirty ordinary men.

The story, to touch only its very highest spots, runs something like this: An old Germanic king has built a great all-purpose hall for his retainers, but they are unable to use it for sleeping purposes because of a dire monster who comes nightly and carries them off to his den beneath the dark waters of a mere or great lake, Now comes Beowulf from across the sea; he has heard of this ravaging monster, Grendel (spelled capital G-r-e-n-d-e-1), and is determined to vanquish him or die. Vanquish him he does, for that night he feigns sleep until the monster enters the hall, then grapples with him stoutly, and at length tears his arm off at the shoulder. 

Grendel escapes and dives beneath the dark waters of the mere, but all know that he is done for and all believe that the terror is now over. But it is not: Grendel's mother now enters the scene and begins gruesomely to avenge her offspring. So Beowulf must dispose of her too. And of course he does, after a terrific battle with her in her underwater home. Then he sails home to his own people, richly laden with gifts. Fifty years later, and now king, Beowulf, though an old man, is still as brave as ever, and so fights, in the interests of his people, a monstrous fire-breathing dragon who guards a precious hoard of gold: he slays the dragon, but is himself mortally wounded and soon dies, loudly lamented by his retainers, who burn his body on a funeral pyre, and who also, over his ashes and the cursed gold, build a noble memorial mound. (189)

Epic V. 

Such is the story in bald outline, but did you ever hear of any bold outline that did justice to the original? Well, neither does mine. Read the poem for yourselves: there are only 3,182 verses to it. It's best editor is Klaeber (spelled capital K-1-ae-b-e-r); his edition of the text, superceding all others, was first published by Q. C. Heath and Company in 1922. In the Introduction, Klaeber lists the translations that have been made of this epic: nine in prose and seven in verse. The best of these, in my opinion, and I've read either all or some of most of them, is the translation of Francis B. Gummere (spelled, to repeat, capital 

G-u-m-m-e-r-e), made in the original metre, and published by the 

Macmillan Company, first in 1920.

Scholars believe that Germanic bards brought this story of Beowulf from the Continent to England late in the sixth century, and that it was written down, largely as we have it today, late in the seventh or early in the eighth century, though the unique manuscript in which it is preserved is unquestionably a tenth century transcription. "Germanic bards brought it": quite obviously, then, it is a folk epic, not an art epic. And of course you could see, even as I lamely retold it, the joints, as it were, of the several stories of which it is made up: first, the fight with Grendel; second, the fight with Grendel's dam; and, third, the fight with the dragon. And had I told more, you could have seen other stories, shining through, many of them. 

Yet the man who forged this poem, from a mass of mingled mythology, folk-lore, legend, history, custom, and observation, was a genuine poet, one who was animated by zeal for his art, who could tell a story well,, who had command of poetic structure and form, and who was rich in language and literary style. These qualities are 1 manifest even in the translations of Beowulf, and are the basis of my recommending it to you. You must understand that this folk epic of Beowulf has not "inspired or even affected" any subsequent English epics (as has, for instance, such an art epic as Virgil's Aeneid); it was almost wholly lost and unknown even to scholars until the early nineteenth century; but you will find, on the other hand, that is more than merely historically significant: it is good reading in itself.

Let's take Gummere's translation in hand, and read and talk about some portions of it. 

We'll find no epic epithets or epic similes in anything like the Homeric manner, but we'll find features quite distinctive. One such feature is the use of kennings. (Kenning is spelled k-e-n-ni-n-g.) A kenning, says Gummere, in plain, colloquial style, 'is where one speaks of the sea as "the whale's road" or "the gannet's bath;" as if the phrase were a "token" of the thing." Take these lines:

To him the stateliest warrior spake in answer; 

The warriors' leader his word-hoard unlocked. (190)

4444~ONN obus alet olde A. Bater 

AUT 

thagod A

Epic V 

In that couplet, again to quote the lucid Gummere, "spake in answer. is literal; its variant, "unlocked his word-hoard," is metaphorical; and "word-hoard" is kenning for "thoughts" or "intention". When the reader grows accustomed to this cross-pattern of repetition, and he has no quarrel with it in its somewhat different guise in the Psalms, he will appreciate its importance as a factor in the old poetry, and he will not be unduly baffled by its persistence..' 

For my part, I believe you will come to take great pleasure in kennings: bone-house instead of body, heaven's candle for sun, these and many others will surely delight you. 

Another stylistic feature of Beowulf is the extensive use of litotes (spelled l-i-t-o-t-e-s), the technical term for "under-. statement", such, for a contemporary instance, as saying of a lovely woman "She's not hard to look at" when you really mean "She's beautiful". Here, for an instance in Beowulf, is how the poet tells of the refurbishing of the king's hall, called The Hart (spelled capital H-a-r-t), or, in untranslated Anglo-Saxon, Heorot (spelled capital H-e-o-r-o-t), after the fight between the hero and Grendel: 

There was hurry and hest in Heorot now for hands to bedeck it, and dense was the throng of men and women the wine-hall to cleanse, the guest-room to garnish. Gold-gay shone the hangings that were wove on the wall, and wonders many to delight each mortal that looks upon them.

Though braced within by iron bands, that building bright was broken sorely; rent were its hinges; the roof alone held safe and sound, when, seared with crime, the fiendish foe his flight essayed, of life despairing.

Many at morning, as men have told me, warriors gathered the gift-hall round, folk-leaders faring from far and near, o'er wide-stretched ways, the wonder to view, trace of the traitor. Not troublous seemed the enemy's end to any man (191)

Not troublous seemed the enemy's end to any man And now finally, since our time is almost up, here's a famous bit of nature description in Beowulf. The poet is describing the : stern, nay, the utterly forbidding character of the home of the fiends Grendel and his dam: 

Untrod is their home; by wolf-cliffs haunt they and windy headlands, fenways fearful, where flows the stream from mountain gliding to gloom of the rocks, underground flood. Not far is it hence in measure of miles that the mere extends, and 6'er it the frost-bound forest hanging, sturdily rooted, shadows the wave. 

By night it is a wonder weird to see, fire on the waters. So wise lived none of the sons of men, to search those depths! 

Nay, though the heath-rover, harried by dogs, the horn-proud hart, this hold should seek, long distance driven, his dear life first on the brink he yields ere he brave the plunge to hide his head: 'tis no happy place! 

Indeed it's not! But that's powerful descriptive, isn't it?

THE EPIC 

VI 

Yesterday we spent the entire class-period talking about Beowulf, that rugged Anglo-Saxon epic that was shaped from Germanic (not German, mind you, but Germanic: capital G-e-r-m-a-n-i-c) that was shaped from Germanic mythology, folk-lore, legend, history, custom, and observation, shaped late in the sixth century, written down late in the seventh or early in the eighth. century, and transcribed as we have it today in the tenth century. I recounted to you the three chief episodes of the story, and you could see that each of them would make, indeed once was, a story be itself, and, as you know, such amalgamation of popular elements is the hall-mark of the folk epic. Nevertheless, I declared the Beowulf poet to be a thoroughly accomplished poet and his poem a really good piece of reading in and by itself, though of no influence in later English literature because it lay practically unknown in manuscript until as recently as the beginning of the nineteenth century. Then, prying into its literary style more closely, we talked of kennings, such as whale-road for sea, and such as bone-house for body. Next we talked of instances of litotes, that is, instances of understatement, such as the phrase no light thing that instead of the exact statement of the word impossible. At the close of the period we read the celebrated description of the Grendel-mere in winter, a place so fearful that stags preferred to be torn to pieces on its brink rather than to plunge into it with any hope of escaping the pursuing dogs by swimming across to safety.

And so we might spend many pleasant class-periods, reading and talking. 

We might talk of what has been called "The first Arthuriad" the Mort D'Arthur (spelled capital M-o-r-t, capital D, apostrophe, capital A-r-t-h-u-r), or, translated, The Death of Arthur, that legendary Welsh figure who drew all sorts of romances unto himself as a powerful magnet draws iron filings. William Caxton (spelled capital C-a-x-t-o-n), the first English printer, when he printed the Mort D'Arthur in 1485, spoke of it appreciatively and concisely in words that cannot be improved upon: it is, he said, a "noble and joyous book ... of the birth, life, and acts of said King Arthur, of his noble knights of the Round Table, his marvelous enquests and adventures, their achieving of the Sangreal (that is, of the Holy Grail), and in the end of the dolorous death and departing out of the world of them all". 

Or we might talk of "England's first attempt at any form of art-epic" as it is seen in Edmund Spenser's Faerie Queene (spelled capital F-a-e-r-i-e, capital Q-u-e-e-n-e; Spenser, as you will remember, is spelled capital S-p-e-n-s-e-r), an elaborate, allegorical, colorful romance-epic inspired largely by the earlier romance-epics of the Italian Renaissance, especially by Tasso's, but going far beyond even Tasso's didactic and moralistic purpose in aiming (to quote Spenser's own words) "to fashion a gentleman or noble person in virtuous and gentle discipline". (193)

EPIC VI A gentle Knight was pricking on the plaine, Ycladd in mightie armes and silver shielde,

Wherein old dints of deepe woundes did remaine, The cruell markes of many a bloody fielde; 

Yet armes till that time die he never wield. 

His angry steed did chide his foming bitt, As much disdayning to the curbe to yield: 

Full jolly knight he seemed, and fuire did sitt, As one for knightly giusts and fierce encounters fitt. 

That's how it begins, in Spenser's own peculiar stanzas (a rich contribution to English stanza forms), and so it goes musically on for a thousand pages.

We might talk about the epic that John Dryden planned to write about King Arthur, or the one that Alexander Pipe planned to write about Brutus (spelled capital B-r-u-t-u-s), the legendary Trojan refugee who came and gave his name to the island, Britain. 

We might talk about Hyperion. (spelled capital H-y-p-e-r-i-o-n), a glorious epic-fragments by John Keats (spelled capital K-e-a-t-s), and a poem that, when I reread it, invariably leaves me with a heurt of lead because Keats didn't finish it, and a poem that I recommend to you most emphatically.

We might well talk, too, about The Dynasts (spelled capital D-y-n-a-s-t-s) by Thomas Hardy (spelled capital H--r-d-y), finished as recently as 1908; a vast epic-drama, in nineteen acts and more than a hundred scenes built on the theme of the rise and overthrow of Napoleon considered as an embodiment of the great World Will. 

And, to bring this list of "mights" to a summary close, we might talk about an epic published just before the outbreak of the Great War: Drake (spelled capital D-r-a-k-e), by Alfred Noyes (spelled capital N-o-y-e-s), - an epic of some hundred and eighty pages which one evening last week I read with much of the eagerness and intensity of a youngster, and an epic that I unreservedly guarantee to be to the liking of any normal boy in the land: a swiftmoving narrative of that hardy sea-dog who won for England her place as mistress of the seas, and yet a narrative shot through with lovely rainbow colors and a mild romance.

Listen to this impassioned passage from the exordium: 

Mother and love, fair England, hear my prayer;

Help me that I may tell the enduring tale Of that great seaman, good at need, who first Sailed round this globe and made one little isle, One little isle against that huge Empire Of Spain whose might was paramount on earth, O'ertopping Babylon, Nineveh, Greece, and Rome, Carthage and all huge Empires of the past,

He made this little isle, against the world 

Queen of the earth and sea. Nor this alone 

The Theme; for, in a mightier strife engaged

Even than he knew, he fought for the new faiths, (194)

EPIC VI 

Championing our manhood as it rose. 

And cast its feudal chains before the seat 

Of kings; nay, in a mightier battle yet

He fought for the soul's freedom, fought the fight 

Which, though it still rings in our wondering ears,

Was won then and for ever--that great war, That last Crusade of Christ against His priests, Wherein Spain fell behind a thunderous roar 

Of ocean triumph over burning ships

And shattered fleets, while England, England rose, Her white cliffs laughing out across the waves, Victorious over all her enemies.

In the first book (there are twelve), Queen Elizabeth, still sparring with Spain, commissions Drake to put to sea, Fly the black flag of piracy awhile 

Against these darkest foes of all mankind. 

Nay; what hast man to do with piracy?

This is no piracy, but glorious war, Waged for thy country and for all mankind, but she bids him to do it secretly, for, she says, For now, if my Lord Burleigh heard one word 

Of all thou hast in mind, (Lord Burleigh was next most powerful in the government, after the Queen) he is so much 

The friend of caution and the beaten road, He would not rest till he had spilled thy hopes 

And sealed thy doom! his own

So in Book II, with a small fleet of five ships. ship, the Golden Hynde; the Elizabeth; the Christopher; the Swan; and the Marygold (Aren't these proper names for sailing ships?) 

Drake and the tiny fleet set sail; 

Yet gallantly and with heroic pride, Escutcheoned pavisades, emblazoned poops,

Banners and painted shields and close-fights hung 

With scarlet broideries. Every polished gun

Grinned through the jaws of some heraldic beast, Gilded and carven and gleaming with all hues; 

While in the cabin of the Golden Hynde Rich perfumes floated, given by the great Queen 

Herself to Drake as Captain-General;

So that it seemed her soul was with the fleet, A presence to remind him, far away, Of how he talked with England, face to face, (195)

EPIC VI 

No pirate he, but Gloriana's knight. 

Silver and gold his table furniture, Engraved and richly chased, lavishly gleamed 

With streaming flags and ensigns and sweet chords 

Of music struck by skilled musicians

Whom Drake brought with him, not from vanity But knowing how the pulse of men beats high 

To music; and the hearts of men like these 

Were open to the high romance of earth,

And they that dwelt so near God's mystery 

Were proud of their own manhood. They went out 

To danger, as to a sweetheart, far away. 

That's the style in which to go a-pirating, isn't it?

There's a traitor on board the Golden Hynde, one Thomas Doughty a gentleman born but now a hireling spy in the service of our Lord 

Burleigh. Unsuspecting Drake takes this viper to his bosom, tells him the whole story of his life, and even goes so far as to hint at his commission from the Queen. All the while, Doughty is trying covertly to stir up mutiny, and at last succeeds, after a fashion; but Drake overtakes the ship on which Doughty is fleeing and, after prayer and a trial and then more prayer, removes the villain, in Book III, by beheading him with a broadsword. (Doesn't that smack of the Spanish Main?)

Before we leave Book II, however, let's watch while Drake captures and despoils a Spanish galleon: So all night long they sailed; but when a rift 

Of orchard crimson broke the yellowing gloom 

And barred the closely clouded East with dawn, Behold, a giant galleon overhead,

Lifting its hugh black shining sides on high, Loomed like some misty monster of the deep: 

And, sullenly rolling out great gorgeous folds, Over her rumbled like a thunder-cloud

The heavy flag of Spain. The splendid poop, Mistily lustrous as a dragon's hoard 

Seen in some magic cave-mouth o'er the seat 

Through shimmering April sunlight after rain, Blazed to the morning; and her port-holes grinned 

With row on row of cannon. There at once, One sharp shrill whistle sounded, and those five,

Small ships, mere minnows clinging to the flanks of that Leviathan, unseen, unheard, Undreamt of, grappled her. She seemed asleep,

Swinging at ease with great half-slackened sails, Majestically careless of the dawn.

There in the very native seas of Spain, There with the yeast and foam of her proud cliffs,

Her own blue coasts, in sight across the waves, Up her Titanic sides without a sound

The naked-footed British seamen swarmed 

With knives between their teeth: then on her decks (96)

EPIC VI 

They dropped like panthers, and the softly fierce Black-bearded watch of Spaniards, all amazed, Rubbing their eyes as if at a wild dream, Upraised a sudden shout, El Draque! El Draque!

And flashed their weapons out, but all too late; 

For, ere their sleeping comrades reached the deck, The little watch, out-numbered and out-matched, Lay bound, and o'er the hatches everywhere The points of naked cutlasses on guard Gleamed, and without a struggle those below 

Gave up their arms, their poignards jewelled thick

With rubies, and their blades of Spanish steel. 

There's a boy's dream, of what you might call high-minded piracy, come true! 

Would you like some more of that? Well, then, here's the next paragraph, so to speak: 

Then onward o'er the great grey gleaming sea

They swept with their rich booty, night and day. 

Five other prizes, one for every ship,

Out of the seas of Spain they suddenly caught And carried with them, laughing as they went-- "Now, now indeed the Rubicon is crossed; 

Now have we singed the eyelids and the beard Of Spain; now have we roused the hornet's nest; 

Now shall we sail against a world in arms; 

Now we have nought between us and black death But our own hands, five ships, and three score guns." 

So laughed they, plunging through the bay of storms, Biscay, and past Gibraltar, not yet clothed 

With British thunder, though, as one might dream,

Gazing in dim prophetic grandeur out Across the waves while that small fleet went by, Or watching them with love's most wistful fear

As they plunged Southward to the lonely coasts 

Of Africa, till right in front up-soared, Tremendous over ocean, Teneriffe, Cloud-robed, but crowned with colours of the dawn. 

Now, right when we're really just getting into this epic, our time's up, and you must finish it by yourselves! Well, that's as it should be, I guess. (I've been reading, you may wish to know, from the first volume of Noyes's Collected Poems, published by the 

Frederick A. Stokes Company and copyrighted in 1913.)

At our next meeting we shall go back from the twentieth century to the seventeenth, and pay homage to John Milton. (197)

Is Drake more suitable for girls to read or for boys? For boys. 

Is Noyes pretty good at narrative? Yes, indeed. 

What was the name of Drake's ship? The Golden Hynde. 18. 19. 20. 

When was Drake published? 

Just before the outbreak of the 

Great War. (204)

A LAST WORD 

"All out! The end of the line!

We haven't got anywhere in particular, it seems to me; but the scenery has sometimes been interesting and attractive, hasn't it? 

What say we go it alone from here on? "All out! This is as far as we go!" 205
